PREFACE 

This book is designed as a companion volume to the Second Edition of the author's textbook "The Law of Evidence." In the latter treatise will be found a large number of illustrations, from actual cases, further to reinforce and elucidate the text. From the nature of things a textbook can employ but a limited number of such illustrations, however valuable such illustrations may be to the student. A book for collateral study, composed entirely of digests of a great multitude of decisions in leading jurisdictions, should add greatly to the effectiveness of law teaching.

This volume contains practically all the cases in "Thayer's Cases on Evidence," as well as the important Massachusetts decisions. The cases are topically arranged, in chapters and sections to correspond with similar chapters and sections in the author's textbook on "The Law of Evidence," thus rendering it an effective companion volume for the use of instructor and student. 

G. L. A.

Digest of Evidence Cases CHAPTER I. 

GENERAL PRINCIPLES.

6. Functions of Judge and Jury. 

§ 1. Evidence Defined. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 2. Basis of Rules of Evidence. 

(See textbook.) 

3. Kinds of Evidence-Direct Evidence. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 4. Kinds of Evidence-Circumstantial Evidence. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 6. Functions of Judge and Jury. 

Bartlett v. Smith, 11 M. & W., 483 (1843), Thayer, 144.

Assumpsit by the indorsee against the drawer of a bill of exchange. The bill when produced at the trial appeared to be drawn in Dublin, payable in London, and

was stamped as a foreign bill. The defendant objected that the bill was in fact drawn in London, and being therefore an inland bill required a higher denomination of stamp in order to be valid. The question was later left to the jury to decide whether the bill was foreign or domestic. The jury found for the plaintiff. 

On appeal the court granted a new trial, saying: "All questions respecting the admissibility of evidence are to be determined by the judge, who ought to receive that evidence, and decide upon it without any reference to the jury.

In all cases where an objection is made to the competency of witnesses, any evidence to show their incompetency must be received by the judge, and adjudicated on by him alone. So, in the present case, evidence offered to impeach the admissibility of the bill, on the ground that it was improperly stamped, should have been received by the judge, and determined by him before the bill was allowed to be read to the jury." 

Commonwealth v. Robinson, 146 Mass., 571 (1888), Thayer 146.

The defendant was on trial for murder by poisoning of her brother-in-law. In order to establish the defendant's motive for the crime the government offered evidence that Freeman, the deceased, had insured his life for $2,000 in favor of his wife, and that the defendant in order to get possession of this money had poisoned her sister, induced Freeman to make her the beneficiary of the policy, and then had murdered him by poison. 

The defendant objected to the admission of the evidence concerning the death of Annie Freeman, as well as of the alleged scheme. The court admitted the evidence and the defendant alleged exceptions. The exceptions

GENERAL PRINCIPLES 

21 were over-ruled by the Supreme Court. "Ordinarily," said the court, "questions of fact are exclusively for the jury, and questions of law for the court. But when, in order to pass upon the admissibility of evidence, the determination of a preliminary question of fact is necessary, the court in the due and orderly course of the trial must necessarily determine it, as far as is necessary for that purpose, and usually without the assistance, at that stage, of the jury. In all such cases. the court, in deciding to admit the offered testimony, does no more than to hold that enough has been shown to make it proper to submit the question to the jury, leaving its weight and credit for their determination.

The decision of the judge does not relieve the party offering the testimony from the necessity of establishing every material fact to the satisfaction of the jury." 

Panton v. Williams, 2 Q. B., 169 (1841), Thayer, 148.

Ann Williams sued William Panton for malicious prosecution on two indictments for forgery. She was accused on one count of having forged a will purporting to be that of Jones Panton and, in another, of forging a codicil to the will of Jones Panton. She was acquitted. This suit was brought and the jury found a verdict for Ann. The case was taken to the Exchequer 

Chamber on a writ of error because in the trial the jury had been instructed as follows: "That if they thought there was reasonable and probable cause for taking the steps against the plaintiff, their verdict must be for the defendant." The defendant urged that the judge "was bound to state to the jury what facts, if proved, would amount to probable cause, leaving to them only the question, whether they believed the evidence adduced

in order to prove such facts." The Court of Exchequer ordered a venire de novo because of misdirection, saying that "it is the duty of the judge to inform the jury, if they find the facts proved and the inferences warranted by such facts, that the same do or do not amount to reasonable or probable cause, so as thereby to leave the question of fact to the jury, and the abstract question of law to the judge. . . . Upon the whole, as the question both of law and of fact was left in this case entirely to the jury, we think the exception must be allowed, and that ere must be a venire de novo.” 

Ryder v. Wombwell, L. R. 4 Ex., 32 (1868), Thayer, 151.

The declaration was for money payable for goods sold and delivered. Plea: Infancy. Replication: Neces saries. Issue thereon. At the trial it appeared that the defendant was the younger son of a deceased baronet. 

During his minority he had an income of £500 per annum, but upon attaining majority he came into an income of £20,000. The articles purchased on credit were ornamental studs or buttons worn by gentlemen as fastenings for the wristbands of shirts, and also a goblet intended as a present. The studs were composed of diamonds and rubies, and amounted to £25. The goblet was priced at £15 15s. Whether the articles named could be termed necessaries was the question before the court. The matter was left to the jury, who returned a verdict that the articles named were necessaries to a person of defendant's station in life. On appeal the court reversed the judgment and entered a non-suit, saying that the question whether the articles were necessaries was improperly left to the jury, saying that no court "is bound to consider itself ignorant of every

GENERAL PRINCIPLES 

23 usage of mankind, and therefore bound, in the absence of all evidence on the subject, to take the opinion of a jury as to whether it is not so necessary for a gentleman to wear solitaires of this description, that though an infant, he must obtain them on credit rather than go without."

Bridges v. No. London Ry. Co. L. R., 7 H. L., 213 (1874) 

Thayer, 157. 

Action for damages for negligence in causing the death of the plaintiff's husband. The fatal accident occurred at about 7 o'clock in the evening. The deceased was in the last coach. The train was long and when it stopped two coaches were not even with the station platform. The deceased, being nearsighted, attempted to alight and fell. When found, his leg was broken, and he had received mortal injuries. The judge before whom the case was tried, was of the opinion that there was no negligence on the part of the defendant company, and directed a non-suit, but the jury, expressing a strong opinion to the contrary, brought in a verdict for the plaintiff. The damages were set at £1200. The case was appealed on the question of whether there was sufficient evidence of negligence to be submitted to a jury. The House of Lords upheld the verdict, saying that: "what men of ordinary care and skill would or would not do under certain circumstances is a matter of experience, and so of fact, which a jury only ought to determine." 

Lorenzo v. Wirth, 170 Mass., 596 (1898), Thayer, 167.

Action of tort for personal injuries occasioned by falling into a coal hole on the defendant's premises.

The coal hole was about eighteen inches from the house, which set back from the street several feet. The paving was continuous from street to house and there was nothing to give notice that this space was not a part of the sidewalk, except that it was generally used by the tenants to deposit barrels, etc., and the further fact that the steps from the house which plaintiff had just passed came out to the street line. At the time of the accident a coal wagon was backed up and coal was being shoveled or poured down the open coal hole. The coal covered the whole sidewalk. The plaintiff, a Spanish woman, who claimed that she had never seen coal put through a coal hole before, stepped upon the steps of the adjoining building. From thence she stepped upon the coal. At her second step on the coal she sank into the coal hole as far as nature would permit, with one leg above ground. The judge was asked to direct a verdict for the defendant. He refused to do so and the jury brought in a verdict for the plaintiff. The Supreme 

Court declared that the trial court erred because the defendant was not guilty of negligence. "A heap of coal on a sidewalk in Boston is an indication according to common experience that there very possibly may be a coal hole to receive it. But without saying that it always is a sufficient warning to look out for one, we are of opinion that, as against a person coming from where the plaintiff came from, with a coal hole situated as it was... the defendant cannot be said to have been wanting in due care." 

Terre Haute, etc., R. R. v. Voelker, 129 Ill. 540 (1889), Thayer, 170.

Action on the case for damages for the death of Edward Voelker, who received fatal injuries in crossing

GENERAL PRINCIPLES 

25 the railroad track of the Terre Haute Railroad. It appeared in evidence that the railroad company was required by law to cause a heavy bell to sound on its engine for at least eighty rods before reaching the crossing. Because of failure to perform this duty, the deceased, while in the exercise of due care, was fatally injured. The defendant contended that unless the deceased stopped, looked and listened before driving onto the track he was guilty of contributory negligence per se, which would bar the right of recovery. The Supreme 

Court declared on this point that: "It has been the uniform doctrine of this court that negligence is ordinarily a question of fact for the jury. . . . It may be a duty of a person about to cross to stop and look, to ascertain if the train is coming; but it is always a question of fact for the jury to determine, from the evidence, whether the person injured has exercised proper care and caution in crossing a railroad track, and not a question of law. It is the province of the jury to determine whether the plaintiff was guilty of negligence, and not for the court to tell the jury that certain facts constituted negligence.... Non-performance of a statutory duty, resulting in injury to another, may therefore be pronounced to be negligence as a conclusion of law." 

Stackus v. N. Y., etc., R. R., 79 N. Y. 464 (1880), Thayer, 174.

Appeal from a judgment of the general term of the Supreme Court, in the fourth judicial department, affirming a judgment in favor of defendant, entered upon an order non-suiting plaintiff on trial, and affirming an order denying a new trial. The plaintiff was non-suited on the ground of contributory negligence. It appeared

that the plaintiff was travelling in a covered buggy on a highway that crossed the defendant's railroad at an acute angle. He testified that when he reached the warning signboard he stopped his team and looked up and down the track. He drove onto the track without thought of danger, but was struck by a train before getting across the track. The Court of Appeals reversed the judgment and granted a new trial. Said the court: "It is difficult to conceive of a question of fact more appropriate for the consideration of the jury than this. It is a question in respect to which men may differ. The learned judge who delivered the opinion at General Term upon the first appeal, expressed himself emphatically that it was a palpable case of negligence. It is not needful to say that this was an error as a question of fact; but it seems to me clear that it cannot be so adjudged as a question of law." 

Hutchinson v. Bowker, 5 M. & W., 535 (1839), Thayer, 178. 

The defendants offered to sell "good barley." The plaintiffs in accepting used the term "fine barley." When the barley was delivered it was "good barley," but not "fine barley." The plaintiff sued for damages. Evidence was introduced to the effect that the terms used signified different grades of barley, and the judge left it for the jury to interpret the contract. They did so and declared that the parties did not understand each other, but nevertheless found a verdict for the plaintiff. The defendant obtained a rule to show cause why this verdict should not be set aside, and a non-suit entered. 

The Court of Exchequer ordered a non-suit, Lord Abinger saying: "It appears to me that the question as to

GENERAL PRINCIPLES 

27 interpretation of this contract is a question entirely for the court and not for the jury. That they should ever he the judges on such matter was founded on this, that there might be technical words used in a contract, which the jury might understand, and the court might not; but it would be contrary to all practice to say, after the terms are explained to the satisfaction of the court, that the jury are to have the interpretation of the contract, and not the court. . . . In this case, if they had said they were satisfied that there was no difference in the words, I should then have directed them to find for the plaintiffs; but they told me they were of opinion that there was a difference in the words, but they did not think the contract should be interpreted with reference to that distinction, as the parties did not understand each other. 

I think that they had no right to assume that.” 

Spragins v. White, 108 N. C., 449 (1891), Thayer, 181.

The defendant purchased shoes of the plaintiff and, when sued for the price, set up in defence that he had ordered the shoes to be delivered within two weeks in order to fill a shipment to be made to a customer at that time. He claimed that without the promise to deliver in two weeks he would not have given the plaintiff the order. The plaintiff denied any such agreement. The court submitted two propositions to the jury: "Did it mean that the plaintiffs were to insure, at all events, the delivery by the transportation company of the goods in two weeks, and that in failure of such delivery in two weeks the sale was to be void at the option of the defendant, and he might return the goods to the plaintiffs? 

If so, the plaintiffs are not entitled to recover. But if it meant that the plaintiffs were to use all due diligence

in forwarding the order, in packing and shipping the goods by the common carrier, and the plaintiffs did all these things, then the plaintiffs are entitled to recover the bill and interest, as before stated." The jury found for the plaintiffs and the defendant appealed on the ground that the court erred in leaving the interpretation to the jury. The Supreme Court ordered a new trial, saying that the charge "assumes that the terms of the contract are ascertained, but at the same time leaves its interpretation to the jury. The court should have interpreted this meaning according to the terms of the assumed contract, and not according to absent terms incorporated into the same by what the jury were to infer was the meaning of the parties. In this there was error." 

Commonwealth v. Porter, 10 Metc. (Mass.), 263 (1846), Thayer, 185.

The defendant, convicted of violation of the license laws, took his case to the Supreme Court on a bill of exceptions. The ground of his complaint was that his lawyer was not permitted to address the jury upon questions of law embraced in the issue. The court held that while the jury were to pass upon the facts yet it was necessary also that the law bearing upon these facts be explained and the attorneys for both parties have a right to comment on the law in their pleas to the jury. "The defendant has a right by himself or his counsel to address the jury, under the general superintendence of the court, upon all the material questions involved in the issue, and to this extent, and in this connection, to address the jury upon such questions of law as come within the issue to be tried. Such address to

GENERAL PRINCIPLES 

29 the jury, upon questions of law embraced in the issue, by the defendant or his counsel, is warranted by the long practice of the courts in this Commonwealth in criminal cases, in which it is within the established authority of a jury, if they see fit to return a general verdict, embracing the entire issue of law and fact."

§ 7. Witnesses at Common Law. 

(See textbook.) 

Thurston v. Whitney, 2 Cush. (Mass.), 104 (1848), Thayer, 1083. 

§ 8. Religion or Lack of Religion. 

At the trial of an action in the Court of Common Pleas the defendants offered a witness to be sworn who was objected to by the plaintiff on the ground of want of religious belief. The plaintiff offered to produce evidence that the witness did not believe in the existence of 

God, or in a future state of rewards and punishments.

The presiding judge declined to hear evidence on the alleged incompetency of the witness and permitted him to testify. There was a verdict for the defendant. The plaintiff carried the case to the Supreme Court on exceptions. In the latter court the exceptions were sustained, the court declaring in part as follows: "All the

WITNESSES 

31 authorities agree that an atheist, who disbelieves in the existence of God who is 'the rewarder of truth and avenger of falsehood,' cannot be permitted to testify. 

It would indeed seem absurd to administer to a witness an oath, containing a solemn appeal for the truth of his testimony, to a Being in whose existence he has no belief. . . . But the defendants' counsel contend that this rule of law has been abrogated by the Constitution, part Ist, article 2nd, which provides 'that no subject shall be hurt, molested or restrained in his person or estate for worshipping God in the manner and season most agreeable to the dictates of his own conscience, or for his religious profession or sentiments.' In the opinion of the court, this article has no reference to atheists and to their competency as witnesses. It was intended to prevent persecution by punishing anyone for his religious opinions, however erroneous they might be. But an atheist is without any religion, true or false. The disbelief in the existence of any God is not a religious but an anti-religious sentiment." 

§ 9 Personal Interest in Outcome of Trial. 

Sparhawk v. Sparhawk, 10 Allen, (Mass.), 155 (1865), Thayer, 1093.

Edward Sparhawk was a brother and heir at law of Catherine Cole, who died possessed of considerable property, leaving a will to which Edward was one of three attesting witnesses. No property was left to Edward, but, by the terms of the will, the bulk of the estate was left to Edward's son. The question arose whether Edward was competent to act as an attesting witness. The court answered this question in the af-

firmative, saying: "It is conceded that he was one of the heirs-at-law of the testatrix, and, if she had died intestate, he would have been entitled to one-fourth part of her estate. It is also agreed that by her last will the testatrix devised and bequeathed the larger part of her estate to the son of the attesting witness, and made no devise or bequest whatever to the latter. It is very clear, therefore, that the pecuniary interest of the witness would be promoted by a failure to prove the due attestation of the will, and that his testimony in support of it would operate directly against this interest. . 

It is only when the pecuniary interest of a person will in some way and to some appreciable extent be aided or promoted by a judgment or decree in favor of the party calling him, that he is excluded from testifying, according to the rule of the common law." 

Sears v. Dillingham, 12 Mass., 358 (1819.) 

Dillingham, one of the subscribing witnesses to a will, was appointed executor thereof. At a trial in the 

Probate Court he was not permitted to testify to the soundness of mind of the testator. This was one of the grounds of appeal, it being alleged that Dillingham was competent as a witness because he had no personal interest in the outcome of the suit. The Supreme Court affirmed the action of the lower court in excluding the witness, saying: "An executor, in this State, who by statute is liable to costs upon an appeal, is not competent to testify upon a trial; for he is not merely a nominal party but is directly and personally interested in the event of the suit. The interest may be slight, but it may also be important, as the costs in the case of a will, contested on the ground of the insanity of the testator,

WITNESSES 

33 are usually not inconsiderable. But this rule has been invariably adhered to in the courts of this State, there being no instance of an executor, administrator or guardian being admitted to testify, to support actions in favor of the estates they represent; their being parties, and liable eventually to costs, having been always deemed a sufficient objection." 

McLaren v. Gillespie, 56 Pac. Rep., 680 (1899), Thayer, 1096. 

The plaintiff brought an action against Gillespie, a constable, for an alleged wrongful levy upon goods claimed by her. During the course of the trial, Patterson, who was the defendant's sole attorney in the case, offered to testify in the defendant's behalf, whereupon the court, on its own motion, refused to permit the witness to testify unless he first retired from the case as attorney for the defendant. This he refused to do, and was barred from the witness stand. The Supreme Court held that the trial court erred. Said the court in conclusion: "While we hold that the attorney competent witness, under the statute of this State, we do not wish to be understood as commending the practice of calling an attorney, engaged in the trial of a case as such, to testify as a witness. This practice should not be indulged in, except in cases where it is absolutely necessary. Cases may arise, however, where it is absolutely necessary that the attorney be called as a witness; but when such exigency arises and the attorney can safely do so, with respect to the interests of his client, he should invariably retire from the case. Judging from the statement of counsel in their brief, the was

exigency suggested, requiring his testimony, arose in this case." 

§ 10. Conviction of Infamous Crime. 

Logan v. United States, 144 U. S., 263 (1892), Thayer, 1068.

The defendants were found guilty of statutory conspiracy and sentenced, but sued out a writ of error alleging that two of the witnesses against them, Martin and Spear, were incompetent to testify. Martin was shown by certified copies of the court records to have been convicted of felonious homicide in North Carolina and had there served a sentence of imprisonment. 

Spear was twice convicted of felonious larceny and had served two terms of imprisonment in Texas. The government offered proof however, that Spear had since been pardoned by the Governor of Texas. The trial at which these witnesses appeared was in the Federal courts of Texas. The Criminal Code of Texas provided that all persons who had been convicted of felony in Texas or elsewhere should be incompetent to testify "unless such conviction has been legally set aside, or unless the convict has been legally pardoned." Section 

858 of the Revised Statutes of the United States provided that "the laws of the State in which the court is held shall be the rules of decision as to the competency of witnesses in the courts of the United States in trials at common law and in equity and in admiralty." The 

Supreme Court held that this provision had “no application to criminal trials and, therefore, the competency of witnesses in criminal trials in the courts of the United States held within the State of Texas is not

WITNESSES 

35 governed by a statute of the State which was first enacted in 1858, but, except so far as Congress has made specific provisions upon the subject, is governed by the common law, which, as has been seen, was the law of Texas before the passage of that statute and at the time of the admission of Texas into the Union as a State. 

At common law . . . a conviction and sentence in another State... can have no effect, by way of penalty, or of personal disability or disqualification, beyond the limits of the State in which the judgment is rendered." 

§ 11. Lack of Mental Capacity. 

Rex v. Brasier, Leach (4th ed.), 199 (1779), Thayer, 1074.

The defendant was indicted and upon trial was found guilty of an assault with intent to commit rape upon 

Mary Harris, a child under seven years of age. The case against the prisoner was proved by the mother of the child and by another woman who lodged with her, to whom the child, immediately upon coming home, told all the circumstances of the injury which had been done to her. The only evidence connecting the defendant with the outrage or confirming the child's story, was the fact that he lodged at the very place described by her and that she, on seeing him the next day, declared that he was the man who had hurt her. The child was not sworn or produced at the trial. The defendant was later pardoned because the judges of the spring assizes of 1779 were unanimously of the opinion that "no testimony whatever can be legally received except upon oath; and that an infant, though under the age of seven years may be sworn in a criminal prosecution, provided such infant appears, on strict examination by the court, to possess a sufficient knowledge of the nature and con-

sequences of an oath, for there is no precise fixed rule as to the time within which infants are excluded from giving evidence; but their admissibility depends upon the sense and reason they entertain of the danger and impiety of falsehood, which is to be collected from their answers to questions propounded to them by the court; but if they are found incompetent to take an oath, their testimony cannot be received." 

Coleman v. Commonwealth, 25 Gratton, (Va.), 865 (1874), Thayer, 1075.

This case was taken to the Court of Appeals on a writ of error because the prisoner was convicted in part upon the testimony of an alleged lunatic, Joseph Mayo, Jr. 

It was admitted that on two occasions shortly before he testified Mayo was decidedly insane and also that a few days after the trial he was in the same condition. 

The court refused to set aside the conviction on the ground that during the two days in which Mayo was on the witness stand he showed no evidence of mental derangement. Said the court: "In deciding this question we have the benefit of an actual examination of Col. Mayo as a witness on two successive days-the whole examination relating to complicated transactions of a delicate and painful character; yet during that protracted examination nothing occurred which excited even a suspicion of insanity in the mind of the plaintiff in error, or his learned and astute counsel; although they did think that he was under the influence of stimulants; nor upon a close and critical review of his testimony, since it was given, have they been able to point the court to anything in it evincing delusion or inconsistency, any want of intelligence or want of memory. 

Whilst on the other hand, Judge Guigon certifies that

WITNESSES 

37 although evidently drinking deeply, he was at the time of his examination, ‘a competent and proper witness, and not laboring under any mental disability whatever.' 999 

Commonwealth v. Robinson, 165 Mass., 427 (1896).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for an indecent assault upon a girl who was five years and five months old at the time of the alleged assault. The trial occurred within a few months. The child was questioned by the judge and then permitted to testify. 

The defendant was convicted. One of the grounds of exception was the age of the child. The Supreme Court declared: "The law fixes no limit of age which children must have reached in order to be competent as witnesses; and unless for manifest error we cannot revise the decision of the presiding justice that the little girl in this case had sufficient intelligence and sufficient sense of the duty of telling the truth to enable her to testify. No such error appears in the present case, although there is a certain incongruity in administering an oath to one who cannot be convicted of perjury. 

Nevertheless, the fact that the child was too young to be convicted of perjury is not decisive against her competency. Of course, upon the bill of exceptions, we have not to consider how much weight might properly be given to her testimony." 

§ 12. Accomplices as Witnesses. 

Commonwealth v. Bishop, 165 Mass. 148, 42 N. E. 560, (1896). 

At the trial of an indictment for procuring an abortion, which resulted in the death of the woman, an ac-

complice of the defendant was placed upon the witness stand by the prosecution. He testified that he went to a newspaper office, bought a newspaper and examined its advertising columns with the idea of locating a doctor who would perform the abortion. In consequence of seeing an advertisement of the defendant he went to the latter's office and made arrangements for the operation. The defendant himself admitted on the witness stand that he advertised in the newspaper in question and stated what was the language of the advertisement. 

This language was of such a nature that it might be understood as an offer to perform abortions. The Supreme 

Court held that the evidence of the accomplice was sufficiently corroborated to be admissible. 

Commonwealth v. Snow, 111 Mass., 411 (1873).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for sodomy with one Smith. The latter was the chief witness against him. There was some question whether 

Smith had voluntarily participated in the act, thus making him an accomplice. He testified to the circumstances of going to the defendant's room and also to the fact that the defendant locked the outer door of the house after admitting him. They were interrupted by a loud knocking at the outer door and the defendant went down and admitted another tenant, explaining that he had locked the door because he was taking a nap. 

This evidence was corroborated by the tenant. peared also that about two weeks later Smith took poison. The attending physician testified that the defendant applied to him several times to know whether 

Smith would die, and if he had told anybody why he had taken poison. This was deemed sufficient corrobo- It ap-

WITNESSES 

39 ration to justify admission of the testimony of the accomplice.

15. Privilege of Witnesses. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 16. Privilege-Self Criminating Evidence. 

Bull v. Loveland, 10 Pick. (Mass.), 9 (1830), Thayer, 1107.

In this case the question arose whether a witness, without his consent, can be called to testify to any fact pertinent to the issue between other parties, where such testimony may tend to charge him with a debt, or subject him to pecuniary loss or liability. The Supreme 

Court, in reviewing the law on this point, called attention to the fact that early decisions in this country held that a man in a civil case was not bound to testify against his interest. This evidently followed by analogy the doctrine adhered to in the law of crimes. The mod-

ern development was thus stated: "In a recent case in this court it has been held that one summoned as a trustee cannot avoid answering questions put to him in regard to the validity of a conveyance under which he claims title to property, on the ground that it may affect his own pecuniary interest. Devoll v. Brownell, 5

Pick 448.... On the whole, we think the weight of authority is in favor of the rule that a witness may be called and examined in a manner pertinent to the issue, where his answers will not expose him to criminal prosecution or tend to subject him to a penalty or forfeiture, although they may otherwise adversely affec his pecuniary interest, and that the witness was properly called and examined in the present case." 

State v. Ober, 52 N. H., 459 (1873), Thayer, 1123.

The defendant in a liquor case, having been duly advised of his right to decline to testify, voluntarily took the witness stand and denied certain charges against him. He was then asked in cross examination if he had not sold spirituous liquor within one year prior to the indictment, but he declined to answer on the ground that it might tend to criminate him. In the closing argument for the prosecution the attention of the jury was called to the failure to answer, whereupon the defendant's counsel raised the objection that the counsel for the State had no right thus to argue. The objection was over-ruled. In his charge to the jury the court declared that the failure to answer "might be given such weight as they thought it entitled to." The Supreme 

Court over-ruled the objections and declared that the defendant should have been compelled to answer the questions in the first place since he had voluntarily

waived his right to refuse to answer questions. "Having testified," said the court, "concerning a part of the transaction, in which it was alleged that he was criminally concerned, without claiming his constitutional privilege, it was too late for him to halt at that point which suited his own convenience. It is clear, upon reason and authority, that he might have been compelled to answer the question propounded by the State's counsel. It was material to the issue, if not directly involved in his own proffered testimony. At this point for obvious reasons, he saw fit to close his lips, and the court allowed him to remain silent. Of this mistaken clemency he cannot now be heard to complain." 

Commonwealth v. Smith, 163 Mass., 41 (1895), Thayer, 1129.

The defendants were on trial for conspiracy to solicit applicants for licenses to sell intoxicating liquors to bribe such of the defendants as were aldermen of Haverhill for the purpose of influencing their action in the granting of liquor licenses. Some of those under indictment took the stand in their own behalf. They were severally asked on cross examination if they had not, when summoned before the grand jury, refused to testify on the ground that it might criminate them. The trial judge ordered them to answer the question. This was one of the chief grounds for appeal. The Supreme 

Court declared: "By universal consent, if one under indictment avails himself of a statute allowing him to testify, and voluntarily becomes a witness in his own behalf, he waives his constitutional protection, to a certain extent at least. He was not bound to testify. He may remain silent, relying on the statutory provision that

his neglect or refusal to testify shall not create a presumption against him. But if at his own request he becomes a witness in own behalf, he takes an oath to tell the whole truth. In this commonwealth, the cross-examination is not restricted to matters inquired of in chief. He may be cross-examined like other witnesses. He may be questioned as to all incriminating circumstances, and he must answer all such questions as are relevant to the subject of the charge against him." 

Georgia R. R. v. Lybrend, 99 Ga., 421 (1896), Thayer, 1131. 

Lybrend brought an action of tort against the railroad for expelling him from one of its cars. This was evidently a second trial. At the first trial Lybrend had taken the stand and had testified concerning the causes for his expulsion from the train. But at the present trial he refused to testify; and the railroad was defeated. 

It alleged exceptions on the ground that his waiver of the constitutional privilege in the first trial precluded him from exercising it thereafter. The Supreme Court over-ruled the exceptions, saying: "Many of the details of Lybrend's trip to Ohio and of his association with the woman, Martha Comer, certainly were irrelevant; but such facts as tended to show that she was upon the train of the Georgia railroad on the night he was expelled therefrom were, for a reason already stated, both relevant and material; and in view of the defendant's right by a thorough and sifting cross-examination, to compel Lybrend to admit that she was upon that train, inquiries concerning his whereabouts and movements, and the extent of his travels in company with the woman for some time previous, might have been legiti-

mately pressed to a considerably extent, if counsel for the defendant had not encountered the difficulty which arose when Lybrend claimed his privilege as stated. 

He was protected under that clause of the Bill of Rights which declares that 'no person shall be compelled to give testimony tending in any manner to criminate himself' (Code Sec. 4998), and under Section 3814 of the

Code. ... The fact that Lybrend had made the waiver at a former hearing before a different jury, and under circumstances necessarily to a greater or less extent unlike those surrounding the last trial, did not preclude him from exercising his privilege at a later stage of the case. He may, on one occasion, have had reasons for speaking out which were entirely satisfactory to himself; and on the next, he may have been influenced to keep silent for other reasons which were, in his opinion, equally cogent." 

Counselman v. Hitchcock, 142 U. S., 547 (1892), Thayer, 1136. 

Counselman was a witness before a Federal grand jury which was investigating violations of the Interstate Commerce Act by certain corporations. He refused to answer questions relating to his dealings with the railroads on the ground that his answers might tend to criminate him. He was committed for contempt and failing to secure his discharge upon a writ of habeas corpus, appealed to the U. S. Supreme Court. Said the court: "It is broadly contended on the part of the ap pellee that a witness is not entitled to plead the privilege of silence, except in a criminal case against himself; but such is not the language of the Constitution. 

Its provision is that no person shall be compelled in any

criminal case to be a witness against himself. This provision must have a broad construction in favor of the right which it was intended to secure. The matter under investigation by the grand jury in this case was a criminal matter, to inquire whether there had been a criminal violation of the Interstate Commerce Act. If

Counselman had been guilty of the matters inquired of in the questions which he refused to answer, he himself was liable to criminal prosecution under the act. The case before the grand jury was therefore, a criminal case. 

The relations of Counselman to the subject of inquiry before the grand jury, as shown by the questions put to him, in connection with the provisions of the Interstate Commerce Act, enabled him to invoke the protection of the Constitution." 

People v. Gardner, 144 N. Y., 119, Thayer, 1142. "Upon the trial it was proved that defendant and Mrs. Amys were together upon certain occasions having a material bearing upon the case, and a witness was called to identify the defendant as the person who was in her company at one of the times and places referred to. The witness was asked: 'Do you know Mr. Gardner? Answer: 'I do not.' Question: 'Would you know him if you saw him?" Answer: 'Yes, sir.' The court thereupon directed the defendant to stand up. The defendant's counsel objected to his standing up, or that he should be compelled to stand up, or to testify against himself. The court replied: "The prisoner will rise; stand him up.' And then, against the objection of his counsel, the defendant was forcibly compelled to stand up. He was identified by the witness. It is now claimed on his behalf that this action on the part of the

court violated his constitutional rights by compelling him to be a witness. It was insisted that this was to make the prisoner furnish evidence against himself; that he had a right to be there and confront his accusers, and that for the State to take advantage of his presence to have him pointed out and identified placed him in the dilemma of either abandoning his constitutional right to be present, or of affording the means of his conviction by its exercise. The court held against this contention, and that no error was committed." 

§ 17. Privilege State Secrets. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 18. Privilege-Deliberation of Grand Jurors. 

Commonwealth v. Hill, 11 Cush., (Mass.), 137 (1853).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for feloniously receiving goods alleged to have been stolen "by a person unknown, and to be the property of a person unknown." McLellan, the principal thief, turned "State's evidence" and testified among other things that he appeared before the grand jury that returned the indictment against the defendant and that he then confessed his guilt and told who owned the stolen property. 

This evidence was seized upon by the defendant to establish a variance between the indictment and the proof. 

The commonwealth, to rebut the alleged variance, called the foreman of the grand jury in question. 

Against the defendant's objection the witness testified that McLellan never appeared before the grand jury and therefore that they had no means of identifying the thief or the owner of the goods. The Supreme Court

declared that it was proper for the foreman of the grand jury thus to testify. Said the court: "The extent of the limitation upon the testimony of grand jurors, is best defined by the terms of their oath of office by which 'the commonwealth's counsel, their fellows, and their own, they are to keep secret.' They cannot therefore be permitted to state how any member of the jury voted or the opinion expressed by their fellows or themselves upon any question before them, nor to disclose the fact that an indictment for a felony has been found against any person, not in custody nor under recognizance, nor to state in detail the evidence on which the indictment is founded. We know of no authority which carries the rule of exclusion further, and we can see no ground or policy or sound reason for its extension." 

$ 19. Privilege-Deliberations of Petit Juries. 

Hannum v. Belchertown, 19 Pick., (Mass.), 311 (1837).

This was an action against a town, under a statute, to recover double damages for an injury caused by an alleged defect in a highway within the town. The defect was a rock that protruded from the ground three or four inches which caused his heavily loaded wagon to overturn. The defendant contended that the rock was outside the travelled highway, but the jury found a verdict for the plaintiff. Under the statute the judge was to double the verdict. One of the grounds for appeal was that the jury had already doubled the damages in assessing the amount of the verdict. This fact the defendant sought to prove by testimony of members of the jury before whom the case was tried. The Supreme 

Court declared that such evidence was inadmissible. 

It declared, after examining the depositions of the ju-

rors, that even if the evidence were permitted it would disprove the defendant's contention. "Nothing could better illustrate the wisdom of the rule," said the court, "which holds the deliberations of the jury room to be inviolable, and precludes jurors from giving evidence of their own misconduct, of the reason and grounds of their determinations, and the motives which governed their conduct. . . . Men of strong minds and sound judgments, who are very sure to come to wise and just conclusions, would, if called upon to state the grounds of their opinions, often give very insufficient and unsatisfactory reasons for their decisions. The secrecy of the deliberations and discussions of the jury and the exemption of jurors from the liability of being questioned as to their motives and grounds of action, are highly important to the freedom and independence of their decisions." 

Chadbourn v. Franklin, 5 Gray, (Mass.), 312 (1855).

This was an action of tort for injury occasioned to the plaintiff's horse and chaise by a collision with the defendant's hack on a highway in Roxbury. There was conflicting testimony as to whether the defendant was on the wrong side of the street when the accident occurred, but the jury evidently believed the latter at fault and found a verdict accordingly. One of the grounds alleged for a new trial was that on a Sunday during the trial one of the jurors had gone to the scene of the accident and had examined it for the purpose of informing himself upon the subject matter of the trial. This fact he proposed to prove by the juror himself. The court ruled that the juror could not be permitted to testify, in support of this motion, to these acts tending to show his

own misconduct. The Supreme Court held that the trial court ruled correctly and cited in support of the contention: Hannum v. Belchertown, 19 Pick. 313; Murdock v Sumner, 22 Pick. 158; Cook v. Castner, 9 Cush. 278;

Folsom v. Manchester, II Cush 334; Boston v. Worcester R. R. v. Dana, I Gray 105; I Greenleaf on Evidence Sec. 252a. 

Hix v. Drury, 5 Pick., (Mass.), 296 (1827).

In an action concerning defamation of character a large number of depositions, both impeaching and sustaining the character of certain witnesses, were filed in court. The defendant moved for a new trial, alleging that two depositions not read in court were included among the papers that went into the jury room. This was apparently entirely accidental. Eleven of the jurors were questioned by the court and all agreed that the two depositions in question were not read during their deliberations in the jury room. The twelfth juror, who was absent at the time, made an affidavit that the depositions were not read to him. The Supreme Court upheld the decision of the lower court in permitting the jury to explain the situation as to what happened to the depositions in the jury room. 

Woodward v. Leavitt, 107 Mass. 453, 4 Am. Rep. 49 (1871.)

In this case a motion was made for a new trial because a certain juror named Brown, was alleged to have formed and expressed an opinion prior to the trial. In support of this contention, three witnesses were introduced, each of whom testified to the statements of Brown. The latter was examined. He admitted that

when he was being considered for jury duty he had been asked by the judge if he had formed or expressed an opinion on the merits of the case but declared that he then had no recollection of having expressed an opinion and that he was not conscious of any bias. The judge, against the plaintiff's objection, allowed others of the jury to be examined, and they testified that Brown did not take part in discussions, and did not attempt to influence them. Brown was also permitted to state that he voted in favor of the plaintiff at first and did not vote against him until after all the other jurors had. The 

Supreme Court declared that the testimony of Brown with reference to his conduct prior to the trial was proper, but that it was improper to permit him or any other members of the jury to testify as to what transpired in the jury room. For this reason a rehearing of the motion for a new trial was granted. 

20. Privilege-Professional Secrets. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 21. Attorney and Client. 

Foster v. Hall, 12 Pick. (Mass.), 89 (1831), Thayer, 1150.

In a lawsuit involving the title to land the tenants offered the testimony of Robinson, an attorney at law. 

He stated that he knew nothing in regard to the conveyance except what the grantor had told him when consulting him with reference to the conveyance. This was objected to on the ground that it was a privileged communication made to him in his professional capacity and that he could not be called upon to disclose it to the injury of any one claiming under said conveyance. The

trial judge rejected the evidence and a verdict was returned for the demandant. The tenant took the case to the Supreme Court. Chief Justice Lemuel Shaw in his opinion explained the doctrine of confidential communications between a client and his attorney, and declared that the lawyer "shall not be permitted to disclose it by his testimony, if ever so much inclined to do so, unless released from the obligation by the client." 

On the question of whether this was limited to communications with reference to the prosecution or defence of a suit at law, existing or contemplated, or whether it extended to all cases was thus discussed: "The privilege extends to communications made to a legal adviser, duly qualified as such, employed and acting in that capacity, where the object of the party is to obtain a more exact and complete knowledge of the law, affecting his rights, obligations or duties relative to the subject-matter to which such communications relate.” 

Potter v. Inhabitants of Ware, 1 Cush. (Mass.), 519 (1848).

In the trial of an action in the court of common pleas the attorney who drew the writ and who was also actively enaged in the trial, having opened the case for the jury, was called by the plaintiff as a witness. To this the defendant objected and after a verdict for the plaintiff took the case to the Supreme Court. The latter court declared that: "By the common law, persons are competent witnesses, unless they are made incompetent by want of capacity, or of religious faith, by infamy, or by direct interest in the result of the cause. 

Witnesses are every day permitted to testify, whose wishes for the success of the party who calls them are as strong as those of the party's attorney or counsel.

And until the long-established rules of evidence are changed by legislative enactment, we cannot exclude a witness, merely because his testimony is to be given in behalf of his client. . . . In most cases, counsel cannot testify for their clients without subjecting themselves to just reprehension. But there may be cases in which they can do it, not only without dishonor, but in which it is their duty to do it. Such cases, however, are rare; and whenever they occur, they necessarily cause great pain to counsel of the right spirit." 

Chapin v. Lapham, 20 Pick. (Mass.), 467 (1838).

In an action brought to recover the amount paid by the plaintiff on a note signed by himself and the de fendant's minor son, it was claimed that the plaintiff signed merely as a surety and at the implied request of the defendant. In the course of the trial the plaintiff's counsel called the defendant's counsel to the witness stand to prove what took place on a previous trial. The latter, not recollecting the evidence, was requested to look at his minutes of the trial then lying upon the table to refresh his recollection. This he refused to do unless so ordered by the court. The court gave him directions accordingly. After a verdict for the plaintiff, the defendant excepted. The Supreme Court (per Chief 

Justice Shaw) declared that the judge had a right to order the witness to refresh his recollection and spoke in part as follows: "Such a witness is not only fully protected, but he is inhibited by law from disclosing any facts communicated to him, or of which he has acquired a knowledge, in consequence of his being professionally engaged in the cause. . . . The question then is whether a witness who has the means of power can lawfully be

required to look at such papers, to enable him to ascertain a fact with more precision, to verify a date, or to give more exact testimony than he otherwise could, as to times, sums, members, quantities and the like There may be times undoubtedly, in which it would be a great hardship upon a witness to require him to qualify himself, so to speak, to testify, by reference to papers and documents in his power; as when it would subject him to much trouble and expense, or involve any breach of confidence, or duty or of honorary obligation, or unreasonably disclose a knowledge of his own affairs. But there are other cases, in which it would lead to an entire perversion and frustration of the purposes of justice, if a witness could not be required to refresh his memory.... It is a question of sound judicial discretion, for the judge at the trial, to direct how the examination shall be conducted." 

§ 22. Waiver of Privilege. 

Green v. Crapo, 181 Mass. 55, 62 N. E. 956 (1902).

In an appeal from a decision of the Probate Court it was proposed to inquire of the appellant's attorney, while on the witness stand in her behalf, concerning certain conversations had with his client. This was objected to on the ground that the same were privileged communications. It was proved, however, that in the original trial in the Probate Court both the lawyer and the client voluntarily testified as to these conversations. 

The court held that: "The privacy for the sake of which the privilege was created was gone by the appellant's own consent, and the privilege does not remain under such circumstances for the mere sake of giving the client an additional weapon to use or not at his choice.

Whether a different rule should obtain with regard to testimony tending to criminate the witness is unnecessary to decide." 

§ 23. Husband and Wife. 

Raynes v. Bennett, 114 Mass. 424 (1874).

The defandant was being sued for jewelry purchased by his wife shortly before and some of it on the day she' deserted him. He defended on the ground that the goods were not necessaries, and that they were purchased without his consent. The defendant's wife was called to the stand as a witness for the plaintiff. Hen evidence was as follows: "Q. “Had you been in the habit of purchasing goods there?" She replied: "At one time my husband told me I could go and buy goods there." Q. "What was the conversation between you and your husband, about your buying goods at plaintiff's store?" The question was objected to and the objection was over-ruled. Her answer was: "I don't remember, only he told me at one time I could get spoons and things there." The Supreme Court held that: "The testimony of the wife as to conversations between herself and her husband when alone were inadmissible by the common law and our statutes have not changed the law in this respect. Dexter v. Booth, 2 Allen 559." 

Baldwin v. Parker, 99 Mass. 79, 96 Am. Dec. 697 (1868).

A witness was asked on cross examination, whether at a certain date he had heard anything on a certain subject in a family of which he and his wife were members, the questioner specifying that he did not wish the witness, in answering, to name the conversation in which nor the person from whom he had heard it. 

This ques-

tion was objected to so far as it related to information derived by the witness from private conversations with his wife unless he should be allowed to state the details of the conversation. The presiding judge ruled that the witness could not testify to private conversations between himself and his wife, and that the question should be modified so as either to exclude information so derived, or not to confine the source of information to that family. The Supreme Court held that there was no ground of exception to this ruling. ... 

Dexter v. Booth, 2 Allen (Mass.), 559 (1861).

The plaintiff, a jeweler, brought an action against an executor to recover the price of goods purchased by the testator's wife. The widow was called to the witness stand by the plaintiff and was permitted to testify that after the purchase of the goods in question her husband ratified it in a private conversation with her. The question was taken to the Supreme Court where it was decided that the testimony was improper. "It is admitted," said the court, "that at common law she is excluded from testifying to confidential conversations between herself and her husband, and that the exclusion remains unaffected by his death. The question is, whether this rule extends to his ratification of a purchase made by her, which in its nature does not seem to be confidential though made in a private conversation. 

Our legislature has by statute extended the compe tency of witnesses as far as it was deemed safe to do so; and when it makes husbands and wives admissible, it provides 'that they shall not be allowed to testify as to private conversations with each other.' This includes conversations on subjects which are not confidential in their nature."

Fay v. Guynon, 131 Mass. 31 (1881). 

Michael Guynon was frequently drunk and disorderly.

A certain parcel of land was conveyed to his wife (presumably by a relative) with the provision "that the said Mary Guynon hereby agrees to furnish a home and support to her said husband when he is sober and well behaved, not otherwise. In spite of this provision for his benefit, Michael soon relapsed into his drunken ways.

He beat and abused his wife and conducted himself in such a beastly manner that he was arrested for drunkenness and sentenced to prison. After he was released from prison the drunken husband demanded that his wife furnish him support as before. When she refused he caused an action to be brought in the name of the donor of the land. He testified that he had a conversation with his wife about coming home at the time of his release from prison and that his wife's sister was present and might or might not have heard this conversation. 

The wife objected to the admissibility of the conversation on the ground that it was a private conversation between husband and wife. The court held that the conversation was not private and also that the unfortunate wife was obliged to support her husband whenever he was sober. 

24. Evidence of Eavesdroppers, etc. 

Hoy v. Morris, 13 Gray (Mass.), 519, 74 Am. Dec. 650 (1859).

This was an action for breach of promise of marriage. 

In order to prove the promise the plaintiff offered as one of her witnesses Thomas Aldrich, who testified to the following facts: A few days after the defendant's marriage Aldrich was in the office of an attorney at law

when the defendant and Ferguson came in and asked to see the lawyer privately. They passed into a private office. Aldrich overheard the defendant tell the lawyer that he was afraid that the plaintiff might sue him for breach of promise and therefore wished to convey cer tain real estate to Ferguson. This evidence was objected to on the ground that it was of a privileged communication. The trial judge over-ruled the objection, and the case was taken to the Supreme Court where the decision in favor of plaintiff was sustained. "The privilege of exemption from testifying to facts already known," said the court, "is extended only to an attorney or legal adviser who derives his knowledge from the communication of a client who applies and makes his disclosures to him in his professional character, and to those other persons whose intervention is strictly necessary to enable the parties to communicate with each other. . . . Aldrich was not an attorney, nor in any way connected with Mr. Todd; and certainly in no situation where he was either necessary or useful to the parties to enable them to understand each other. On the contrary he was a mere bystander, and casually overheard conversation not addressed to him nor intended for his ear, but which the attorney and client meant to have respected as private and confidential. Mr. Todd could not have revealed it, but in consequence of a want of proper precaution, the communication between him and his client was overheard by a mere stranger." 

Foster v. Hall, 12 Pick. (Mass.) 89 (1831).

In a controversy over the right to possession of land the tenants called Robinson, an attorney, and sought to have him testify to a conversation with the plaintiff

with reference to the land, tending to show fraud on the part of the plaintiff. This evidence was objected to and excluded. The facts under which the conversation took place were as follows: Robinson's office in Lowell, where he was planning to settle, was not ready. He went to pass the day in the office of an attorney who was absent at the time Foster called. The latter asked him if he were a lawyer, and upon an affirmative reply stated to him the circumstances under which he held the land. 

Robinson was never paid for the advice given to Foster. 

The Supreme Court held that the communication was privileged. "The rule in such a case," said the court, “is, that the privilege of confidence is the privilege of the client and not of the attorney, and therefore whether the facts shall be disclosed or not, must depend upon the just application of the rule of law, and not upon the will of the witness. . . . That no fee was paid is immaterial; the legal obligation to pay a quantum meruit being in this respect as effectual a retainer as an actual payment. . . As Mr. Robinson knew nothing except what Ne hemiah Foster communicated to him as an attorney, for the purpose of obtaining legal advice, we think that his testimony was rightly rejected." 

Hatton v. Robinson, 14 Pick. (Mass.), 416, 25 Am. Dec. 415 (1834). 

This was an action of trespass for taking certain personal property. The defendant had attached the property as belonging to a debtor, Winch. The plaintiff claimed under a bill of sale from Winch, which the defendant alleged to be fraudulent. In support of this allegation he offered a deposition by Ames, an attorney. 

The latter, in his deposition declared that Winch enployed him to draw up the bill of sale and explained that

he was trying to raise money to pay his creditors in a certain city, but would be unable to pay his other creditors. Winch expressed a fear that the other creditors. might attach the goods unless he gave the bill of sale. 

This action would be deemed an act of bankruptcy. The evidence was objected to, but was admitted and the plaintiff became non-suit. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision, saying: "We cannot perceive that the communications were made to him by Winch with the purpose of instructing him in any cause or engaging him in the conduct of any professional business, or of obtaining any legal advice or opinion. . . . Here was no legal advice asked, no opinion requested as to the effect and operation of such a conveyance in point of law, and none given. . . . In such case, the fact that the agent sustains the character of an attorney, does not render the communications attending it, privileged; and they may be testified to by him, as by any other agent." 

§ 25. Unlawfully Obtained Evidence. 

Commonwealth v. Tucker, 189 Mass. 457 (1905).

The defendant was on trial for murder. He objected to the introduction in evidence of a broken sheath knife found in his coat pocket on the ground that such evi dence was unlawfully obtained. The facts were that officers of the law obtained access to the defendant's room on pretence of making search for a stolen phonograph. The court admitted the evidence and the Supreme Court sustained the decision. 

Commonwealth v. Brelsford, 161 Mass. 61 (1894).

The defendant was on trial for keeping and maintaining a liquor nuisance. He objected to the introduction

of evidence of samples of liquor taken from the cellar of his dwelling house, claiming that the same were unlawfully obtained. The Supreme Court declared: "It does not appear that the samples of liquor analyzed had been illegally taken from the defendant's premises by the officers; but if they had been, this fact does not render the evidence that they were found by analysis to contain more than one per cent of alcohol incompetent."

§ 28. Impeachment of One's Own Witness. 

Commonwealth v. Starkweather, 10 Cush. (Mass.), 59 (1852).

In a prosecution for perjury the defendant offered as a witness the daughter of the defendant Doolittle in the suit in which the alleged perjury occurred. Doolittle had denied that he ever purchased or carried to his home the goods sued for by Starkweather. When the daughter took the stand she confirmed her father's testimony in the previous suit. Starkweather then proposed by other witnesses to prove that the daughter had recently declared to them that her father did purchase the goods of Starkweather and that he did bring them home, but de-

clared that she must not tell of it lest her father punish her. The evidence was objected to and excluded. The 

Supreme Court affirmed the decision, saying: "The witness was the defendant's own witness, not called of necessity as an attesting witness but freely at the party's own option. By offering her, he offers her as a person entitled to belief, and therefore is not allowed to go into proof merely to discredit her. . . . It is undoubtedly true that a party is not bound by the facts testified by his own witness, but he may prove the facts to be otherwise, by any competent evidence pertinent to the issue. This may in a certain sense contradict the witness, but the purpose is not to discredit him, but to prove the issue on his own part, which is a legitimate and proper purpose." 

Commonwealth v. Welch, 4 Gray (Mass.), 535 (1855).

In a prosecution in the court of common pleas the first witness called by the government testified that he did not know the defendant's business and did not know where his shop was situated. He testified with a great deal of reluctance and manifested a strong disposition to conceal the facts which he knew, frequently evading the questions put to him by the district attorney. The latter asked the following question: "Did you not swear before the grand jury that you did know the defendant's business, and that you did know where the defendant's shop was situated?" Objection was duly made, but the judge permitted the question to be answered. The defendant was found guilty. The Supreme Court sustained the exceptions, saying: "The evidence of what the witness testified before the grand jury ought not to have been received. It bore upon no question pertinent

IMPEACHMENT OF WITNESSES 

63 to the issue. It was not to neutralize the effect of evidence given by the witness against the party calling him; for the witness had given none. It could only be to disparage the witness, and show him unworthy of credit with the jury, which was inadmissible." 

Adams v. Wheeler, 97 Mass. 67 (1867).

This was an action of tort, brought against a deputy sheriff, for conversion of a horse and wagon which he had attached and sold as the property of Eliza Adams, the mother of the plaintiff. Eliza was summoned by the plaintiff, but was not called upon to testify. The defendant, however, called her to the stand, expecting to prove by her that the plaintiff was not the owner of the property. She testified that the plaintiff was and always had been the owner. The defendant, alleging that he was surprised by this testimony, offered a witness to prove that on the day before the alleged conversion Eliza told him that the property was hers and that no one else had any title to it or lien on it. Upon objec tion the evidence was excluded. The Supreme Court approved the action of the court, saying: "A party may doubtless introduce evidence of any competent and material fact, although the fact has been denied by one of his own witnesses, and although the evidence may have the effect of discrediting that witness; but he cannot be allowed to introduce evidence for the mere purpose of impeaching the credit of a witness whom he has himself produced." 

§ 29. Impeachment of Witnesses of the Adversary. 

(See textbook.)

§ 30. Impeachment by Character. 

Commonwealth v. Churchill, 11 Metc. (Mass.) 538, 45 Am. 

Dec. 229 (1846).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for "lewdly and lasciviously associating and cohabiting with Lavina Cash." At the tral in the court of common pleas 

Lavinia testified in behalf of the commonwealth. For the purpose of discrediting her testimony, the defendant offered to prove that Lavina then was and for ten years had been, a common prostitute "and obtained her living, in whole or in part, by the prostitution of her person" for hire and gain. Upon objection this evidence was excluded. In the appeal it was pointed out that in Com monwealth v. Murphy, 14 Mass. 387, this very type of evidence was declared admissible. Chief Justice Shaw in over-ruling the objections took occasion to over-rule the case cited, declaring that it was a decision "made in the course of a capital trial, and probably not much time for deliberation, or reference to authorities." 

Commonwealth v. Stevenson, 127 Mass. 446 (1879).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for false pretences in persuading Eliza D. Mayo to sign a lease. Eliza was of course the chief witness against him. For the purpose of impeaching her testimony, the defendant sought to introduce evidence that in divorce proceedings between Eliza and her husband, the jury had found her guilty of adultery with James M. Huse. 

The judge excluded the evidence. In affirming the ruling of the trial judge the Supreme Court declared that the record of conviction for adultery did not show the conviction of the witness of any crime which, under the statute would be admissible to affect her credibility."

IMPEACHMENT OF WITNESSES 

65 

§ 31. Conviction of Crime as Ground for Impeachment. 

Commonwealth v. Walsh, 196 Mass. 369 (1907).

The defendant was tried upon a complaint for the illegal selling of intoxicating liquors. He offered himself as a witness in his own defence and in cross-examination was asked, “Have you ever been convicted of illegally keeping intoxicating liquor for sale?" Although the record of conviction was not produced in court, the defendant was compelled to answer and replied in the affirmative. After his conviction he carried the case to the 

Supreme Court on exceptions. The exceptions were sustained. The court discussed the matter in part as follows: "It has been argued in behalf of the commonwealth that the rule should not be applied to parties, but should be confined to witnesses not parties. No such distinction can be drawn from the language of the statute. . . . It is next urged that these decisions should be over-ruled and the rule established permitting proof of such conviction by a cross-examination of the witness. 

We see no sufficient reason for over-ruling Commonwealth v. Quin, ubi supra. It has been an established rule of practice in this commonwealth for many years, and has its foundation in the common law of England. While the doctrine of stare decisis does not prevent re-examination and correction of principles previously declared we have no question that the practice prevailing in this jurisdiction has been correctly expounded in the cases we are now asked to over-rule." 

Commonwealth v. Bonner, 97 Mass. 587 (1867).

The defendant was on trial for an attempt to commit larceny from the person of a woman by picking her

1 pocket. He took the stand in his own behalf. On crossexamination he was asked "if he had been in the house of correction for any crime?" The defendant's counsel waived any objection to the question on the ground that the record was the proper evidence of the conviction of the defendant for any crime; but objected to it on the ground that the defendant had not put his character in issue. The court ruled that the government might show that the defendant had been convicted of a crime, to affect his credibility as a witness but not to impeach his character (the issue not having been raised.) The defendant then testified that he had been imprisoned for larceny, for breaking and entering and for an attempt to rescue a prisoner. His exceptions to the ruling of the court were over-ruled by the Supreme Court. The court said: "The statute allowed the defendant, at his election, to become a witness. . . . By availing himself of the privilege he assumed the character of a witness, and subjected himself to the liabilities incident to that position.

The statute does not exempt him from cross-examination and impeachment as a witness; and there is no reason why he should be exempt from it." 

Commonwealth v. Danton, 243 Mass. 552 (1923).

The defendant was on trial for larceny. In cross-examination he was asked if he was not practicing medicine wthout being a registered physician. Under protest, he testified that he had been tried in the district court on that charge, but was acquitted. Evidence was introduced of his conviction in the district court of having narcotics in his possession. It appeared, however, that he had appealed from this judgment and that in the Superior Court the case was nol prossed. He testi-

IMPEACHMENT OF WITNESSES 

67 fied however, that he was acquitted in the district court. 

The judge allowed the record of the latter court to be introduced to contradict him. Subject to the defendant's exception the court permitted evidence to be introduced that in 1920 he was licensed to operate an automobile under the name of De Vell. The Supreme Court de clared that the lower court erred in admitting evidence of other offences not in any way connected with the larceny and having no tendency to show that he was guilty of that crime. In respect to his credibility as a witness the court said: "The record of the district court showing that the defendant had been found guilty of having narcotics in his possession, from which decision he appealed, was not admissible to affect the defendant's credibility, as it appeared that the case was subsequently nol prossed in the Superior Court. It was not a conviction of the defendant. . . . It could not be used to contradict him, and was inadmissible for this purpose."

§ 32. In General. 

33. Burden of Establishing a Case.

35. Proof Beyond Reasonable Doubt.
