38. Summary of Burden of Proof. 

§ 33. Burden of Establishing a Case. 

Hingeston v. Kelly, 18 L. J. (Exch.) 360 (1849), Thayer, 76.

The plaintiff, a lawyer, with the assent of the defendant, acted for him as an election agent in a contest in which the defendant was seeking election to Parliament. 

Not being paid for his services, the plaintiff brought suit. The defendant contended that the plaintiff had agreed to act in his behalf gratuitously and pointed out the fact that the plaintiff had voted in the election, this being unlawful for a paid agent to do. At the trial the court instructed the jury that the plaintiff, “having proved the services rendered, was prima facie entitled to be paid, and that they should find for the plaintiff, unless the defendant had distinctly proved to their satis-

faction that the contract was that the services were to be gratuitous, in which case they ought to find for the defendant." The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff. The defendant's counsel applied for a new trial on the ground of misdirection. The appellate court granted a new trial, saying: "The burthen of proof was never altered. The plaintiff being a professional man, and performing professional services, was prima facie entitled to remuneration. His voting, indeed, was an act which amounted to a statement by himself that he was not to be paid. Still, if the case had rested there, the jury, notwithstanding the voting, might have believed that the contract was that the plaintiff was to be paid. Then came the evidence for the defendant to show that the agreement was that the plaintiff should not be paid. 

After this was given, the question for the jury still remained, whether on the whole evidence the plaintiff had made out his title to remuneration." 

Abrath v. North Railway Co., 11 Q. B. D. 440 (1883), Thayer, 78.

Action for malicious prosecution. The judge instructed the jury that in establishing want of probable cause it was necessary for the plaintiff to prove that the railway company did not take reasonable care to inform themselves. The jury returned a verdict for the defendants. The plaintiff applied to the Queen's Bench for a new trial on the ground of misdirection. The judges in this court ruled that the burden was upon the defendants to prove that they had reasonable and probable cause since they were the only ones who knew the facts. 

Thus the case was overturned. The defendant appealed. 

The appellate court reversed the judgment of the court

of Queen's Bench and affirmed the decision of the trial court. "The ground of our decision," said the court, "comes back to what was suggested. Who had to make good their point as to the proposition whether the defendants had taken reasonable and proper care to inform themselves of the true state of the case? The defendants were not bound to make good anything. It was the plaintiff's duty to show the absence of reasonable care; and it is, I think, because the court below has been drawn, if I may use the expression, into thinking that what Cave J., was really leaving to the jury was an issue upon a subordinate fact, that I respectfully differ from their view." 

Barry v. Butlin, 2 Moore P. C. 480 (1838), Thayer, 82. 

Pendock Barry died at the age of seventy-six, leaving a will in which he left £3,000 to his attorney, Percy; £2,000 to the defendant, who was named executor, and £3,000 to his butler. The plaintiff was the only son of 

Pendock Barry. He contested the validity of the will on the ground that its execution was procured by fraud and conspiracy of the three legatees above named. Upon trial the court found the will to have been properly exe cuted and entered a decree accordingly. Upon the question of burden of proof the case was carried to the appellate court where the decision was affirmed. "The onus probandi," said the court, "lies in every case upon the party propounding the will; and he must satisfy the conscience of the court that the instrument so propounded is the last will of a free and capable testator."

Another rule was stated to be, "that if a party writes or prepares a will, under which he takes a benefit, that is a circumstance that ought generally to excite the sus-

picion of the court, and calls upon it to be vigilant and jealous in examining the evidence in support of the instrument, in favor of which it ought not to pronounce unless the suspicion is removed, and it is judicially satisfied that the paper propounded does express the true will of the deceased. . . . Whitehead's authority and power over his master is, no doubt, sufficiently established; but that such authority and power were in any way exercised to procure this will to be made is only conjecture; and there is nothing like proof of authority or control of any kind on the part of Butlin or Percy." 

Davis v. United States, 160 U. S. 469 (1895), Thayer, 90.

This was a prosecution for murder in which the defence was insanity. The trial court instructed the jury that the defence of insanity could not avail the accused unless it appeared affirmatively to the reasonable satisfaction of the jury, that he was not criminally responsible for his acts. "The court further declared that the fact of the killing being clearly proved, the legal presumption, based upon the common experience of mankind that every man is sane, was sufficient to authorize a verdict of guilty, although the jury might entertain a reasonable doubt upon the evidence whether the accused, by reason of his mental condition was criminally responsible. In other words, if the evidence was in equilib rium on the question of sanity the presumption of sanity would prevail. The U. S. Supreme Court over-ruled this decision, saying that the burden was on the government to prove guilt beyond a reasonable doubt. That burden is not fully discharged," said the court, "nor is there any legal right to take the life of the accused, until guilt is made to appear from all the evidence of the

case. His guilt cannot in the very nature of things be regarded as proved if the jury entertain a reasonable doubt from all the evidence whether he was legally capable of committing crime. 

People v. Cannon, 139 N. Y. 32, Thayer, 92.

The defendant was found guilty of violating a statute that forbade the use in certain ways of stamped and registered bottles and declaring that possession of them by second-hand dealers, without written consent, "presumptive evidence of said unlawful use." The validity of the statute was attacked by the defendant in his appeal. The court declared the statute constitutional, 'saying: "A provision of this kind does not take away or im pair the right of trial by jury. It does not in reality and finally change the burden of proof. The people must at all times sustain the burden of proving the guilt of the accused beyond a reasonable doubt. It, in substance, enacts that, certain facts being proved, the jury may regard them, if believed, as sufficient to convict, in the absence of explanation or contradiction. . . . Without the aid of the statute, the presumption provided for therein might not arise from the facts proved, although the statute says they shall be sufficient to authorize such presumption. The legislature has power to make these facts sufficient to authorize the presumption and the jury has the power, in the absence of all other evidence, to base its verdict thereon, if satisfied that the defendant is guilty. But the jury must in all cases be satisfied of guilt beyond a reasonable doubt, and the enactment in regard to the presumption merely permits, but cannot in effect direct the jury to convict under any circumstances."

Jones v. Bond, 40 Fed. Rep. 281 (1889), Thayer, 94.

This was an appeal from a decision under a statute which provided: "In all actions against railroad companies for damage done to persons or property, proof of injury inflicted by the running of the locomotives or cars of such company shall be prima facie evidence of the want of reasonable skill and care upon the part of the servants of such company in reference to such injury."

The defendant was the receiver of a railroad company. 

The offence alleged was the killing of a dog. The engineer and fireman running the train testified that the killing was not the result of negligence on their part, but was the fault of the dog which rendered the killing unavoidable. The lower court found for the defendant. 

On appeal the decision was affirmed. "The reason for this exceptional rule of evidence," said the court, "is that these injuries are often committed (especially on property) when no one else observes them except the employees operating the train; and this is often the case in regard to injuries done to persons. Hence, after the injury is admitted or proved, it is but reasonable that the railroad company '. shall be called upon to explain how the accident or injury occurred. But I be lieve a fair construction of the statute is that as soon as the prima facie case thus made out is rebutted by the evidence on the part of the defendant, the whole testimony is considered as in other cases. . . the controversy to be decided by the weight of the evidence." 

Burnham v. Noyes, 125 Mass. 85 (1878).

This was an action of tort for conversion of a check signed by the defendant. The declaration alleged that the defendant gave a check to D which was endorsed

by the latter to the plaintiff. The defendant stopped payment on the check. At a personal conference with an agent of the plaintiff he secured possession of the check on a pretence that he wished to examine it, but thereafter refused to return it. The plaintiff contended that the conversion of the note and the defendant's conduct with reference thereto constituted fraud, liability for which would not be affected by the defendant's subsequent discharge in bankruptcy. At the trial the court instructed the jury that if the defendant did any acts in relation to the retaining and suppressing the check that were unlawful for him to do, thereafter no presumption existed that he was innocent of wrongful intent in so taking and retaining it. In such case, the court declared, the burden was on the defendant to prove that he acted honestly and under a belief that he had a right to take and retain the check. The Supreme Court declared this instruction erroneous because it "leaves out of view the fact that fraud by the defendant is an integral part of the plaintiff's case, as to which the burden of proof is on him, and the principle of law that such burden is on him to the end."

S.34. Preponderance of Evidence Civil Cases. 

Schmidt v. New York Mutual Fire Ins. Co., 1 Gray (Mass.) 

529 (1854). 

Schmidt brought action against the defendant on a policy of insurance on his dwelling house and barn. The answer set forth the following (1) "That the fire occurred through the negligence of the plaintiff." (2) "That the fire was set with the previous knowledge and consent of the plaintiff." (3) "That the fire was set by the plaintiff and was his own fraudulent and wilful act." 

The court instructed the jury that the burden of proof

upon this issue was upon the defendants to prove the same to the reasonable satisfaction of the jury. The plaintiff contended that the proof of this allegation against him must be established beyond a reasonable doubt. The Supreme Court upheld the ruling of the lower court and quoted Greenleaf on Evidence thus: "The jury in the civil action must decide upon the mere preponderance of evidence, whereas, in order to a criminal conviction, they must be satisfied of the party's guilt, beyond any reasonable doubt."

Richardson v. Burleigh, 3 Allen (Mass.), 479 (1862).

Clara Richardson, being about to be delivered of a bastard child, charged the defendant with the dubious distinction of being its father. The bastardy complaint was tried in the Superior Court. The defendant requested the court to rule that on the evidence the jury must be satisfied of the truth of the charge beyond a reasonable doubt. The judge declined so to rule and instructed the jury that they might find for the defendant if they were satisfied that the preponderance of the evidence indicated that he was the father of the child. The 

Supreme Court affirmed the ruling, saying: "In cases strictly criminal it is an imperative rule of evidence that the allegations of the prosecutor must be proved beyond reasonable doubt. In others, the law, in general, only requires that the fact in issue shall be established by the party having the burden of proof, to the reasonable satisfaction of the jury." 

Anderson v. Edwards, 123 Mass. 273 (1877).

The defendant was on trial for fraudulent concealment of assets. It was alleged that after contracting the debts

later sued upon the defendant purchased in the name of his wife a certain house in Northampton for $3,200 paying for it in whole or in part out of assets that should have been reserved for creditors. The defendant requested the court to rule that the burden of proof was on the creditors to prove the alleged fraud of the debtor beyond a reasonable doubt, but the court instructed the jury that they might find the defendant guilty upon any of the alleged acts of fraud which in their judgment were supported by a clear preponderance of evidence. 

The defendant was found guilty and alleged exceptions. 

The Supreme Court affirmed the decision saying that: "Proceedings under the Gen. Sts. c. 124, for the relief of poor debtors arrested on mesne process or execution, are essentially in their nature civil proceedings. . . . We are of opinion that it is not the purpose or effect of this provision to change the rules of evidence and to put upon the creditor the burden of a greater stringency of proof, It follows, therefore, that the Superior Court correctly held that the rule of evidence, applied in stricty criminal cases, was not applicable to this case." 

35. Proof Beyond Reasonable Doubt. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 36. Burden of Proving Affirmative Allegation. 

Crowningshield v. Crowningshield, 2 Gray (Mass.), 524 (1854), Thayer, 100.

Appeal by the heirs at law of Edward Crowningshield from a decree of the Probate Court allowing a will. Issues were framed for a jury. Edward was under guardianship as an insane person at the time of the making of the will. The court instructed the jury that the burden

of proof in a case of this kind was on the proponents of the will to prove that the testator was sane at the time of executing the will. The jury found that the testator was non compos mentis. The case was taken to the Supreme Court where the verdict was upheld. "We can perceive here no shifting of the burden of proof,” said the court. "The issue throughout is but one: was the testator of sound mind? And the affirmative of this was upon the party offering the will for probate. Again, that issue is an issue of fact, and is to the jury. . . . Nor does the existence of a general presumption that men are sane change the burden of proof. It may stand in the stead of proof; it may make a prima facie case; where the question of sanity is made, it may render necessary greater weight of evidence in him who seeks to impeach it; but it does not change the burden of proof. But when the evidence is in, on the one side and the other, the issue still continues as before; and he to whose case the proof of such sanity is necessary has the burden." 

Broaders v. Toomey, 9 Allen (Mass.), 65 (1864).

The plaintiff brought suit to recover for board furnished to the defendant's minor son upon the agreement of defendant to pay for the same. The defendant admitted that there was originally an agreement to that effect, but alleged in defense to the action that the plaintiff had subsequently agreed that the boy's labor should be taken as equivalent to his board. He asked the court to rule that the burden was on the plaintiff through the whole case to satisfy the jury that the defendant agreed to pay for the boy's board, and that if this were left doubtful the plaintiff could not recover. The judge so ruled but added that "if the defendant relies upon

such new and subsequent agreement, modifying and altering the original one, the burden of proof as to such new agreement is on him." The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendant alleged exceptions. 

The Supreme Court over-ruled the exceptions and declared that the effect of the judge's ruling was that the burden of proof was on the plaintiff throughout. The defendant, however, was under the burden of proving the affirmative defence relied upon by him to defeat the plaintiff's action. 

Gray v. Gardner, 17 Mass. 188 (1821).

This was an action of assumpsit on a written promise dated April 14, 1819, to pay $5,198.87, with the following condition annexed: "On condition that if a greater quantity of sperm oil should arrive in whaling vessels at Nantucket and New Bedford, on or between the first day of April and the first day of October of the pres ent year, both inclusive, than arrived at said places, in whaling vessels, on or within the same term of time last year, then this obligation to be void." The defendant claimed that the condition had been fulfilled. The judge ruled that the burden of proving this allegation rested upon the defendant. The Supreme Court in affirming this ruling declared: "The very words of the contract show that there was a promise to pay, which was to be defeated by the happening of an event, viz., the arrival of a certain quantity of oil at the specified places, in a given time. It is like a bond with a condition; if the obligor would avoid the bond, he must show performance of the condition. The defendants, in this case, promise to pay a certain sum of money, on condition that the promise shall be void on the happening of an It is plain the burden of proof is upon them; event.

and if they fail to show that the event has happened the promise remains good." 

Thayer v. Connor, 5 Allen (Mass.), 25 (1862).

Connor who occupied a bird store in Boston contracted with Thayer to instal a furnace and other furniture in the said store. At the time of the agreement there was uncertainty whether Connor might not be ousted from the store by the acts of third parties. A note for a part of the price was now being sued upon. 

It read as follows: "Boston, Jan. 22, 1859. Three months after date, I promise to pay Lewis Thayer or order thirty dollars on demand, and if the said Connor should be removed from this building as used for a bird store then this note is null and void and the said Thayer is to have the furnace in the cellar. ((Signed) James 

Connor." The answer denied that the defendant was permitted to occupy the building. The judge instructed the jury that the burden was on the plaintiff to prove that the defendant was not removed from the store within the three months. The jury returned a verdict for the defendant, and the plaintiff alleged exceptions. 

These exceptions were sustained by the Supreme Court which declared: "The happening of the event was to defeat the promise, and it seems to us, upon the authority of Gray v. Gardner, the burden of proof was on the defendants to show that the event had happened."
