$39. Relevancy in General.

The Columbia, etc. R. R. v. Hawthorne, 133 U. S. 202 

(1892), Thayer, 229.

This action was brought in the District Court for the Territory of Washington, against a railroad corporation owning a saw-mill by a man who had been employed therein as a "trimmer" to recover for the defendant's negligence in providing an unsafe and defective machine. The accident was caused by the coming off of one of the pulleys which ran the belt transmitting power to the saw. The pulley fell upon the plaintiff and in-

RELEVANCY 

81 jured him. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff for $10,000 damages. The defendant appealed to the 

Supreme Court of the district which affirmed the judgment. He thereupon sued out a writ of error. The U. 

S. Supreme Court reversed the judgment and ordered a new trial because the trial court permitted evidence to be introduced proving that after the accident the defendant made alterations or repairs to the machine. 

This was deemed prejudicial and irrelevant, the court saying: "The evidence is incompetent, because the taking of such precautions against the future is not to be construed as an admission of responsibility for the past, has no legitimate tendency to prove that the defendant has been negligent before the accident happened, and is calculated to distract the minds of the jury from the real issue, and to create a prejudice against the defendant." 

Sawyer v. Arnold Shoe Co., 90 Me. 396 (1897), Thayer, 231.

The defendant corporation was sued for damages alleged to have been the result of the negligence of the corporation. It was brought out during the trial that the corporation was insured against liability for such injuries. The trial court charged the jury as follows: 

"Now it is contended by the plaintiff that they (referring to the defendant corporation) exercised no care, that they intrusted to their insurance. And there I think it admissible that it should appear that there was an insurance, or that they trusted to one rather than upon any investigation, inquiry, or experiment, or conduct otherwise of their own. The Supreme Court declared this erroneous, saying: "The fact that the de-

fendant was insured against accidents should have no legitimate bearing, it might very naturally have an improper influence upon the jury in passing upon the one question involved, whether or not the defendant had failed to exercise that degree of care which the law required of it. The burden of proving that the defendant had failed in this respect was upon the plaintiff, and we do not think that because the defendant had taken the precaution to be insured against accident, that it should have any influence with the jury in determining that question. . . . The question is not, as to how much or how little motive the defendant had for being careful, but whether or not it had in fact exercised due care." 

Cass v. Boston & Lowell R. R., 14 Allen (Mass.), 448 (1876), Thayer 248.

The plaintiff was suing to recover the value of a tub of sugar that had been left in the defendant's warehouse but had been stolen therefrom. The defendants offered to prove that the same care was exercised in relation to this property as was usually exercised by other railroad corporations in the vicinity, but the court excluded the evidence. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendants alleged exceptions. The Supreme 

Court sustained the exceptions, saying: "If the defendants exercised due and ordinary care in the custody of the property they cannot be charged for its loss. What constituted such care was a question of fact to be judged of with reference to all the circumstances, and especially with reference to the degree of care which other persons engaged in similar business in the vicinity were in the habit of bestowing on property similarly situated. 

The standard of ordinary care varies necessarily in dif-

RELEVANCY 

83 ferent localities. One degree of diligence would be required for the city, and a less or greater for the country; depending on a great variety of circumstances." 

The court said that the evidence offered should have been received. 

Maynard v. Buck, 100 Mass. 40 (1868), Thayer, 249.

Action of contract for the value of a pair of steers alleged to have been lost through the defendant's negligence. The defendant was a drover engaged in driving cattle from Brighton to various points between that place and Worcester. On November 9, 1865, he received from the plaintiff a pair of steers to drive from 

Brighton to Northborough for a stipulated price. Evidence was offered that the drove of cattle were frightened by a train at about dusk of the first day of the trip and that they stampeded into the adjoining fields; that as soon as he could, with the aid of his men, he got the drove back into the road and proceeded to the place where they were to remain for the night. The cattle were there counted and it was found that out of one hundred and twenty-three cattle nine were missing. In accordance with the custom of drovers these cattle were delivered before returning to seek the strayed animals. 

Seven of them were later found, but the steers were never located. The jury found for the plaintiffs and the defendant alleged exceptions because the judge refused to instruct the jury that "if he did the things that drovers of common prudence, engaged in the same business, ordinarily do, he was not guilty of such negligence as will make him liable in this action." The Supreme Court declared that "the usual practice made up of particular instances of conduct" was not admissible.

Said the court: "That which is admissible in evidence is, not the particulars, but what the witnesses state from their knowledge of those particulars to be usual, or the course ordinarily pursued. . . . The effect and purpose of the evidence is to aid the jury in forming their judgment of what the party was bound to do, or was justified in doing, under all the circumstances of the case." 

The exceptions were over-ruled. 

Wabash R. R. v. McDaniels, 107 U. S. 454 (1882), Thayer, 251. 

McDaniels, an employee of the delinquent company, brought suit for damages for injuries sustained in the collision of two freight trains brought about by the negligence of a telegraph operator employed by the road. 

Plaintiff was given a verdict which the court refused to set aside. The case was carried, by writ of error, to the 

U. S. Supreme Court where the judgment was affirmed. 

The defendant contended that the road was liable only for ordinary care. The court thus commented upon it: "To say as a matter of law, that a railroad corporation discharged its obligation to an employee-in respect to the fitness of co-employees whose negligence has caused him to be injured-by exercising, not that degree of care that ought to have been observed, but only such as like corporations are accustomed to observe, would go far towards relieving them of all responsibility whatever for negligence in the selection and retention of incompetent servants. If the general practice of such corporations in the appointment of servants is evidence which a jury may consider in determining whether, in the particular case, the requisite degree of care was observed, such practice cannot be taken as conclusive upon the inquiry as to the care which ought to have been exercised."

RELEVANCY 

85 

Sheldon v. Hudson River R. R., 14 N. Y., 218 (1856), Thayer, 252.

The plaintiff was the owner of a building called a mill house which stood about sixty-seven feet from the track of the defendant railroad. This building was destroyed 

February 7, 1852, by fire alleged by the plaintiff to have been set by a locomotive on the defendant's road. The outer doors of the mill were locked and no fire was used therein. On the floor of the first story (where the fire started) there were shavings and a quantity of lumber. 

Some of the glass in the windows had been broken. 

The plaintiff offered evidence that the day was windy, and also that a train passed about twenty minutes before the fire. A witness was offered who would testify that he had seen sparks and fire thrown out by the smokestack of the locomotive, and that he had picked up live coals after the train passed. This evidence was excluded. Other evidence was offered that the defendant's locomotives were not equipped with spark arresting devices. The trial court non-suited the plaintiff. The Supreme Court reversed the judgment saying: "It is argued by the defendant's counsel that the evidence offered and rejected was too remote and indefinite to have a just influence upon the particular question in issue, that it did not refer to any particular engine, and it may be that the one that ran past the plaintiff's premises, just before the discovery of the fire, was quite a different one from those which scattered fire on the occasion to which the evidence offered would apply. This argument is not without force; but at the same time I think it is met by the peculiar circumstances of this case. 

These engines run night and day, and with such speed that no particular note can be taken of them as they The evidence excluded had a bearing upon pass.

both branches of the case of which the plaintiff undertook to establish. It not only rendered it probable that the fire was communicated from the furnace of one of the defendant's engines, but it raised an inference of some weight, that there was something unsuitable and improper in the construction or management of the engine which caused the fire." 

Grand Trunk R. R. v. Richardson, 91 U. S. 454 (1875), Thayer, 255.

Action to recover damages for the destruction of sawmill, lumber shed, store, boarding house, manufactured lumber and other personal property alleged to have been set by a locomotive engine belonging to the defendant railroad. The fire broke out on the westerly end of a covered railroad bridge shortly after the locomotive had passed. The wind was blowing and the season was very dry so the fire spread with great rapidity. The bridge was more than one hundred feet long and its easterly end was within a few feet of the saw-mill. The volume of flame from the burning bridge set off the mill. The flames spread to the other property mentioned in the declaration. The jury brought in a verdict for $22,312, and the defendant alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court affirmed the judgment. Said the court: "The issue to be determined was, whether the defendant had been guilty of negligence; that is, whether it had failed to exercise that caution and diligence which the circumstances demanded, and which prudent men ordinarily exercise. Hence the standard by which its conduct was to be measured was not the conduct of other railroad companies in the vicinity; certainly not their usual conduct. . . . The usual practice of other

RELEVANCY 

87 companies in that section of the country sheds no light upon the duty of the defendant when running locomotives over long wooden bridges, in near proximity to frame buildings, when danger was more than commonly imminent." The court also said that evidence that at various times during the summer the defendant's locomotives had scattered fire was admissible. 

Murphy v. Stanley, 136 Mass. 133 (1883).

The defendant, a contractor engaged in razing a building piled some lumber on the wharf of the Philadelphia Coal and Iron Company, having obtained permission so to do. The plaintiff was a driver of a coal wagon engaged in transporting the coal from the wharf. 

He was suing defendant for injuries sustained by reason of stumbling over a protruding end of a railroad sleeper in the pile of lumber. In order to prove that the defendant was negligent in leaving the lumber as it was the plaintiff called as a witness the superintendent of the wharf who would testify that he had notified several of the defendant's workmen to tell the defendant to remove the sleepers. The judge ruled that such evidence would not be competent without proof that the notice was communicated to the defendant. One of the defendant's laborers was called. He testified that the superintendent had told him to notify his employer about the sleepers, but he had merely piled them more carefully and said nothing to the defendant. The judge excluded the evidence. After a verdict for the defendant the plaintiff alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence excluded was irrelevant. It made a similar decision as to evidence concerning lack of permission to pile the lumber on the wharf. "The

issue," said the court, "was, whether the defendant had been guilty of negligence; and the evidence as to license was material or immaterial, according as it did or did not bear upon that issue." 

Whitney v. Leominster, 136 Mass. 25 (1883).

The plaintiff brought an action against the town of Leominster for injuries sustained by reason of a defect in a certain street. It appeared in evidence that it was good sleighing at the time of the accident. The plaintiff claimed that he was travelling five or six miles an hour, but witnesses for the defendant testified that he was travelling at a high rate of speed, one witness setting it as fifteen miles per hour. This evidence was in support of the defendant's contention that the plaintiff was guilty of contributory negligence. Further evidence was offered that the horse of the plaintiff had been driven on a race course both before and after the date of the plaintiff's injury. The plaintiff objected to this evidence as irrelevant, but the judge admitted it. 

After a verdict for the defendant the plaintiff alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court over-ruled the exceptions saying: "The fact to be determined was undoubtedly the rate of speed at which the horse was driven at the time of the accident; but when the testimony showed a very high rate of speed, as bearing upon its reasonableness and probability, it was competent to show that he had great capacity for speed, even if it might also be true that he was not then driven at his full speed. 

It would have been competent for the plaintiff to show, in answer to the defendant's testimony, that the horse was incapable of such a rate as fifteen miles an hour, and that he could be driven or urged more than five

RELEVANCY 

89 miles per hour. The evidence offered and received here was of the same description, even if less conclusive." 

Niles v. Patch, 13 Gray (Mass.), 254 (1859).

The plaintiff sued the defendant in tort for "pulling down, carrying away and converting to his own use a gate and part of a wall" that stood across the entrance to a certain beach in Gloucester. He also charged with trespass in taking and carrying away sand and seaweed from the beach. The defendant claimed a right to the beach. The plaintiff offered as a witness Greenleaf who had occupied the premises before him, under a bond for a deed. Greenleaf testified that while so occupying the premises he had a controversy with Patch for similar trespass on the beach. The admission of this testimony was one of the grounds of defendant's appeal. 

The Supreme Court thus commented upon it: "As to the testimony actually given by Greenleaf, of the controversy with Patch, and the suit and settlement of it, the fact of the controversy with Patch was competent, as the acts and declarations of the party to the suit, offered by the adverse party, by way of admission or confession. As to what he testified respecting a suit at law, and the settlement of it, if it had been objected to on the ground that it was not the best evidence, and that the record ought to be produced, it would perhaps have been a good objection to the viva voce testimony on the point; but no such objection was made.”" 

Thayer v. Thayer, 101 Mass. 111 (1869).

Mrs. Thayer sued her husband for divorce on the ground of adultery with a Mrs. Parmalee, alleged to have been committed on October 2, 1866, the libel being

filed November 27, 1866. It appeared that Mrs. Parmalee had lived in the Thayer family for three years prior to the separation and filing of the libel. While there was evidence of acts of familiarity and circumstances of suspicion prior to the filing of the libel, yet the only really incriminating evidence related to acts of the alleged adulterers after the suit was begun. On crossexamination the libellant's attorney was permitted, against objection, to question the pair as to their acts at certain times and places named. Both denied immoral relations. The libellant then introduced witnesses to prove that at such times and places the libellee had committed adultery with Mrs. Parmalee. The judge admitted the evidence "for the purpose of showing what was the character of the relations between the libellee and Mrs. Parmalee, while living together in the same house before the separation of husband and wife, "and further ruled that such adulterous relations at any time prior to the trial had some tendency to show that their association before the filing of the libel was of an adulterous character." This ruling was appealed on the ground that it was contrary to the decision in Commonwealth v. Horton (2 Gray, Mass. 354) which held that adulterous acts subsequent to the filing of the libel could not be proved, and Commonwealth v. Thrasher (11 Gray, Mass. 450) which declared that evidence of adultery between the same parties prior to the date of the acts relied upon was inadmissible. The Supreme 

Court declared that in both cases there had been "a plain misapplication of the rules of evidence." "The evidence by which the act of adultery is proved," said the court, "is seldom direct. The natural secrecy of the act makes it ordinarily impossible to prove it, except by circumstantial evidence. But ..

RELEVANCY 

91 when adulterous disposition is shown to exist between the parties at the time of the alleged act, then mere opportunity, with comparatively slight circumstances showing guilt will be sufficient to justify the inference that criminal intercourse has actually taken place. The intent and disposition of the parties towards each other must give character to their relations. . . . An adulterous disposition existing in two persons towards each other is commonly of gradual development; it must have some duration and does not suddenly subside. 

When once shown to exist, a strong inference arises that it has had and will have continuance. . . . The concurrent adulterous disposition of the defendant and the particeps criminis cannot be shown by stronger evidence than the criminal act itself." The decree of divorce was affirmed. 

§ 40. Direct Evidence Confessions. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 41. Direct Evidence-Eye Witnesses. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 42. Indirect Evidence. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 43. Conduct of the Parties. 

Egan v. Bowker, 5 Allen (Mass.), 449 (1862).

In an action to recover for building à road over lan of defendants the latter offered to prove that the plain-

tiff, under an assumed name, had gone to a notary public with a girl and procured from her a false deposition concerning the case. This deposition was not in fact used. A later deposition by the girl was offered by the defendant in which she recited the facts of the alleged subornation of perjury, declaring the deposition to have been obtained while she was under the influence of liquor and under a promise of pecuniary reward, but that the statements then made were wholly false. 

This evidence was excluded. The Supreme Court sustained the defendant's exceptions, declaring, "These facts were in the nature of admissions implied from the conduct of the party that his claim against the defendant was false and unjust. The inference is a reasonable and proper one, that a person having an honest and fair debt which he claims to be due will not endeavor to support it by falsehood and fraud; and the fact that he resorts to such means of proof has a tendency to show that he knows he cannot maintain his suit by evidence derived from pure and incorrupt sources. Truth does not ally itself with falsehood, but falsehood will often endeavor to make it appear that truth is on its side. 

When, therefore, it is shown that a party to a suit has sought to suborn witnesses to swear falsely in his behalf, and has been guilty in his own person of fraud and deceit in the maintenance of his action, such evidence is competent, as an admission of the falsity or fraudulent nature of the claim."

Ryerson v. Inhabitants of Abington, 102 Mass. 526 (1869). 

This was an action to recover for personal injuries alleged to have been occasioned to the plaintiff by falling into a hole in a public street which the defendant town

RELEVANCY 

93 3335 was obliged to keep in repair. The defendant offered evidence that at a former trial of the same case the plaintiff's son-in-law had called him "a d- fool for walking so fast," when going from the court house to the depot while the jury were still deliberating on the case. 

The plaintiff was alleged to have replied that "it is all over now" and that he sat up near the court at the table and knew "how to play it on the judge." This evidence was excluded by the trial court but this exclusion was declared to be error by the supreme court. "The evidence of the plaintiff's statements in conversation with Wales," said the court, "should have been admitted, as bearing upon the question whether the plaintiff was fraudulently making a claim against the town, which he knew to be unfounded." 

§ 44. Motive. 

Commonwealth v. Hudson, 97 Mass. 565 (1867).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for burning in the night time a dwelling house occupied by him as a tenant. It appeared that the defendant kept a provision store in a room in the same building. There was insurance on his furniture and also on his stock in trade. No part of the insured property was burned. same.

It was proven that five days after the fire a deputy sheriff attached the defendant's provisions and sold the This witness and others testified that the value of both furniture and provisions was much less than the amount of the insurance. It was argued therefore that the defendant had a motive for setting the fires. The defendant was found guilty and alleged exceptions. The 

Supreme Court over-ruled the exceptions, saying: "This evidence we think was admissible. It tends to repel the

presumption which exists, in addition to the general presumption of innocence, that a man will not commit a crime without reason, inducement or temptation. The evidence should undoubtedly be of a motive peculiar and special to the person accused." 

§ 45. Facts showing Preparation. 

§ 46. Capacity and Opportunity. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 47. Character Evidence. 

Bodwell v. Swan, 3 Pick. (Mass.), 376 (1825). 

Miss Bodwell was schoolteacher against whom charges of unchasity were lodged with the school committee. These charges were laid before the committee by a prominent citizen. The injured lady sued her detractors. One of the suits was against the wife of Swan for having accused her with the crimes of fornication and adultery. In defence under the general issue the defendant sought to prove instances known to them of improper conduct on the part of the plaintiff with men, both married and unmarried. They also offered evidence of reports current in the town of the same or similar improprieties of conduct in order to show that in speaking thus of the plaintiff they were not acting maliciously. But the judge excluded all such evidence. 

The supreme court upheld the ruling, saying that the general reputation of the plaintiff might be proved but

RELEVANCY 

95 that particular instances of unchastity could not be offered "since it would be unreasonable to require of him to have witnesses to disprove particular facts, which he had no notice are intended to be proved against him." 

Stone v. Varney, 7 Metc. (Mass.), 86 (1843).

The defendant published in a newspaper the statement that the plaintiff was guilty of "heartless cruelty toward his child." Upon being sued for libel the defendant offered evidence in justification tending to prove the truth of the charge. He also offered to show, in mitigation of damages, that before the publication of the alleged libel the general reputation of the plaintiff, in the community, as a man of moral worth, was bad. This evidence was at first rejected by the court but in a later stage of the trial was admitted. A verdict for nominal damages was returned by the jury. The plaintiff alleged exceptions to the admission of the character evidence. 

The Supreme Court declared that the ruling of the lower court was correct. "The theory of trials," said the court, "is that the jury are to decide solely upon the evidence before them. If so, they surely cannot make the distinction between a plaintiff of good name and fame, and one who is really infamous, unless evidence of this fact is allowed to be given. Cases often occur where the jury are entire strangers to the parties, and if not so, they ought not to act upon the statements of any of their fellows, given in the jury room, and not under the sanction of an oath. There seems to be no alternative but to admit this species of evidence; and this as well when there is justification pleaded, as when the defence is on the general issue alone."

Clark v. Brown, 116 Mass. 504 (1875).

In an action of tort for slander it was alleged that the defendant went to the plaintiff's father and declared “Joseph, I accuse Frank of stealing my iron bar and 

I can prove it. By God, I can prove it. 

He stole my iron bar, cart pin and ox-yoke. By God, it is no secret, I am going to publish it." In another count the defendant was charged with making similar accucations to 

Vinton concerning the plaintiff, a minor. Upon the question of damages, the defendant called witnesses who testified that the plaintiff's reputation for honesty and integrity was bad. He offered to prove by the same witnesses that the plaintiff's reputation in respect to thieving was bad. This evidence was excluded and the defendant alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court declared that the exclusion of the evidence was improper. Said the court: "While there has been some discussion upon the question whether, in actions of this nature, the defendant might show that the general reputation of the plaintiff was bad, and different results have been reached by some of the courts of other states and in England, it is well settled in this Commonwealth that such evidence is admissible in mitigation of damages. . . . The action is for injury to the position and standing of the plaintiff among his fellows by the utterance of slanders tending to degrade him in their estimation, and perhaps expose him to punishment; and the defendant may show that the plaintiff's general reputation is already bad, with a view of showing that no serious injury can have been inflicted upon him. When, therefore, the plaintiff alleges this injury to have been occasioned by slanders affecting his character in any particular respect, it would fairly tend to mitigate the damages if it were shown that, at

RELEVANCY 

97 the time of the utterance of the slanders alleged, his general reputation in that respect is already bad." 

Commonwealth v. Hardy, 2 Mass. 317 (1807), Thayer, 272.

The defendant was on trial for murder. At the trial evidence was offered by the prisoner's counsel as to his character, urging that such testimony would have material bearing upon the inquiry in a case where the issue was doubtful, and also that if such evidence were rejected a man's good name would cease to be of value to him at a time where it ought to be inestimable. The court admitted the evidence, saying: "In doubtful cases, a good character, clearly established, ought to have weight with the jury; but ought not to prevail against the positive testimony of credible witnesses. Whenever the defendant chooses to call witnesses to prove his general character to be good, the prosecutor may offer witnesses to disprove their testimony. But it is not competent for the prosecutor to go into this inquiry until the defendant has voluntarily put his character in issue; and in such case there can be no examination as to particular facts." 

Commonwealth v. Leonard, 140 Mass. 473, 54 Am. Dec. 485 

Thayer, 273 (1886).

The defendant was indicted and placed on trial for the felonious receiving of stolen goods. It appeared that he was a licensed junk dealer and there was some question whether he received the goods under such circumstances that he would be presumed to know that they had been stolen. Two wholesale dealers in junk testified that the prices paid by the defendant for the goods in question

were reasonable. The defendant also introduced testimony of many respectable witnesses, among them the chief of the state police, aldermen and others, who testified to his good character. He also requested the judge to charge the jury that, "good character, like all other facts in the case, should be considered by the jury, and if therefrom a reasonable doubt is generated in the mind of the jury as to the guilt of the accused, it is their duty to acquit." The judge refused to give this instruction but gave instructions that amounted to saying that character evidence should not be considered by the jury unless the other evidence in the case created a doubt in their minds as to his guilt. The jury returned a verdict of guilty and the defendant alleged exceptions. 

The Supreme Court sustained these exceptions, saying: "If evidence of reputation is admissible at all, its weight should be left to be determined by the jury in connection with all the other evidence in the case. The circumstances may be such that an established reputation for good character, if it is relevant to the issue, would alone create a reasonable doubt in the minds of the jury, although without it the other evidence would be convincing. . . . The old practice of charging juries, that evidence of character was of little weight, except in doubtful cases, undoubtedly grew up when judges were accustomed to express their opinions to jurors upon matters of fact, and the weight to be given to evidence, and was perhaps sufficiently just in particular cases; but we think it ought not to be made a rule of universal application, that is, a rule of law; and since the passage of Gen. Sts. c. 115, sec. 5, it is open to the objection that it is charging juries upon the weight to be given to evidence, when the law, in our opinion, does not define the degree of weight to be attached to it."

RELEVANCY 

99 

Commonwealth v. O'Brien, 119 Mass. 342, 20 Am. Rep. 325.

The defendant was on trial under an indictment charging assault with a dangerous weapon with intent to kill one Ellis. During the trial the defendant offered evidence that he had a reputation of being a peaceable and quiet person. To rebut this evidence the prosecutor was permitted, over defendant's objection, to introduce a court record showing that in another instance the defendant had been indicted for assault on Walsh and had pleaded guilty, but the case had been placed on file. 

The Supreme Court held that the trial court erred in admitting the evidence. Said the court: "Where a party undertakes to show that his reputation is good, or that the reputation of the other party or a witness is bad, he cannot put in evidence of particular facts to prove the general reputation he is endeavoring to establish. And to meet evidence of general reputation the opposing party may put in evidence to the contrary of a like general character. But he cannot prove particular facts for the reason that a particular fact does not necessarily establish a general reputation or fairly meet the issue presented, and may also raise collateral issues; and for the further reason that while a party is presumed always to be ready to defend his general reputation, he is not expected to be prepared to meet a distinct and specific charge." 

Regina v. Rowton, Leigh & Cave, 520 (1865), Thayer, 275.

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for indecent assault on a boy fourteen years old. Several witnesses for the defendant testified to his good character as a moral and well conducted man. The prosecution called a witness to contradict this. Defendant

objected that such evidence was inadmissible but the court admitted the testimony which was as follows: "I know nothing of the neighborhood's opinion, because I was only a boy at school when I knew him; but my own opinion, and the opinion of my brothers who were also pupils of his, is that his character is that of a man capable of the grossest indecency and most flagrant immorality." The Appellate Court set aside the conviction because the evidence should be of general reputation with which the witness testified he was unfamiliar. 

The court said: "It must be restricted to the man's general reputation, and must not extend to the individual opinion of the witness." 

So. Kansas R. R. v. Robbins, 43 Kansas 145 (1890), Thayer, 268. 

Patterson, a passenger conductor, was killed while in the performance of his duty. His administrator brought suit against the company alleging that the negligence of the company was responsible for the death. On the day in question he was in charge of an excursion train. 

Owing to a scarcity of passenger coaches, two "caboose" cars had been attached to the train. In order to pass from one "caboose" to another it was necessary to climb over a box car that was in between them. Having collected the fares in one "caboose" he started for the other one and climbed up the end of the box car, clinging to the "hand-holds" as he went, for the train was travelling rapidly. Witnesses testified that as he had nearly reached the top of the car he apparently reached for a rung which should have been on the top but was not there. At the same time he apparently released his grip on the ladder and fell. Another conductor was permitted

RELEVANCY 

IOI to testify that the deceased was a careful and skilful conductor. For this error the decision in favor of the plaintiff was reversed and a new trial granted. Said the court: "The determination of whether he was exercising due care when he fell from the car does not depend upon the care exercised by him at any other times, or whether he was usually careful in the performance of his duties as a railroad man but does depend upon his conduct at the time of the accident. . . . Testimony of this character is no more admissible than an offer by he railroad company to show his want of care at the time of the accident by proving that he was negligent at other times, or generally careless." ... 

Harriman v. Pullman Co., 85 Fed. Rep. 353 (1898), Thayer, 270. 

The plaintiff, a passenger on a sleeping car, sued for injuries received by the alleged carelessness of a porter. 

She was sitting in the seat to which she had been assigned when she received a severe blow on the head from a partition between the berths which was being handled by the porter in making up the berths. Against plaintiff's objection the defendant was allowed to introduce evidence that the porter in question was usually careful, competent, courteous, and attentive. The jury returned a verdict for the defendant, but the Court of Appeals reversed the judgment and ordered a new trial, saying that the issue was not that the company had employed an incompetent or careless employee but that he was careless on this occasion. "When, therefore," said the court, "a complaint does not charge incompetency, but simply alleges that an employee acted carelessly on a given occasion, the proof should be confined to his acts

on that occasion, and should not embrace an inquiry concerning his conduct on other occasions, or his general conduct, which is a subject in nowise involved in the issue."

Attorney General v. Bowman, 2 B. & P. 532 Note (a) 

(1791), Thayer, 272.

Upon the trial of an information against the defendant for keeping false weights and for offering to corrupt an officer, the defendant's attorney called a witness to testify as to his character and as evidence that he could not be guilty of such a charge. Eyre, C. B., declared: "I cannot admit this evidence in a civil suit. The offence imputed by the information is not in the shape of a crime. It would be contrary to the true line of distinction to admit it, which is this: That in a direct prosecution for a crime such evidence is admissible; but where the prosecution is not directly for the crime, but for the penalty, as in this information, it is not. If evidence to character were admissible in such a case as this it would be necessary to try character in every charge of fraud upon the excise and Custom-House Laws.” 

McIntire v. Levering, 148 Mass. 546 (1889).

This was an action of tort for malicious prosecution. 

The charge had been larceny. Against the defendant's objection, the plaintiff was permitted to introduce evidence of her general reputation in the community for honesty of character. The Supreme Court held that the ruling of the lower court was correct. "In a suit of this kind," said the court, "where the prosecution complained of was for an offence implying moral turpitude, the plaintiff's general reputation at the time of the

RELEVANCY 

103 prosecution, if the defendant was where he would be likely to know it, is always involved in the issue, and the defendant may properly be permitted to show that it was bad. We see no good reason why the plaintiff should not be permitted, on the other hand, to show affirmatively that it was good. It is true that everyone is presumed to be of good character until the contrary appears, and this presumption ordinarily saves the necessity of proof. Indeed, in civil cases, as a general rule, evidence of reputation is not competent upon a question as to liability for a particular act. But whenever character is an issue, the rule is different. . . . To prove that the attack was originally made without probable cause, we think he should be permitted to show his good reputation known to the defendant when the prosecution was commenced." 

Kennon v. Gilmer, 131 U. S. 22 (1888), Thayer, 267.

The plaintiff, who had been a passenger in defendant's stage coach, brought suit for injuries received therein. It was necessary to prove the evil disposition of one of the leading horses attached to the coach. For this purpose he introduced evidence that prior to the injury to himself the horse had on various occasions been fractious and vicious and also that twenty months after the accident the horse, when being driven in a buggy had kicked and broken the pole and tried to run away. The defendant objected to this evidence but the 

Supreme Court ruled that it was admissible, saying: "Evidence of subsequent misbehavior of the horse might properly be admitted, in connection with evidence of his misbehavior at and before the time of the accident, as tending to prove a vicious disposition and fixed habit, and to support the plaintiff's allegation that the horse.

was not safe and well broken. The length of time afterwards to which such evidence may extend is largely within the discretion of the judge presiding at the trial." 

§ 48. Res Inter Alios Acta. 

Holcomb v. Hewson, 2 Camp. 391 (1810), Thayer, 232.

Action of assumpsit by a brewer against a tavern keeper on an agreement that he would take all his beer from the brewer or else pay an advanced rent to him as owner of the tavern. The defence was that the quality of beer furnished by the brewer was so bad that the defendant was losing his customers. After changing to a different brewer, his trade was proved to have picked up and the customers were highly satisfied with their beer. For the purpose of proving that the plaintiff's beer was of good quality the latter's lawyer offered to call other tavern keepers who would testify that the plaintiff's beer was supplied to them and gave excellent satisfaction. The court ordered a non-suit of plaintiff, saying of the evidence offered: "This is res inter alios acta. We cannot here inquire into the quality of different beer furnished to different persons. The plaintiff might deal well with one and not with the others. Let him call some of those who frequented the defendant's house and there drank the beer which he sent in; or let him give any other evidence of the quality of this beer; but I cannot admit witnesses to his general character and habits as a brewer."

Emerson v. Lowell Gas Light Co., 3 Allen (Mass.), 410 

(1862), Thayer, 233. 

Action of tort for injury to the health of plaintiff from inhaling gas which escaped from the defendant's pipes.

RELEVANCY 

105 It appeared that gas from the defendant's main pipe line escaped and passed under the frozen ground through sewers and drains into the cellar and house occupied by the plaintiff. It was several days after the defendants received notice before they discovered the place of the leak in the main pipe. The plaintiffs offered to show that a large number of houses in the neighborhood were similarly filled with gas and that wherever gas entered a house sickness occurred. The trial judge refused to receive the evidence and the jury returned a verdict for the defendants. The plaintiff alleged exceptions. The 

Supreme Court declared that the evidence offered was properly excluded. "Each separate and individual case,' said the court, "must stand upon, and be decided by, the evidence particularly applicable to it. The attending circumstances may be so different that the occurrence of sickness in one house would have no tendency to show the cause of illness in the occupants of another. If such evidence was inadmissible, the issues in a single case might be indefinitely multiplied; and this would tend only to confusion and to mislead the jury." The exceptions, however, were sustained because of erroneous instructions on other matters. 

Hunt v. Lowell Gas Light Co., 8 Allen (Mass.), 169 (1864), Thayer, 234.

The plaintiffs lived in New Hampshire. On the 4th of February, 1857, they came to the house of Aaron

Hunt in Lowell and remained there for nine days. It appeared that gas escaped into the house from a defect in the main pipe of the defendant company and the plaintiffs became ill and continued so for several weeks after their return to New Hampshire. Against the de-

fendant's objection the plaintiffs were allowed to prove that up to the time of the gas leak the family of Aaron Hunt had been in perfect health, but that soon after the gas leak occurred every member of the family became seriously ill, but no evidence of the particulars of this illness was admitted. The jury brought in verdicts for the plaintiffs. The defendant took exception to the admission of the evidence as to illness of the Aaron Hunt family, but the Supreme Court declared it admissible. He also objected that if such evidence were permitted at all the court should have permitted the particulars of the illness to be shown. Said the court: "But the sickness of these persons is a collateral fact, and is admissible merely for the purpose of showing the nature of the gas that came into the house to the influence of which all the inmates were subjected alike. . . . If it falls short of proving that the gas caused the sickness of the other persons, it amounts to nothing. 

But it might be very unreasonable to permit the case to branch out into several collateral issues on such a point. 

We see no reason to think that the evidence was unreasonably restricted." 

Temperance Hall Ass'n of Trenton v. Giles, 35 N. J. L. 260 (1869), 236.

In the spring of 1867 a course of lectures was being given at Temperance Hall. On the night of the accident to Mrs. Hall there was an unusually large crowd. 

The night was extremely dark. Mrs. Hall became separated from her escort and fell into an area about ten feet from the main steps leading to the hall. The area was guarded on two sides but there was no barrier to the stairs leading down to the cellar. The defendants,

RELEVANCY 

107 as a part of their case, proposed to prove that over ten thousand persons had passed and repassed the area every year since the hall was built, without accident. 

The Supreme Court declared such evidence rightly excluded as res inter alios acta, "unless their probative force, as presumptions, clearly appears, for the obvious reason that the other party cannot be prepared to meet such proof by counter proof, as to the truth of the facts offered. When such evidence is offered, everything depends on the nature of the case, to sustain which, or against which, the evidence is offered. An area, opening into a public footway, or so near thereto that a person, lawfully using the way, with ordinary caution, might, by accident, fall into it, is per se a nuisance; and only ceases to be such when proper means are adopted, either by enclosing it, or maintaining a light to warn persons of danger, to guard against the occurrence of such accidents.

The admission of this evidence would carry with it the right to cross examine as to the circumstances under which each individual of the multitude passed, and the degree of caution and circumspection used by each."

Amoskeag Mfg. Co. v. Head, 59 N. H. 332 (1879), Thayer, 240.

The plaintiff company filed a petition to have damage assessed for flowage of the defendant's lands. The defendant offered evidence of the sums paid to thirtytwo other landowners by the plaintiff for flowage claims. The court held that this evidence was inadmissible, "if unaccompanied by other evidence tending to show the damage done in those cases, and such a state of facts as would enable the jury to draw a fair

inference as to the value of the defendant's land from the value of other tracts. If such other evidence were offered, one question would be whether the thirty-two other cases should be opened for trial in this case. 

If, as a matter of law, it was not incompetent, it might be excluded on the ground that, as a matter of fact it had so slight or remote a bearing on this case that it would be unjust or unreasonable to prolong and complicate the trial by such an investigation of those cases as would be necessary for obtaining from them any useful information." 

District of Columbia v. Armes, 107 U. S. 519 (1882). 

Thayer, 241.

Action for damages for injuries received by the plaintiff's intestate from a fall caused by a defective sidewalk in the City of Washington. It appeared that the grade of the street had been lowered but only a part of the sidewalk had been lowered, the two portions being connected by three steps. Nothing was placed to warn foot-passengers of the danger. It was at this point, during the night time, that the fatal injuries occured. During the trial a member of the local police force, who saw the deceased fall and went to his assistance afterward, was asked if while on his beat he had ever seen other accidents at the same place. The witness answered that he had seen as many as five persons receive injuries there. Once he had sent a woman home in a cab after she had fallen on these steps. The defendant appealed on the ground of error in admitting this testimony, but the Supreme Court said that while it might have been objectionable as raising too many collateral issues, yet since the accidents

RELEVANCY 

109 referred to were not questioned by the defence the evidence was admissible. The frequency of accidents at a particular place," said the court, "would seem to be good evidence of its dangerous character—at least is some evidence to that effect. . . . Here the character of the place was one of the subjects of inquiry to which attention was called by the nature of the action and the pleadings, and the defendants should have been prepared to show its real character in face of any proof bearing on that subject." 

Aldrich v. Pelham, 1 Gray (Mass.), 510 (1854).

The plaintiff brought an action against the town of Pelham for injuries alleged to have been caused by insufficient width of highway together with an angle in the road or a turn at a bridge crossing a certain stream the bridge having no railing at one end where there was a high embankment. The defendant was permitted to prove that different persons passing over the same road in vehicles had passed each other at the same point without collision or accident and had room to spare on each side. The plaintiff objected to this evidence on the ground that it would raise collateral issues that he was not prepared to meet and also that it was incompetent. The trial court admitted the testi mony. The Supreme Court (Chief Justice Shaw delivered the opinion) declared: "If it were competent for the defendants to prove, that other persons, with carriages and vehicles of a certain width, had passed there without collision, it would be competent for the plaintiff to rebut it, by proving instances in which other persons, driving with equal care, and with carriages of equal width, had met with accidents by collision. Each of these cases would present a distinct

issue, with all its attendant circumstances, and involve the consequences contemplated in Collins v Dorchester, and be directly repugnant to the rule adopted and sanctioned in that case." The plaintiff's exceptions were sustained. 

Kidder v. Dunstable, 11 Gray (Mass.), 342 (1858).

The plaintiff sued the town for injuries sustained by reason of a collision in a portion of the highway badly drifted by snow, the road being narrow. The defendant sought to put the following question to a witness for the plaintiff: "Did you see other teams and sleighs pass and repass at the place of the accident in safety or without difficulty?" The court refused to permit the question to be answered. The Supreme Court declared that the defendant had no grounds for exception. 

Commonwealth v. Tuckerman, 10 Gray (Mass.), 173 (1857).

The defendant was on trial for embezzlement of $5,000 from his employer. It appeared that after the discovery of the crime the defendant made a written confession in which he recited a number of embezzlements committed by him over a period of years. The $5,000 item for which he was on trial was included therein. The defendant objected to the admission of this writing in evidence on the ground that it tended to prove the commission of other crimes than that for which he was on trial. The Supreme Court held that the entire confession was admissible. It also held that proof of other embezzlements might be introduced, as tending to establish the motive with which the defendant took the money in question; the acts being

RELEVANCY 

III so connected that the jury might properly infer that the same intent applied to all the crimes confessed. Jordan v. Osgood, 109 Mass. 457, 12 Am. Rep. 731 (1872). 

In an action of replevin for goods sold to the defendant it was alleged that he had obtained credit by means of false and fraudulent representations as to his pecuniary condition. The plaintiff was allowed to introduce evidence that the defndant had made similar statements to other dealers in order to induce them to deal with him. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendant alleged exceptions. The 

Supreme Court held that the trial court erred in admitting the testimony. Said the court, "The fact that a defendant has committed a similar but distinct crime or fraud is not competent to prove that he committed the particular crime or fraud with which he is charged. It has no tendency to prove the proposition to be established by the plaintiffs, but is equally consistent with an affirmative or negative decision of that proposition.

The admission of such evidence would introduce a multiplicity of collateral issues, calculated to withdraw the attention of the jury from the real issue in the case; and it would operate unjustly to the defendant, as it requires him to explain his transactions with others, without any notice or opportunity for preparation." 199 

Commonwealth v. Chute, 105 Mass. 551 (1870).

The defendant was on trial for arson. The crime was alleged to have been committed by means of a specially constructed box, lined with zinc and containing a candle and combustible material. For the

II

purpose of proving that the defendant was the maker of the box found at the scene of the arson several items of evidence were introduced despite the defendant's objection: one was an anonymous letter dated five days before the fire in question, addressed to the city marshal (declared by experts to be in the defendant's handwriting), in which it was stated that two recent fires had been set by the writer to get rid of old buildings and make work for carpenters. The method of setting the two fires referred to was described as by means of an incendiary box placed outside the buildings to be destroyed. Another item of evidence was a box found at one of the fires and alleged to be a replica of the partially destroyed box found at the fire. This second box was alleged to have been made of wood exactly similar to boards found in the defendant's shop. The same type of nails and other materials were used in the manufacture of the second box as were found in the defendant's shop. The Supreme Court declared that not only was the letter admissible but also proof concerning the probable maker of the second box since it had a tendency to show that the defendant had the requisite skill, tools, material and opportunity to have made the incendiary box. The defendant's conviction was affirmed. 

49. Exceptions to Rule. (See textbook.) 

§ 50. Facts Part of Same Transaction. 

Waters Patent Heater Co. v. Smith, 120 Mass. 444 (1876). 444 The plaintiff made a contract with the defendant, a manufacturer, to supply the latter with a boiler of certain capacity and of a certain make guaranteed to

RELEVANCY 

113 prevent the transmission of oil to the boilers. Under the terms of the agreement the defendant was to attach the boiler to the system within thirty days of receipt and after thirty days' trial to pay for the same if it fulfilled the conditions agreed upon. The defendant failed to attach the boiler within thirty days and refused to pay for the same. Suit was brought upon the contract. The defendant offered evidence that he had investigated the workings of other boilers of the same identical make sold by the plaintiff to other manufacturers and had found that they would not accomplish the desired result. For this reason he had refrained from attaching the boiler. The defendant offered witnesses in substantiation of his claim that the boilers were inadequate. The judge admitted this testimony, and ruled that it was competent for the defendents to show that the plaintiff's other heaters, if the jury were satisfied they were identical in construction, necessarily transmitted oil to and injured the boilers."

The jury found for the defendant and the plaintiff alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court, in overruling the exceptions declared: "If it had been ascertained that the plaintiff's heaters, exactly like the one which they had sold to the defendants, were incapable from their very construction of doing what was promised, and that they must of necessity fail in the exact particular which had been urged and relied upon as their chief merit, there was no occasion for the defendants to go through the ceremony of putting that particular one to the test of actual experiment. 

The defendants' answer raised an issue of fraud, and upon that issue the evidence admitted was competent and appropriate. 

Such a fraud, if proved, would avoid the contract of sale."

Maguire v. Fitchburg R. R., 146 Mass. 379, 15 N. E. 904 (1888). 

Mrs. Maguire brought suit against the railroad company in tort for negligence of the latter in causing the death of her husband. There were no witnesses of the accident. It appeared that the deceased had been in the employ of a grain elevator and that one of his duties was to assist in the unloading of grain, and to sweep into the hoppers any grain that lodged upon the narrow platform on either side of the cars being unloaded. At the time of the accident two cars of the defendant company were in the elevator building, their brakes set during the unloading operation. Maguire was last seen with a broom in his hand. The accident was caused by the defendant's servants, without warning, sending certain other cars into the elevator. 

They violently collided with the cars already in the elevator, forcing them. some distance from their original position. The platform on either side of the cars was only eight inches wide, between the cars and brick walls. Immediately after the collision Maguire was found dead lying across one of the tracks. One of the questions raised during the trial was whether evidence of the custom of sweeping the grain into the hoppers could be introduced to prove what Maguire was doing with the broom at the time of the accident. 

The Supreme Court held that the evidence of custom was admissible, saying: "The presence of the deceased on the track with his broom, as shown by the discovery of his body there, affords evidence that he was there for the purpose of sweeping. Whether he had actually begun or not is not important, if he was there for the purpose of commencing as soon as practicable. 

He had been dismissed elsewhere, and the sweeping the

RELEVANCY 

115 track was in the line of his duty. . . . The jury might well have believed that he was on the track in the performance of his duty, and in the exercise of all the care to be expected of a prudent man. If rightfully in the place of danger, it is not contended that he had any opportunity to escape."

Bradford v. Boylston Fire & Marine Ins. Co., 11 Pick. (Mass.) 

162 (1831). 

In this case an action was brought against the insurance underwriters to recover for alleged sea damage to bales of blankets bought of the manufacturers in Great Britain and imported into this country. The defence alleged that the damage was of a peculiar kind, and was not due to salt water, but arose in the manufacture or packing of the blankets. In substantiation of this claim they offered evidence that other bales of blankets manufactured and packed by the same manufacturers and imported in other vessels in the same year sustained a like damage, even though no question of contact with salt water could be raised. The Supreme Court held that the evidence in question was admissible. saying: "The object is not to impute a fraud to the manufacturer (for we do not see any motive he could have to destroy the blankets), but to prove in a suit between other parties, that the injury did not arise from sea damage. And the evidence, that the great number of bales of blankets which came that year, in six ships, from Wood's manufactory, had these distinguishing marks upon them, which are ascertained to have been such as would be occasioned by sulphuric acid, is, we think, admissible as tending to disprove the allegations of the plaintiffs, that the injury arose from the perils of the sea."

§ 51. Acts and Declarations of Conspirators. 

Commonwealth v. Scott, 123 Mass. 222, 25 Am. Rep. 81 (1877). 

Scott and Dudley were placed on trial for breaking and entering the Northampton National Bank with intent to commit larceny therein. It appeared from the evidence that the bank was robbed on the night of January 26-27, 1876, and that a large number of securities were stolen therefrom. During the trial the government offered Edison as a witness. Against the defendant's objection Edison was allowed to testify that in the year 1873 Scott, Dunlop, and one Connor entered into a conspiracy with him to rob banks; that it was a part of their plan and understanding that, in their travels through the country, they should obtain information of such banks as were insecure and thus easy to rob and that they should report to each other the results of their observation. Edison also testified that in the summer of 1875 in the course of his employment for a safe maker, he was sent to Northampton and while there secured information concerning the feasibility of robbing the National Bank in that city. This information he communicated to his fellow conspirators and furnished them the means of duplicating the keys of the vault lock. He also testified as to the details of the preparation for the robbery and how Scott and Dunlop went to Northampton but were frustrated in their first attempt because the dials of the vault got out of order and extra guards were placed while repairs were being made. After several months of inactivity the conspirators resumed work and so continued until the robbery. 

The defendants contended that the evidence of the prior conspiracy and attempt was not admissible in the

RELEVANCY 

117 present case. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence was admissible, saying: "The evidence. excepted to tends to show the beginning of the plot or scheme of this robbery in the summer of 1875. The fact of the general conspiracy, formed in 1873, was so connected with this scheme as to make it competent. 

It tended to explain and make intelligible the testimony of Edison as to the beginning of this scheme, as it accounts for the fact of his reporting to the defendants at Wilkesbarre, and furnishing them with the means of duplicating the keys of the vault lock. . . . But the evidence was competent as tending to prove the crime charged, and it is not rendered incompetent because it also tends to prove the commission of other crimes." 

Commonwealth v. Crowninshield, 10 Pick. (Mass.), 497 (1830). 

George Crowninshield was on trial for murder of White. Palmer testified that four days prior to the murder he was in the room of George and Richard Crowninshield. George declared that one Knapp had offered him $1,000 if he would kill White, and that he had told him that he must wait until the next day for his answer. George then asked the witness Palmer if he would commit the act, and promised him one-third of the reward. Richard suggested that if Palmer would lay in wait for White and overturn his carriage it would probably result in his death, owing to his advanced age. 

Palmer claimed to have refused to participate in the crime. The question arose whether Palmer's confession, he having turned State's evidence, was admissible against George Crowninshield. The Supreme Court held that the declarations of a conspirator were admissible against a fellow conspirator if the conspiracy had

first been proved by a competent witness. It declared that a conspiracy between George and Richard had been proved and that the evidence was admissible. 

§ 52. Explanatory Facts. 

Post v. City of Boston, 141 Mass. 189, 4 N. E. 815 (1886). 

The plaintiff brought suit for personal injuries occasioned by an alleged defect in a certain street in Boston. 

The accident occurred in the evening when the plaintiff and her sister were returning home. It was then raining heavily. They came to a flooded section of the street. The plaintiff saw in the darkness at the side of the crossing what appeared to be a pile of dirt or a plank in the water. She stepped upon it and went into a cesspool up to her armpits and received the injuries complained of. It appeared from the evidence that the cover of the cesspool was made of wood and not fas tened down. For the purpose of proving that the defect was known to exist the plaintiff offered evidence that on various prior occasions, during rainstorms, when the sewer was stopped the cover had risen and floated away from the hole. The Supreme Court held that the evidence thus offered was admissible "to show that the cover of the cesspool was liable to come off, and to float away, from the accumulation of water in such rainfalls as often occur, and such as the jury would be warranted in finding that reasonable care, diligence, and prudence should guard against." . . . The evidence that the cover had been off on other occasions within the year prior to the accident was competent, as bearing on the question whether the defect might have been remedied, or the injury prevented by reasonable care and diligence on the part of the city.

RELEVANCY 

119 

Kaplan v. Boston Gaslight Co., 177 Mass. 15, 58 N. E. 183 (1900). 

Action for injuries sustained from an explosion of gas in the subway at the corner of Tremont and Boylston streets in Boston. The gas was alleged to have leaked from pipes belonging to the defendant company into the subway excavation. Various witnesses were permitted to testify that for some time prior to the accident they had noticed strong odors of gas in the basements and other portions of buildings near the scene of the explosion, and that notice of these odors had been sent to the defendant company. The Supreme Court declared: "This evidence tended to show a defective condition of the gas pipes at the place of the accident, and it had some tendency to prove that this defective condition continued until finally it resulted in such a discharge of gas as to cause the accident. The testimony of each witness is not to be considered alone, but in connection with all other evidence. . . . If testimony introduced afterward makes it improbable that the earlier observations of the witnesses were from the same cause as the discharge which caused the accident, that fact does not render the evidence incompetent. 

The jury were instructed not to regard this evidence unless they were satisfied that the odors of gas to which the witness testified had their origin in the cavity where the explosion occurred. We are of opinion that this class of testimony was rightly admitted." 

§ 53. Collateral Facts Showing Intent or Malice. 

Regina v. Oddy, 2 Den. C. C. 264 (1851), Thayer, 257.

The defendant was indicted and found guilty of larceny by feloniously receiving stolen property. It was

proven that the cloth mentioned in the indictment had been stolen from a mill and about a week later had been found in the defendant's possession under circumstances indicating an attempt to conceal it. He claimed to have received it from a woman, but the latter denied it. The prosecution was permitted to introduce evidence that three months before this occurrence the defendant had been found with stolen cloth in his possession, the property of different manufacturers not parties to this prosecution. The appellate court held that the evidence was improperly received, saying: "It would have been evidence of the prisoner being a bad man, and likely to commit the offences there charged. But the English law does not permit the issue of criminal trials to depend on this species of evidence. . . . . The evidence merely went to show that the prisoner was in possession of other property that had been stolen in the previous December, and not that he had received such property knowing it to be stolen. Now the mere possession of stolen property is evidence, prima facie, not of receiving but of stealing; and to admit such evidence in the present case would be to allow a prosecutor, in order to make out that a prisoner had received property with a guilty knowledge, which had been stolen in March, to show that the prisoner had in the December previous stolen some other property.... Such evidence cannot be admissible." 

Queen v. Francis, L. R. 2 C. C. R. 128 (1874), Thayer, 262.

Francis was on trial for conspiracy to defraud and also for an attempt to obtain money on false pretences. 

He had attempted in several places to pawn a worthless ring as a diamond ring. His defence was that he did

RELEVANCY 

121 not know that the ring was false, being employed by Roberts who was likewise on trial. For the purpose of proving guilty knowledge, evidence was introduced. that he had previously offered other fake articles to pawnbrokers. It was proven that he had pawned a chain as gold which proved to be silver coated with gold, and that he had given a false address. The question arose whether the trial court erred in admitting this evidence. On appeal the court affirmed the conviction, saying: "It tends to show that he was pursuing a course of similar acts, and thereby it raises a presumption that he was not acting under a mistake.

Evidence that he, on the 6th of January, at Bedford obtained money from another pawnbroker on the pledge. of a chain which he represented to be gold, when it in fact was not gold, was surely matter from which the jury might infer that he was in the course of cheating pawnbrokers by knowingly passing off on them false articles under the pretence that they were genuine." 

Commonwealth v. Jackson, 132 (Mass.), 16 (1882).

The defendant was on trial for obtaining money and other property on false pretences. The false pretence alleged was that he had warranted a certain horse to be sound and kind. For the purpose of proving the defendant's fraudulent intent the government was permitted to offer evidence of three other sales made at intervals a few weeks prior to the present case, in which the defendant is alleged to have been guilty of the same offence. The parties to such sales were permitted to testify that the statements made by the defendant to them as to the horses sold were false. The Supreme Court declared that this evidence was improperly admitted.

"The transactions," said the court, "formed no part of a single scheme or plan, any more than the various robberies of a thief. They were entered upon as from time to time he might succeed in entrapping credulous or unwary persons. Even if they were transactions of the same general character, they differed in all their details, and the defendant was compelled to defend himself against three distinct charges in addition to the one for which alone he was indicted. Evidence of the commission of other crimes by a defendant may deeply prejudice him with the jury, while it does not legally bear upon his case." 

Commonwealth v. Hall, 4 Allen (Mass.), 305 (1862).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment uttering and passing counterfeit bank bills. Evidence was admitted that the defendant had been employed in the business of printing genuine bank bills. The Supreme Court held that this evidence was pertinent for the purpose of showing his knowledge in respect to bank bills. It also tended to show guilty knowledge that the bills he passed were counterfeit. 

Another item of evidence was objected to, viz: that upon the defendant's arrest he swallowed another counterfeit bill similar to the one concerned in the present case. This was held to be competent evidence, since it tended to prove guilty knowledge and guilty intent. 

Commonwealth v. Jenkins, 10 Gray (Mass.), 485 (1858).

The defendant was on trial for receiving stolen goods. 

Graham, one of the thieves, turned State's evidence.

While on the witness stand Graham was asked whether

RELEVANCY 

123 he had had previous interviews with the defendant concerning the matter. Despite the defendant's objection he was permitted to testify that about six weeks before the sale of the stolen goods the defendant had offered to buy any goods that he might steal and had told him that it was unsafe for him to take it to dealers lest the theft be detected. The Supreme Court held that this evidence was admissible "to prove the guilty knowledge of the defendant in receiving the stolen property." 

There was another question of evidence, however, on which the defendant's exceptions were sustained. 

In the cross-examination Graham was asked if in the police court he had not made a different and contradictory statement concerning the transactions in question. The attorney for the commonwealth for the purpose of corroborating Graham's story offered evidence that on an occasion other than the examination in the police court, but when not under oath, Graham had related substantially the same story as in the present trial. The Supreme Court held that such "corroboration is altogether too slight and remote. Indeed, if admitted and followed out to its legitimate result, it might properly lead to a protracted inquiry to ascertain which of the two statements had been made most frequently by the witness. . . . It is obvious that such a course of inquiry would furnish no means by which the credit due to the testimony of a witness could be satisfactorily ascertained." 

§ 54. Value of Land. 

Petition of Thompson, 127 N. Y. 463 (1891), Thayer, 245.

The Commissioner of Public Works of the city of 

New York was authorized by law to divert the waters

of the Bronx River from its regular channel. The claimant, Butler, was the owner of a large and valuable farm through which the river ran. The commissioners awarded him $7,270 damages. Evidence had been rejected of what the city had paid for water rights appurtenant to a neighboring parcel on the same river. In denying the appeal the court said: "The White waterpower was in actual use in the operation of a mill, while the water power of Mr. Butler had not been utilized in any degree whatever. True, as much water will be diverted from the Butler property as the White property, but it does not follow that the respective water powers are of equal value. The value of water power depends on its availability for use. And as a matter of common observation, at certain points along a stream the water power can more readily and cheaply be made available for industrial purposes than at others." 

Standish v. Washburn, 21 Pick. (Mass.), 237 (1838).

This was an action by the owner of a certain meadow along a stream against the defendants who were mill owners to recover damages for flowage to the meadow. 

He introduced evidence that there had been a material change in the quality and quantity of the produce of his land since the flowage began as compared with the former products of the same land. This was relied upon as furnishing ground for the jury to infer damage. The defendants sought to combat this evidence by the testimony of a land owner whose land was not flowed to the effect that a similar deterioration in the quality and quantity of the produce of his land had occurred during the period. It appeared, however, that the land in question was more than two miles distant

RELEVANCY 

125 on the same stream, and the judge ruled that unless the defendants could give evidence that the meadows were similar as to soil the testimony offered was inadmissible. Because they could not offer such proof the evidence was excluded and the jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision, saying: "Evidence that other meadows not similar to the plaintiff's were from natural causes rendered less productive, would clearly be inadmissible.

The defendant did not prove that the meadow of the witness was similar, and that fact is not to be presumed." 

MacNaughton v. Commonwealth, 220 Mass. 551 (1915).

The Legislature authorized and directed a commission to take, or acquire by purchase, gift or otherwise land situated in a certain mountain range for a State Reservation. The commission took from the plaintiff two parcels of land, one of 250 acres including a mountain peak and the other, 62 acres in extent, including a pond. The values of these tracts were more for their scenic and sentimental value than otherwise. For the purpose of proving that the land was not worth what the plaintiff claimed for it the defendant offered to prove what other land on the same mountain and locality was sold for. The Supreme Court declared that the trial court properly excluded the proffered evidence, saying: "We are of opinion that there was such dissimilarity between the Whitbeck property and the two parcels of the petitioner as to justify if it did not require the exclusion of the testimony.

Questions which arise as to the competency of evidence of this character must be left largely to the discretion of the presiding judge."
