56. Facts Admitted. (See textbook.) 

57. Admissions by Pleadings. 

Johnson v. Russell, 144 Mass. 409 (1887).

This was an action of contract upon an order for $600, dated March 13, 1882, drawn upon the defendant by Campbell in favor of the plaintiff, and payable out of the "last payment." The facts were that Campbell was

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 127 building a house for the defendant and was indebted to the plaintiff for materials used in the construction of the house. For the purpose of proving an acceptance of the above order the plaintiff offered to introduce the following evidence: Campbell brought suit against the defendant Russell, claiming the balance due. In his answer the defendant had set forth, among other orders, the order now being sued upon and contended that such orders should be deducted from Campbell's claim. The trial court excluded the answer and found that there were no sums due from Russell to Campbell. The Supreme Court sustained the plaintiff's exceptions, saying: "The evidence of the answer of the defendant in the former suit was competent, and, if it had been considered by the court, might have led to a different finding; and it would have been before the court but for the erroneous ruling excluding it."

Central Bridge Corporation v. City of Lowell, 15 Gray (Mass.) 

106 (1860).

In proceedings to assess damages for the taking of a bridge as a public way by the city of Lowell, the petitioner, as a part of the proof in his case, sought to introduce the answer signed by the mayor and city solicitor in another bill in equity between the same parties. 

The respondents argued that the mayor and city solicitor had no authority to bind the city by their signatures. The Supreme Court held that "the respondent's former deliberate declarations were competent and rightly admitted." 

City of Boston v. Richardson, 13 Allen (Mass.), 146 (1866).

The demandant brought a writ of entry to recover certain flats on which were wharves and docks. To

meet the claim of the tenant that he owned a certain dock the demandant sought to introduce the record of a previous action brought by the tenant against the demandant, in which he alleged that he owned the wharves on each side of the premises in question, but described the intervening dock as not belonging to him. This was held to be admissible against him to show that he did not own the dock which he now claimed. 

§ 58. Admissions in Open Court. 

(See textbook.) 

$ 59. Judicial Notice. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 60. Constitutions and Public Laws. 

Commonwealth v. Inhabitants of Springfield, 7 Mass. 9 (1810).

The inhabitants of Springfield were being prosecuted by the State for maintaining a public nuisance in permitting two miles of highway within the said town to continue in a ruinous and dangerous condition. The defendants demurred to the indictment and alleged as one of the causes of demurrer that it was not alleged that the town was within the county of Hampshire in which the trial was being held. The Supreme Court held that the court might take judicial notice of the fact that Springfield was within the said county. It explained the law thus: "Our county limits, and also the boundaries of our several towns, are prescribed by public statutes, of which we are bound judicially to take notice. When, from these limits or boundaries, it appears that every part of any town is in the same county, of that fact we can judicially take notice. Now, as

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 129 judges, we know from our several public statutes, that 

Springfield lies wholly in the county of Hampshire.

When, therefore, the indictment alleges that the inhabitants of the said town of Springfield, in the county of Hampshire, ought to repair, and that the defective road lies in Springfield aforesaid, we can take notice that the defective road lies in the county of Hampshire." 

Prince v. Crocker, 166 Mass. 347, 44 N. E. 446, 32 L. R.

A. 610 (1896). 

A bill in equity was filed by eighteen inhabitants and taxpayers of Boston to restrain the Transit Commissioners from proceeding with the construction of a projected subway and thus involving the city of Boston in a very heavy indebtedness. One of their grounds of complaint was that the statute under which the commissioners were assuming to act would deprive the city of many of its rights, with especial reference to control of its streets. The Supreme Court, in dismissing the bill, pointed out that under the terms of the statute in question the Transit Commission was not permitted to take any land or commence the construction of the proposed subway, "until this act shall be accepted by a majority of the voters of said city voting at some special election called by the mayor." There was no averment in the pleadings that such a vote had been taken, but the court took judicial notice of the fact that the special election had been duly held and that the legislation had been accepted by the citizens of Boston. 

Mahar v. Steuer, 170 Mass. 454, 49 N. E. 741 (1898).

This was an action of tort for personal injuries to a ten year old girl, through the alleged negligence of the

defendant. It appeared that the defendant was erecting a building on his premises. Under his direction a pile of heavy stones for the foundation had been so placed as to interfere with foot passage. The plaintiff who was on her way home, paused for a moment near the pile, while waiting for a team to pass. The top stone was shaken down by the team and this caused the injury. 

The defendant was found liable and appealed the case. 

One of his exceptions was that the court refused to rule that he had "a right to use so much of the highway as was permitted under the laws of the city of Boston," but instructed the jury that the right to use the street was limited by conditions of traffic in the locality. The local ordinance on this point was not put in evidence. The court over-ruled the exceptions, and declared as follows: "No city ordinance had been put in evidence, or brought to the court or jury, but the defendant cites one to us, and argues that the court at the trial should have taken judicial notice of it. But courts unless municipal courts, do not take judicial notice of municipal ordinances and by-laws." 

Haines v. Hanrahan, 105 Mass. 480 (1870).

The plaintiff, who resided in New Hampshire, brought suit against the defendant, who lived in Massachusetts, to recover the price of ale sold to him during 1867 and 1868. It was admitted that the ale was delivered in 

New Hampshire. The plaintiff requested the court to rule "that, if said ale contained a very small or infinitesimal quantity of spirit, the same was not spirituous liquor within the meaning of the laws of New Hampshire." The judge refused to give the requested ruling but instructed the jury, "that, in determining the ques-

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 131 tion whether the ale was intoxicating, they were to consider whether in the common use of the word it was an intoxicating liquor." The jury returned a verdict for the defendant and the plaintiff alleged exceptions but did not recite the terms of the statute in the bill of exceptions. In passing upon this point the Supreme Court declared: "If the law of New Hampshire on which the plaintiff relied, was contained in a written statute, the question of its construction was doubtless for the court. But no statute of another State can be considered by the court, which is not made a part of the bill of exceptions." 

Haven v. Foster, 9 Pick. (Mass.), 112 (1829).

Cragie of Cambridge, Mass., died leaving real estate both in Massachusetts and in New York State. In the settlement of the estate a controversy arose in the Massachusetts courts over the disposition of the New York property. In the opinion rendered by the Supreme Court the following language occurred: "The courts of this State are not presumed to know the laws of other States or foreign nations, nor can they take judicial cognizance of them, till they are legally proved before them. But when established by legal proof, they are to be construed by the same rules and to have the same effect upon all subjects coming within their operation, as the laws of this State. . . . The parties knew, in fact, that the intestate died seized of estate situated in the State of New York. They must be presumed to know that the distribution of that estate must be governed by the laws of New York. But are they bound, at their peril, to know what the provisions of these laws are? If judicial tribunals are not presumed to know,

why should private citizens? . . . It appears that the defendant received a part of the consideration for which the plaintiff's estate (in New York) was sold; that it was received by mistake and that this mistake was a matter of fact. He therefore has in his hands money which ex aequo et bono he is bound to repay, and there is no principle of law which interposes to prevent recovery of it out of his hands." 

§ 62. Treaties With Foreign Governments. 

(See textbook.) 

63. Public Boundaries. 

(See textbook.) 

64. Jurisdiction of Courts. 

(See textbook.) 

Hanley v. Donoghue, 116 U. S. 1 (1885), Thayer, 26.

Action was brought by Hanley and Welch against Donoghue in the circuit court for Baltimore County, Maryland, upon a judgment for $2,000 recovered by the plaintiffs against Donoghue in the Court of Common Pleas of Washington County, Penn. The action had originally named two defendants, one of whom had been eliminated because he had never been properly served with process.

The declaration alleged that under the laws of Pennsylvania this judgment against one of the two defendants was valid and binding, and also that the statutory period for appeal had passed and no appeal had been taken. The defendant filed a general demurrer

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 133 to all the courts and the demurrer was sustained. The case was carried to the Court of Appeals of Maryland where it was held that, under common law principles, if persons were sued jointly a judgment could not be rendered against one of them only. The case was taken on a writ of error to the U. S. Supreme Court on the ground that the Maryland Court had violated Sec. 1,

Art. IV of the U. S. Constitution, as to giving full faith and credit in each State to the judicial proceedings of every other State. The Supreme Court declared that the 

Maryland court had erred. It was bound by the Constitution to give full faith and credit to the lawful decisions of the courts of Pennsylvania. It was not bound to take judicial notice of the laws of the latter State since the laws of another State must be proved. But the declaration contained an allegation of fact as to such laws and the defendant admitted the truth of the allegation by filing a demurrer. 

§ 65. Public Officials. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 66. Laws of Nations. 

The Pawashick, 2 Lowell (U. S. Dist. Ct. for Mass.) 142 

(1872), Thayer, 31.

A libel in rem for wages was filed in the U. S. District Court against the British bark "Pawashick" by French, its captain, for unpaid wages. Both French and the claimant of the bark were residents of Prince Edward's Island. The representative of the owner claimed that since no evidence had been introduced as to the maritime law of England it must be presumed to be

like our own by which a master could not proceed in rem (against the ship rather than against his employer). 

The libel was dismissed, the court agreeing that “in admiralty, as in other courts, foreign law must be pleaded and proved as a fact." The various ways in which such proof might be made were discussed at length. The following extracts are from the decision: "Foreign written law may be received when it is found in a statute book, with proof that the book has been officially published by the government which made the law. ... The unwritten law of England may be proved in this court, not by experts only, but also by text writers of authority, and by the printed reports of adjudged cases; and that the written law may be proved by the printed copies, and be construed by the aid of text books as well as of experts." 

§ 67. Matters of Common Knowledge. 

Commonwealth v. Peckham, 2 Gray (Mass.), 514 (1854), Thayer, 17.

The defendant was on trial for selling intoxicating liquor. After conviction he appealed because the jury were instructed that they might infer that gin was intoxicating without any evidence other than that of its properties or qualities. The Supreme Court held that the instruction was correct, saying: "Jurors are not presumed to be ignorant of what everybody else knows. 

And they are allowed to act upon matters within their general knowledge, without any testimony on those matters. Now everybody who knows what gin is, knows not only that it is a liquor, but also that it is intoxicating.... Proof, therefore, that the defendant sold gin is proof that he sold intoxicating liquor."

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 135 

Brown v. Piper, 91 (U. S.), 37 (1875), Thayer, 17. 

Piper filed a bill to enjoin Brown and Seavey from infringing a patent for the preserving of fish in a close chamber "by means of a freezing mixture, having no contact with the atmosphere of the preserving chamber." The defendants denied the novelty of the alleged invention. The U. S. Circuit Court sustained the validity of the patent and issued a permanent injunction. 

Brown and Seavey appealed to the U. S. Supreme Court. 

The decree of the circuit court was reversed. Said the court: "The pleadings and proof in the case under consideration are silent as to the ice-cream freezer. But it is a thing in the common knowledge and use of the people throughout the country. Notice and proof were, therefore, unnecessary. . . . The court can take judicial notice of it, and give it the same effect as if it had been set up as a defence in the answer, and the proof was plenary.... We can see no substantial diversity between that apparatus and the alleged invention of the appellee. 

In the former, as in the apparatus of the appellee, 'the freezing mixture' has 'no contact with the atmosphere' of the chamber where the work is to be done. . . . Examined by the light of these considerations, we think this patent was void on its face." 

Munshower v. State, 55 Md. 11 (1880), Thayer, 21. The defendant was on trial for murder. It became material for the State to prove at what hour the moon rose on the night of Saturday, August 9, 1879. For the purpose of proving the hour of moonrise the State of fered Gruber's almanac for the year 1879, in which was the usual table giving the hours of rising and setting of the sun and moon. The prisoner's counsel objected to

its introduction on the ground that it was not the best evidence but a mere calculation made by someone long anterior to the happening of the event; that it was not evidence that the moon actually rose at the hour and minute predicted. The evidence was admitted and the defendant was found guilty. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence was rightly admitted. The court declared: "An almanac forecasts with exact certainty planetary movements. We govern our daily life by reference to the computations which they contain. No oral evidence of proof which we could gather as to the hours of the rising or setting of the sun or moon could be as accurate as that which we obtain from such a source. . . Courts would justly be the subject of ridicule if they should deliberately shut their eyes to the sources of information which the rest of the world relies upon, and demand evidence of a less certain and satisfactory nature." 

Taylor v. Barclay, 2 Sim. 213 (1828), Thayer, 23.

It was alleged in this case that the defendants, representing themselves to be the agents of the government of the Federal Republic of Central America, "which was a sovereign and independent State, recognized and treated as such by His Majesty the king of these realms," perpetuated a fraud upon plaintiff with reference to certain loans. The plaintiff was seeking discovery of certain facts, but the defendant demurred to his declaration because of the statement with reference to the independent sovereignty of the Federal Republic of Central America. The court said: "Without saying how far the plaintiff might have had the discovery which asks, provided he had represented his case otherwise, yet if he makes this fact the foundation of his case, that

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 137 this is an independent government, recognized by the government of this country, when it is not so, I must judicially take notice of what is the truth of the fact, notwithstanding the averment on the record, because nothing is taken to be true except that which is properly pleaded; and I am of the opinion that when you plead that which is historically false, and which the judges are bound to take notice of as being false, it cannot be said you have properly pleaded, merely because it is averred in plain terms. . I must allow the demurrer.” 

Kearney v. King, 2 B. & Ald. 301 (1819), Thayer, 36.

Action of Assumpsit against the defendant as acceptor of a bill of exchange for £542 11s. 8d. for value received by the defendant and drawn by the creditors Eggles & Power. The bill had been duly accepted by the defendant at Dublin. It appeared in evidence that the bill called for payment in Irish currency which was over eight per cent less in value than English currency. The suit was in the court of King's Bench. The defendant asked for a non-suit because of variance in that the declaration appeared to be for English money, whereas the bill itself proved to be an Irish bill. The court of 

King's Bench agreed to a non-suit declaring that while the averment in the declaration was that the bill was drawn in Dublin "it was not possible for the court to take judicial notice that there is only one Dublin in the world." One judge declared: "The court must see upon the face of the record that the bill was drawn in Ireland, and it cannot take notice judicially that a bill drawn at Dublin is drawn at Dublin in Ireland. The instrument, it is said, is set out in the same words and letters as the bill produced. But that is not enough; for it must be set out the same in substance and in legal operations

which is not done here. The bill in the decaration is in substance and legal operation a bill for so much English money; and the bill when produced, appears to be for the same amount, Irish currency. There is therefore a fatal variance between the declaration and the proof." 

Davis v. Leary, 177 Mass. 526, 59 N. E. 191 (1901).

This was an action of tort to recover for the alleged conversion of fifty tons of hay. It appeared that on 

August 20, 1896, the plaintiff sued the defendant and attached among other property of defendant, 31 head of cattle, four horses and 50 tons of hay. When the suit was terminated it was found that the defendant had in the meantime fed the hay to the livestock in question and that the hay was entirely gone. The plaintiff failed to levy upon the livestock within thirty days. after the recovery of judgment. The trial judge ruled that while there was a failure of attachment as to the livestock, yet the hay, by reason of its bulk, could not easily be removed and that the attachment thereof was valid since by the defendant's wrongful act it was destroyed before levy could be made thereon. The Supreme Court approved this ruling, and commented as follows: "That fifty tons of hay are so bulky that they cannot be readily removed from place to place is a matter of which the court properly could take notice, and could take into consideration in passing upon the validity of the attachment of the hay." 

Commonwealth v. Marzyuski, 149 Mass. 68, 21 N. E. 228 (1889). 

The defendant, the proprietor of a tobacco shop, was on trial for keeping open his place of business on Sun-

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 139 day for the purpose of selling cigars and tobacco. The statute under which he was being prosecuted permitted the sale of drugs and medicine. The defendant offere evidence that tobacco might be classed as a drug or medicine, but the court excluded the evidence. It also refused to submit the question to the jury, but instructed them instead that if the defendant kept open his shop on the Lord's day for sales of tobacco it was their duty to find him guilty. The Supreme Court affirmed the ruling in the following language: "Ordinarily, whether a substance or article comes within a given description, is a question of fact; but some facts are so obvious and familiar that the law takes notice of them and receives them into its own domain. . . . Cigars are manufactured articles familiar to everybody. The materials of which they are composed are carefully prepared and put into form, until they lose their original character as mere materials, and become articles of commerce, known by a new name and adapted to a particular use. We are of opinion that cigars sold by a tobacconist in the ordinary way are not drugs or medicines within the meaning of those words as used in the statute." 

Commonwealth v. King, 150 Mass. 221, 22 N. E. 905, 5 

L. R. A. 536 (1889).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for without license running a steamboat for the conveyance of passengers in and upon the waters of the Connecticut River, "such waters not being then and there within the maritime jurisdiction of the United States." The facts were that the defendant ran his steamboat on the river above the dam at Springfield. The judge refused to instruct the jury that there was no evidence that the

Connecticut River between Springfield and Hadley "was not within the maritime jurisdiction of the United States." The defendant was found guilty. The Supreme Court dismissed his exceptions and commented in part as follows: "We think that the Superior Court might take judicial notice that the Connecticut River above the dam at Holyoke does not, either by itself or by uniting with other waters, constitute a public highway over which commerce may be carried on with other States or with foreign countries, although, if the court had entertained any doubt on the subject, it might have required evidence to be produced. It is well known that the waters of the Connecticut River, at the place where it was alleged that the defendant's steamboat was employed, can be used by vessels only for the transportation of persons and property between different places in Massachusetts. They are, therefore, waters not within the maritime jurisdiction of the United States."

CHAPTER VIII. 

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN. 

§ 68. Presumptions. 

69. 70. 71. 72. 73. 74. 75. 76. 77. 78.

Conclusive and Rebuttable Presumptions.

68. Presumptions. 

Moore v. New York, etc., R. R. Co., 173 Mass. 335 (1899), Thayer, 51. 

A passenger brought suit to recover for damage to her luggage, suffered somewhere in the course of a passage from Charleston, Tenn., to Boston. The baggage was over six connecting railroads. It did not appear where the damage was done and no proof could be offered as to what carrier had been at fault. The court thus disposed of the case: "The so-called 'presumption' was started and justified as a true presumption of fact that goods shown to have been delivered in a good condition remain so until they are shown to be in a bad condition, which happens only on their delivery. But it was much fortified by the argument

that it was a rule of convenience, if not of necessity, like the rule requiring a party who relies upon a license to show it. As we in common with many other 

American courts, hold the first carrier not answerable for the whole transit, and not subject to an adverse presumption (Farmington Mercantile Co. v. Chicago, B. and Q. R. R., 166 Mass. 154), it is almost necessary to call on the last carrier to explain the loss if the owner of the goods is to have any remedy at all. To do so is not unjust, since whatever means of information there may be are much more at the carrier's command than at that of a private person. These considerations have led most of the American courts that have had to deal with the question to hold that the presumption exists." Judgment for plaintiff. 

§ 69. Conclusive and Rebuttable Presumptions. 

Bryant v. Foot, L. R. & Q. B. 161 (1867), Thayer, 46.

Suit was brought against the rector of Horton to determine whether the marriage fees being charged by him (10s. for himself and 3s. for his clerk) were legal.

He claimed the right to the fees charged on the ground of immemorial usage. This was contested by the plaintiff on the ground that the fees were too large to have been ancient fees. The doctrine of immemorial usage in 

England required evidence that it had continued for seven centuries. One of the judges favored giving the decision to the defendant on the ground that long continued enjoyment of a right raised a presumption of immemorial origin. Cockburn, C. J., and the majority of the court held for the plaintiff, saying: "It is clear that a fee on the celebration of a marriage in a parish church

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 143 can only be claimed by virtue of an immemorial custom in the parish, and the question is whether the evidence establishes the existence of custom in the parish of Horton for the payment of this fee from the time of legal memory. . . . I consider myself, therefore, fully at liberty to apply to the present case the principle long since established in cases of modus, that rankness, in an alleged customary payment, is fatal to its validity. 

The utmost length to which the cases have gone is, that to uphold continued possession or enjoyment, a previous possession or enjoyment for an indefinite period, or a last grant, or the like may be presumed, however improbable it may be that the presumption was well founded. It has nowhere been held that such a presumption is to be made where the thing to be presumed is impossible." 

§ 70. Conclusive Presumptions Classified. 

(See textbook.) $71. Presumption of Innocence. (See textbook.) 

§ 72. Presumption of Sanity. 

Sturdevant's Appeal, 71 Conn. 392 (1899), Thayer, 95.

Appeal from a decree of the Probate Court (affirmed by the Superior Court) allowing the will of Emeline Sturdevant. The Superior Court had charged the jury thus: "The burden of proof is in the first instance upon

she the proponents of this will, to show that the testatrix was of sound mind at the time of making this will. But the law, gentlemen, presumes every person to be of sound mind until the contrary is shown; and this presumption makes for the proponents of the will, and is of probative force in their favor, and must be considered by you along with the evidence offered by the proponents. This presumption must be cast into the scale with the evidence. If, gentlemen, using this presumption of sanity as of probative force, and, as suggested, you are satisfied by a fair preponderance of the evidence that Mrs. Sturdevant, the testatrix, had at the time of making this will the kind of capacity, understanding, and mental strength heretofore described to you, it will be your duty to find that she was of sound mind, and upon this issue render a verdict for the appellees. If, on the other hand, using this presumption of sanity as of probative force . . . you are not satisfied by a fair preponderance of the evidence that Mrs. Sturdevant had at the time of executing this will the kind of mental strength and capacity which I have been describing to you, it will be your duty to find that she was not of sound mind, and your verdict upon this issue should be for the appellants, who are contesting this will."

The Supreme Court declared the charge to the jury to have been correct. "The important thing for the jury to understand in the case at bar," said the court, "was that the proponents had something to rely on beside the positive evidence which they had introduced to show testamentary capacity; that this was to be considered together with that evidence; and that it consisted in a presumption, recognized in law as based on the general facts of life, which had probative force

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 145 enough to turn the scale, if otherwise, taking into account all that either party had put in evidence, the balance should seem to them to stand equal. The charge as given sufficiently answered this demand.” 

Sutton v. Sadler, 3 C. B. N. S. 87 (1857), Thayer, 97.

Action of ejectment brought by the heir at law of Walter Sutton against the devisee of the land under the will of Walter. In defendant's answer he admitted that the plaintiff was heir at law, but claimed the land for himself by reason of the will. The defendant claimed the right to begin the trial and was permitted to produce the will. He proved the execution as provided by law and produced witnesses to prove the testator's competency. The plaintiff then introduced evidence tending to prove that the testator had been incompetent since birth and also that, if ever capable of making a will, he had from habitual and incessant drunkenness rendered himself incapable. The learned Baron instructed the jury that the heir at law was entitled to recover unless a will was proved; but that when a will was produced, and the execution of it proved, the law presumed sanity, and therefore the burden of proof was shifted; and that the devisee must prevail, unless the heir at law established the incompetency of the testator, and that if the evidence left them in doubt they should find for the defendant. The jury found for the defendant. A new trial was granted because of misdirection. The court said that the burden of establishing the validity of the will remained on the propounder throughout and that the jury ought not to find a will valid unless they were satisfied from the evidence that it had been proved valid.

Clifford v. Taylor, 204 Mass. 358 (1910).

The will of Lucy French was contested on the ground that she was not of sound mind at the time of executing the same. In the Superior Court it was decided that the testatrix was insane at the time. The court, however, permitted an attorney to testify to certain facts that may have had a controlling influence with the jury, for which testimony the Supreme Court granted a new trial. They were as follows: The attorney was in the employ of Bixby, another attorney, at the time the testatrix applied for the drawing of the will. Bixby told the witness to go to the lady's family physician and find out if he thought the woman was mentally competent to make a will; if so to come back and Bixby would draft the will. The Supreme Court said that 

Bixby's opinion of her mental state as evidenced by his requiring a doctor's certificate was not competent evidence.

Another question raised at the trial was a request that the court instruct the jury that "Although the executor has the burden of proof upon him to satisfy the jury that the testatrix was of sound and disposing mind and memory at the time of the execution of the will, there is a presumption of sanity, and that presumption stands until rebutted." The Supreme Court thus commented upon the charge actually given by the trial court. "Some of the judge's language seems to be substantially in accordance with this proposition. Other language seems to imply that, the moment an issue is presented by a denial of sanity, the presumption becomes of no effect, and the case is to be tried upon the evidence introduced by the parties, as if there were no presumption. The true rule is that the presumption is enough to sustain the burden of proof, until evidence

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 147 is introduced which tends to control it. On the introduction of such evidence, the case is to be determined upon the whole evidence, including the presumption of sanity, and if the preponderance of the evidence is in favor of sanity, the burden of proof is sustained and the jury will find for the executor. If, upon the whole evidence, including this presumption, the scales are in even balance, the finding will be for the contestant, on the ground that the executor has failed to sustain the burden of proof." 

73. Presumed Intention of Consequences.

Fonseca v. Cunard S. S. Co., 153 Mass. 553 (1891). 

The plaintiff took passage on defendant's steamer from Liverpool to Boston. He had with him his trunk containing his clothing and other personal baggage reasonably necessary for a traveller on the ocean. All these things were ruined on the voyage by the negligence of the defendant. When he endeavored to secure redress, however, the plaintiff discovered that the contract ticket which he had received when he paid his passage in Liverpool contained the following: "Passenger's luggage is carried only upon the conditions set forth on the back hereof." Upon the back of the ticket was a proviso that the company should not be liable for loss or injury to baggage due to perils of the sea or negligence of the company's servants. The plaintiff did not read the conditions on the ticket and did not have his attention called to the same. He did not sign it. The court held that although in Massachusetts it would not be legal for a company to exempt itself from liability for negligence, yet it being an English contract the provision would stand. "One who ac-

cepts a contract," said the court, "and proceeds to avail himself of its provisions, is bound by the stipulations and conditions expressed in it, whether he reads them or not." 

Commonwealth v. Murphy, 165 Mass. 66 (1895).

Murphy and Enos were each charged with statutary rape of girls under the age of consent. Murphy's wrong doings with Emma Armitage began when the latter was only fourteen years of age. The transgressions of the defendant Enos with Miriam Stafford occurred when the girl was fifteen years old. He requested the court to rule that unless he knew or had good reason to believe that the said Miriam was under sixteen years of age he could not be convicted. The trial court refused to give the requested ruling. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision and commented as follows: "The defendants in the present case knew that they were violating the law. Their intended crime was fornication at the least. It is a familiar rule that, if one intentionally commits a crime, he is responsible criminally for the consequences of his act, if the offence proves to be different from what he intended." 

74. No Presumption of Survivorship. 

Newell v. Nichols, 75 N. Y. 78 (1878), Thayer, 66. 

In a shipwreck at sea, a father and his two children and his deceased wife's mother, perished. A question arose as to the construction of the wife's will. The heirs and next of kin of the children urged that even if the burden of proving survivorship be upon the appellants yet in this case there is no presumption that either child survived the other, "yet as the law will not

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 149 presume that they died at the same time, a presumption may be indulged that there was a survivor." The court held that there was no presumption that there was a survivor. "It is not claimed," said the court, "that there is any legal presumption that the children died at the same time. Indeed it may be conceded that it is unlikely that they ceased to breathe at precisely the same instant, and as a physical fact it may perhaps be inferred that they did not. But this does not come up to the standard of proof. The rule is that the law will indulge in no presumption on the subject. . . . In this respect the common law differs from the civil law. Under the latter certain rules prevail in respect to age, sex, and physical condition by which survivorship may be determined, but nothing can be more uncertain or unsatisfactory than this conjectural mode of arriving at a fact." The appeal by the heirs and next of kin of the children was dismissed. 

Coye v. Leach, 8 Metc. (Mass.), 371, 41 Am. Dec. 518 (1844). 

This case involved the distribution of the estate of 

Sylvanus Keith, who perished in a shipwreck. But the heirs of Caroline Coye, the daughter of Keith, who perished in the same disaster, claimed the property and argued that since Keith was about seventy years old and his daughter thirty-two there was a presumption that she survived him, thus inheriting his property. According to the facts Keith, his daughter and her husband, as well as the eight year old child of the couple perished in the same shipwreck. There was no evidence whatever on the question of survivorship. In disposing of the matter the Supreme Court declared: "By the civil law, Mrs. Coye, the daughter of Mr. Keith, would

be presumed to have survived her father, as the child, if above the age of puberty, is presumed to have survived the parent. .. But no such doctrine has any sanction in our system of jurisprudence, either as a principle of the common law, or enacted by legislative authority. 

No arbitrary presumption being autorized by law in such cases, arising from age or sex, the consequence is, that those who seek to enforce their rights, as heirs at law of Caroline E. Coye, must fail in establishing their right to a distributive share in the estate of Sylvanus Keith." 

§ 75. Presumption of Death. 

Nepean v. Doe d. Knight, 2 M. & W. 894 (1837), Thayer, 54. 

This was an action of ejectment begun in 1834 to recover possession of certain copyhold premises. The lessor of the plaintiff claimed, as grantee in reversion, of a copyhold estate on the death of Matthew Knight. 

The defendant came into possession as purchaser of the interest of George Knight, who held for the life of Matthew Knight. It appeared in evidence that the said 

Matthew went to America in 1806 or 1807 and was last heard from by a letter received in England in May, 1807. A question arose as to whether the lessor of plaintiff must give some evidence as to the precise time of Matthew's death, in order to show that the action was brought within twenty years of his death, or whether the presumption of his being alive continued to the last moment of the seven years since he was last heard from. The judge told the jury that the plaintiff must prove that he was actually alive within twenty years before the commencement of the action and that

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 151 he had not proved that fact by merely showing that the seven years since Matthew was last heard from had expired within twenty years. The appellate court held that "the presumption of law relates only to the fact of death, and that the time of death, whenever it is material, must be a subject of distinct proof." The verdict for the plaintiff was set aside and a venire de novo awarded. 

Bowditch v. Jordan, 131 Mass. 321 (1881).

This was a bill in equity to compel the defendants to receive a conveyance of certain land. Whether this could be granted depended upon whether a clear title could be conveyed. Fifty years before the present action one Dearborn, in a marriage settlement, created a life estate in the land in favor of his wife if she survived him and after her death to Hannah Freeman and Helen Freeman during their natural lives, the property then to return to the heirs of the said Dearborn. There was sufficient evidence of the death of Dearborn's widow and of Hannah Freeman. There was no evidence of the death of Helen Freeman except the fact that forty years before the present action Helen Freeman took passage on a vessel sailing out of Pernambuco and that neither vessel nor its passengers had been heard from since that date. The Supreme Court held that this evidence justified and required a presumption of her death. The deed was therefore perfectly valid. Specific performance was decreed accordingly. 

Boston & Roxbury Mill Corp. v. Tyndale, 218 Mass. 425 (1914.)

In the settlement of the affairs of the mill corporation it was found that certain dividends had accrued on

five shares of stock standing in the name of Thomas Lloyd from the year 1818. The public administrator of Lloyd's estate claimed the proceeds, but the corporation claimed that the right to recover the dividends had been barred by the statute of limitations. This latter fact turned upon the question of whether the right to dividends declared in 1825 became barred by the statute prior to Lloyd's death. The facts were that shortly after becoming the owner of the five shares Lloyd disappeared and was never again heard from. In disposing of the case the Supreme Court said: “A presumption of his death arose in 1825, seven years after he was last heard of, though this was not conclusive. . . . The first of the dividends was declared in February, 1825; and the ordinary period of limitation would not expire until February 14, 1831. Unless Floyd survived that date, the cause of action would not be barred until two years after the administrator of his estate had been appointed and had given bond for the discharge of his trust, that is not before July 17, 1925. . . . But it is not known what was the time of Lloyd's death. We know only that a presumption that he was dead arose in 1825. 

It was stipulated that the court might draw inferences from the agreed facts. We feel compelled to draw the inference that Lloyd died before February 14, 1831. ... The administrator of Lloyd's estate is entitled to receive the amount of these dividends." 

§ 76. Presumption of Legitimacy. 

Banbury Peerage Case, 1 Sim. & Stuart, 153 (1811), Thayer, 45.

On the question of legitimacy raised in this case the court declared as follows: "That the presumption of le-

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 153 gitimacy arising from the birth of a child during wedlock, the husband and wife not being proved to be impotent, and having access to each other during the period in which the child could be begotten and born in the course of nature, may be rebutted by circumstances inducing a contrary presumption." He gave as his reasons: "That the birth of a child from a woman united to a man by lawful wedlock is generally, by the law of England, prima facie evidence that such child is legitimate. That in every case in which there is prima facie evidence of any right existing in any person, the onus bandi is always upon the person or party calling such right in question. That such prima facie evidence of legitimacy may always be lawfully rebutted by satisfactory evidence that such access did not take place between the husband and the wife as, by the laws of nature, is necessary in order for the man to be, in fact, the father of the child." 

Phillips v. Allen, 2 Allen (Mass.), 453 (1861).

In a dispute over the disposal of an estate the question of legitimacy of a child born to Sarah, the wife of Alvin Allen became all important. The facts were that 

Alvin Allen first met the more or less charming Sarah on March 15, 1859, and was married to her twelve days later. Evidence was offered that Sarah had stated that 

Alvin had had no sexual relations with her prior to the marriage. She was apparently a young woman of very loose morals who had been intimate with other men. 

Alvin died July 10, 1859. The child was born November 26, 1859, and lived but a few hours. Sarah herself died January 1, 1861 and her father was suing as her heir at law. It was argued that since the child was

born almost exactly eight months after the marriage and seemed to be a full grown and natural baby, that it was not the child of Alvin, but of some of Sarah's former associates. The Supreme Court declared: "The legal presumption always is, that a child born in lawful wedlock is legitimate. But this presumption may be rebutted by proof that the husband had no access to the wife during the time when, by the course of nature, he could be father of the child. . . . The child was born in eight months after the marriage, and the fact that a child is born thus soon after the husband first had access to the wife does not prove beyond all reasonable doubt that the child was not his. . . . The bad reputation of a mother before and at the time of her marriage cannot affect the presumption of law that her husband, who has access to her, is the father of her child born after marriage, and within the time when, by the course of nature, it may also have been conceived after the marriage." 

Sullivan v. Kelly, 3 Allen (Mass.), 148 (1861).

Margaret Sullivan, a married woman, instituted bastardy proceedings against the defendant. Notwithstanding the fact that she was living with her husband at the time of her adultery with the defendant the plaintiff nevertheless convinced the jury that the defendant was the father of the child. The Supreme 

Court set the decision aside and declared that every child born in wedlock is presumed to be legitimate and that presumption "cannot be rebutted by proof of the wife's adultery while cohabiting with her husband."

The court also declared: "We do not understand that the present case presents any other phase than that of

THINGS THAT NEED NOT BE PROVEN 155 a child begotten while the husband and wife were living together and cohabiting as man and wife.” 

877. Presumption of Ownership. (See textbook.) $78. Presumption from Non-production of Evidence. 

Mikkelson v. Connolly, 229 Mass. 360 (1918).

In an action of tort for the alleged unlawful taking of household furniture, the defendant was alleged to have demanded a larger amount than was due on the furniture and because the plaintiffs refused to pay the amount claimed, he took the furniture away. One of the questions raised in the case was whether any presumption arose adverse to the defendant because of his failure to produce his ledger at the trial. The Supreme 

Court commented on the case as follows: "The failure of the defendant to produce his ledger containing his account with the plaintiffs was competent for the consideration of the jury. Whether the ledger could have been produced or was lost or destroyed was for the jury to determine. If it was within the power of the defendant to produce it and he failed to do so it could have been inferred that if produced it would not have supported the claim of the defendant." 

Little v. Mass. Northeastern St. Ry., 229 Mass. 244 (1918).

The plaintiff's intestate received injuries which caused his death through the alleged misconduct of a motorman in the employ of the defendant railway. It appeared that the intestate was driving in a covered

carriage with a second horse hitched behind the carriage at the time of the accident. The team was proceeding along a public highway beside which ran a street car line. An approaching trolley car, coming up from behind the horses, sounded its whistle and greatly frightened the horses. The deceased held out his hand to warn the motorman of the difficulty he was having with the frightened horses. Notwithstanding this warning, when the street car drew abreast of the team the motorman blew a shrill blast which caused the horses to become frenzied with fear. They overturned the carriage and inflicted the injuries which caused the death of intestate. At the trial the motorman was not called as a witness by the defendant, although he was in the courthouse at the time. The question arose whether any presumption adverse to the defendant because of this failure to call the motorman might be considered by the jury. The defendant requested the judge to rule that no such inference was justifiable. The judge refused to give the ruling requested. The Supreme Court declared that the judge rightly refused to give the instruction requested, and approved his action in instructing the jury that they were justified in drawing any inference from the circumstance that seemed to them proper.
