§ 79. The Best Evidence Rule. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 80. When Secondary Evidence is Permitted. 

Rex v. Watson, 2 Starkie 116 (1817), Thayer, 797.

In the trial upon an indictment for treason it appeared that a person by the name of Castle took a manuscript to a printer of a notice of a treasonable meeting. It was proved that a few days in advance of the meeting the defendant took away twenty-five copies of the placard and that sundry similar placards were

posted about the city. At the trial the printer produced one of the placards that had remained in his possession. The defendant objected on the ground that without notice to produce the original manuscript it was not proper to offer evidence of the contents of the evidence. The court declared that this contention was unsound. "Every one of those worked off are originals," said the court, "in the nature of duplicate originals; and it is clear that one duplicate may be given in evidence, without notice to produce the other. If the placard were offered in evidence, in order to show the contents of the original manuscript, there would be great weight in the objection; but when they are printed they all become originals; the manuscript is discharged; and since it appears that they are from the same press, they must all be the same. ... This, then, proves that 

Watson knew the contents of the placard posted in the streets relating to that meeting, and if he had notice of the contents of the placard it is evidence against him.” 

Regina v. Farr, 4 F. & F. 336 (1864), Thayer, 794.

Farr and Toiler were indicted for burglary. One of the articles stolen was a gold ring with an inscription inside of it-made on the occasion of the loss of a sister, and in memory of her. The attorney for the prosecution sought to ask the prosecuting witness what the inscription was. This was ruled to be inadmissible because the defendant had not been notified to produce the ring. The witness was permitted, however, to state that there was an inscription on the ring and to describe the ring generally. A companion ring made on the same occasion for another member of the family was produced and shown to a witness who had seen a ring in

the possession of Toiler. She testified that the ring seen by her was exactly like the one produced in court. 

The prisoners were convicted. 

Rex v. Hunt, 3 B. & Ald. 566 (1820), Thayer, 730.

The defendants were on trial on an indictment for criminal conspiracy, unlawfully taking part in a seditious meeting, and riot. At the trial it appeared that there were various flags and banners, containing inscriptions and devices, of a seditious and inflammatory tendency, and that these were seized by the police officers on the dispersion of the mob; these descriptions and devices were described by the several witnesses from memory. It was objected, by the defendants, that the flags or banners ought to have been produced, or that, in order to entitle the prosecution to give secondary evidence, the defendant ought to have had notice to produce the originals. The court of appeal said: “With respect to the last point, the reception of the evidence as to the inscriptions on the flags or banners, I think it was not necessary either to produce the flags or to give notice to the defendants to produce them. The cases requiring the production of a writing itself will be found to apply to writings of a very different character. There is no authority to show that in a criminal case ensigns, banners, or other things exhibited to public view, and of which the effect depends upon such public exhibition, must be produced or accounted for on the part either of the prosecutor or of the defendants. And in many instances the proof of such matters from eye witnesses, speaking to what they saw on the occasion has been received and its competency was never, to my knowledge, called in question until the present time.

Inscriptions used on such occasions are the public expression of the sentiments of those who hear and adopt them, and have rather the character of speeches than of writings. . . . And if such parol proof may be received generally, the proof at this trial was properly received. 

Commonwealth v. Morrell, 99 Mass. 542 (1868), Thayer, 731.

The defendants were on trial upon an indictment for robbery. An officer testified that he and another officer arrested the defendants at Chicago, took possession of their baggage, and detached the tags from their valises for the purpose of preserving them as evidence. The witness was proceeding to state what was written on the tags as detached by him, when the defendants' counsel objected, claiming that the tags must be produced or shown to be lost before the writing thereon could be given by the witness. The witness thereupon gave evidence of an unsuccessful search for the tags. The Supreme Court over-ruled the defendants' exceptions to the admission of the testimony, saying: "The general rule is most frequently applied to writings, where proof is offered of their contents. The writing itself must be produced. But there are many exceptions as to writings. An inscription on a banner or flag carried about by the leaders of a riot may be proved orally. In the present case, the tag referred to was not a document, but an object to be identified. The words written upon it served to identify it, and the court are of opinion that oral evidence was admissible for this purpose, and that it was not necessary to produce the tag. 

An inspection of the tag with the written direction upon it might have been more satisfactory to the jury • ·

than an oral description of it, and therefore might be regarded as stronger evidence; but the strength of evidence and the inadmissibility of evidence are different matters. But even if it ought to have been produced in the absence of evidence of its loss, the proof of loss was adduced to the judge, and his decision as to the credibility of the evidence of loss was conclusive, and not subject to exception." 

§ 81. When Original is a Public Document. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 82. When Original is Outside Jurisdiction. 

(See textbook.) 

83. When Original has been Destroyed. 

Clark v. Wright, 3 Pick. (Mass.), 67 (1825).

William Cloud, in 1820, made a codicil to his will by substituting as devisees Susanna Clark and her children in place of Ann Clough, who died before the making of the will. The will itself was dated May 1, 1811. When it was offered for probate the codicil was missing, and evidence was introduced that the will and codicil had been taken from the testator without his consent and the codicil torn off and destroyed. In the probate court the will was allowed. The great Daniel 

Webster was counsel for the petitioner. He insisted that the codicil might be proved by a copy which he offered. The Supreme Court declared that the codicil should be allowed.

Jones v. Fales, 5 Mass. 101 (1809).

In a second trial of a suit to collect upon certain promissory notes the plaintiff was unable to offer the original notes in evidence but substituted copies thereof. It appeared that the original notes had been offered as exhibits in the first trial, that the defendant had made no objection at the time to their authenticity and that the originals had been lost or destroyed by employees of the court. The clerk of courts testified that he had made diligent search for the missing papers. 

One of the jurors from the first trial testified that he believed the copies to correspond to the four notes that had been presented to the jury in the former trial. The endorser of two of the notes also testified that they were copies of the original. The Supreme Court held that the evidence was properly admitted. The accuracy of the evidence was later confirmed by the finding of the original notes by the clerk of the court. 

$ 84. Destruction by Party Offering Evidence. 

Gage v. Campbell, 131 Mass. 566 (1881), Thayer, 817.

The defendant failed to produce certain receipts demanded as evidence by the plaintiff and gave as an explanation in court that before he ever heard of this suit he had placed the receipts for safe keeping with a third party and had been unable to get them back. The plaintiff was then allowed by court to offer oral and secondary evidence of the contents of the said receipts. Afterwards the defendant offered to show that the said receipts read for interest upon the purchase money and not for rent, as the plaintiff had attempted to show. 

Upon inquiry as to the alleged efforts of the defendant

to recover the original receipts, the court became convinced that the defendant had wilfully suppressed the originals. The defendant's evidence was accordingly rejected. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision of the trial court, saying: "A party who has suppressed a written document, and refused to produce it upon notice, and so compelled the adverse party to resort to secondary evidence thereof, is not afterward entitled to offer proof of its contents. Jannes v. Bennett, 5 Allen 

169; Stone v. Sanborn, 104 Mass. 319, 325. The evidence offered by the defendant, and excluded, appears by the bill of exceptions to have been evidence of the contents of the receipts, not evidence to impeach the credibility of the testimony of the plaintiff's witnesses. 

This exception must therefore be over-ruled." 

Steele v. Lord, 70 N. Y. 280 (1877), Thayer, 818.

This action was brought to recover an alleged balance of an account arising from advances made by the plaintiffs to the defendant for croquet sets agreed to be manufactured by the defendant for the plaintiffs. These advances were made principally by means of drafts drawn by the defendant upon the plaintiffs, accepted by them and paid by their banks. There were one hundred and twelve of these drafts involved in the transaction. Thirteen drafts, amounting to $1,895.58, were missing. The plaintiffs explained that it had been their custom to destroy all bank vouchers as waste paper after ascertaining that the bank balance was correct. 

In this way the missing drafts had been destroyed. 

The defendant contended that the plaintiff had no legal right to prove the contents of the missing drafts. The 

Supreme Court declared: "The truth and good faith of

this explanation were questions of fact for the referee, which it is not the purpose of this court to review. 

The drafts in question in this action were not the contracts upon which, in case of dispute, the action should be brought; they had all been paid, and the action was for a balance of account, and these paid drafts were useful only as vouchers to prove the items of the account in case it should be disputed. The evidence shows, that at the time of the destruction of these paid drafts, no such dispute had arisen or was anticipated. 

If the plaintiffs had supposed that the defendant would deny the payments, it would of course have been prudent to preserve the most authentic proof of them. But the omission to preserve this evidence, when explained as in the present case, should not absolve the debtor from his obligation to repay." .. • 

Pack v. Valentine, 94 N. Y. 569 (1884), Thayer, 580.

The plaintiff, Peck, employed the defendant to act as his agent to conduct his lumber yard. This suit was brought to recover for moneys alleged to have been collected by the defendant and converted to his own use. 

The evidence relied upon to prove that the defendant had not entered all sales in his cash book was as follows: Liggett, who was also employed at the lumber yard, kept a record of sales made by the defendant for eighteen days, writing the same on a loose piece of paper. The defendant was probably unaware of the espionage. The paper was turned over to the plaintiff who copied the entries in a memorandum book and afterward lost the original. Leggett was produced as a witness and swore that the information given by him to plaintiff was true. The plaintiff swore that he

copied them correctly. Against defendant's objections they were received in evidence. A verdict was found for the plaintiff. The Supreme Court declared: "We think that the entries were not competent evidence. 

The original memorandum, if it had been produced, could have been used by Leggett to refresh his recollection; or if he had forgotten the facts stated and could not on seeing the memorandum recall them, yet if he had been able to state that it was a true statement of the transactions known to him at the time, it could have been read in evidence in connection with and as auxiliary to his testimony. . . . In this case the memorandum was not produced, and Leggett was not sworn as to its contents, for the reason doubtless that he could not remember what it contained." 

85. When Original Has Been Lost. 

Keeling v. Ball, Peake Add. Cas. 88 (1796), Thayer, 747.

In an action to recover upon a bond for £200 it appeared that the bond was lost by accident. The plaintiff called a witness who testified that the plaintiff had delivered to him the bond in question, and that he took the bond to the maker Ball, a brother of defendant to whom the latter was heir at law, and demanded payment. Ball promised to pay. The bond was printed in common form and there were subscribing witnesses.

The bond was later entrusted to Carter, a lawyer, for the purpose of commencing action thereon. While in 

Carter's office it was lost. Carter testified that he did not remember the names of the attesting witnesses and apparently no one else could give the necessary information. It was argued by the defendant that the bond could not be proved except by the testimony of the

subscribing witnesses. 

Lord Kenyon said in deciding the case that, "had it appeared who were the subscribing witnesses, the plaintiff must certainly have called them; but that it was the business of courts of justice to apply the general principles of the law to new cases as they arise. This was a new case, for it did not appear that the plaintiff could by any possibility know who the subscribing witnesses were. . The general rule of law is, that the best evidence must be produced which the nature of the case will admit of, and no better evidence could have been procured in the present case than that which the plaintiff has given." 

§ 86. When Instrument is in Possession of Adverse 

Party. 

Doe d. Gilbert v. Ross, 7 M. & W. 102 (1840), Thayer, 812.

In an action of ejectment the issue depended upon the question of whether Miller had acquired a fee simple in certain lands by exercising a power of appointment contained in his father's will. This was claimed to have occurred in a marriage settlement. The document in question was in the possession of the defendant's attorney. He was summoned to produce it but refused to do so, on the ground that he had a lien upon it for professional services. The plaintiff sought to introduce secondary evidence of the contents of the deed. Against the defendant's objection he was permitted to do so and offered in evidence a copy of the deed. This being unstamped, was rejected. It was then proposed to read a shorthand writer's notes of the proceedings of the trial in a former action, when the deed of settlement had been produced and proved. This evidence was admitted and a verdict was found for the plaintiff.

The decision was affirmed in the Court of Exchequer, the court saying: "The law makes no distinction between one class of secondary evidence and another. In cases where the contents of public records and documents are to be proved, examined copies are allowed as primary evidence; but this is upon public grounds; for in these cases, the law, for public convenience, gives credit to the sworn testimony of any witness who examines the entry and produces the copy." 

Goodrich v. Weston, 102 Mass. 362 (1869), Thayer, 814.

In an action of contract for work and materials the plaintiffs were notified by the defendant to produce all letters received by them from the defendant. They produced certain letters, testifying that these were all they could find. The defendant then offered evidence of several other letters which he testified that he had at various times deposited in the post-office, properly addressed to plaintiffs and postage prepaid. He also testified that the letters were first copied in his letter book by a machine press and that his wife afterward copied from the letter book the copies produced in court. He testified that he had compared the copies. prepared by his wife with the letter-book copies and found them correct. The plaintiffs objected to the copies on the ground that they were not copied from the original. The defendant offered to produce the letter book in court but was not required to do so. The 

Supreme Court, in affirming the decision declared in part as follows: "The jury will judge of its weight, and may give due consideration to the fact that a less satisfactory form of proof is offered while a more satisfactory one exists and is withheld, or not produced when

it might have been readily obtained. But there are no degrees of legal distinction in this case of evidence. . . . 

In this case the letter-book, if produced, would have been only secondary evidence. We are satisfied that the copies, admitted by the court below, were sufficiently verified to justify their admission as competent evidence of the contents of the original letters." 

Cameron v. Peck, 37 Conn. 555 (1871), Thayer, 815.

In an action of assumpsit for goods sold it was alleged by the plaintiff that on March 13, 1868, he had written a letter to the defendant in which he had enclosed a statement of the account. This was in accordance with a request of defendant that he send the bill by mail. The contents of the letter became a subject of controversy. Because the defendant could not produce the original letter the plaintiff offered a copy of the letter as transcribed from the impression of the original in the plaintiff's letter book. To its admissibility the defendant objected because it was a copy of a copy and not of the original. The trial court admitted the evidence and the Supreme Court affirmed the decision. "The ground of objection," said the court, "supposes the original to be lost, or out of reach of the plaintiff. 

If it were not so, the objection to any copy would be insuperable. Now the rule that a copy of a copy is not evidence, properly applies to cases where the original is still in existence and capable of being compared with it; or where it is a copy of a copy of a record, the record being still in existence, and being by law as high evidence as the original. The reason for the rule is the same in both cases, the copy offered is two removes from the original. But it is quite a different question

where the original is lost and the record is not deemed in law as high as the original. . . . The witness in this case testified that he knew the paper offered to be an exact copy of the original letter. That we think made it admissible; the proper foundation for the admission of secondary evidence having been previously laid. The facts elicited on cross-examination, at the most, go no farther than to show that this was a second copy, verified as a true copy of the original. It was properly admitted." ... 

Cooke v. Tanswell, 8 Taunt. 450 (1818), Thayer, 750.

In an action on an indenture of apprenticeship there was an averment in the declaration that the indenture was in the possession of the defendant and therefore could not be produced by the plaintiff. Possession by the defendant was proved at the trial and also that he had been notified to produce it. The plaintiff offered in evidence what he supposed was a copy of the document but when it proved otherwise he abandoned it and gave parol evidence of the contents of the original without calling the subscribing witness who was in court. The trial judge ruled that the evidence was sufficient without calling the subscribing witness. The case was appealed but the higher court sustained the ruling of the court below, saying: "I do not think the knowledge of the name of the subscribing witness makes any difference in the case. I take the question to be, whether when one party calls for a deed of the other, who does not produce it, and the party calling for the deed is consequently driven to give parol evidence of its contents, it is necessary for him to call the subscribing witness. Here, the deed was in the hands of the

defendant; if he wished to throw on the plaintiff the burden of calling the subscribing witness, he might have produced the deed. It was alleged on the record, that the deed was in the defendant's hands, that allegation was admitted, and the defendant being called upon to produce it, and refusing to do so, it was not necessary that the plaintiff should call the subscribing witness the deed before he gave evidence of the contents." 

Commonwealth v. Emery, 2 Gray (Mass.), 80 (1854), Thayer, 788.

On a trial of the defendant for being a common seller of intoxicating liquor, it became necessary to prove that the house where the sales occurred was owned by the defendant. For this purpose the district attorney offered a registry copy of a deed to the premises. The defendant objected to the admission of the copy because he had received no notice to produce the original. 

The trial judge overruled the objection. The defendant alleged exceptions and the supreme court granted a new trial. Chief Justice Shaw declared in part as follows: "Upon consideration, the court are of opinion that this copy of a deed ought not to have been admitted, without notice to the defendant to produce the original. The rule, as to the use of deeds as evidence, in this Commonwealth, is founded partly on the rules of the common law, but modified to some extent, by the registry system established here by statute. The theory is this that an original deed is in its nature more authentic and better evidence than any copy can be; that a copy is in its nature secondary; and therefore in all cases original deeds should be required, if they can

be had. . . . The law assumes that the grantee is the keeper of deeds made directly to himself; when then he has occasion to prove any fact by such deed, he cannot use a copy, because it would be offering inferior evidence, when in theory of law the superior is in his own possession or power. It is only on proof of the loss of the original, in such case, that any secondary evidence can be received." 

Colling v. Treweek, 6 B. & C. 394 (1827), Thayer, 795.

In an action to recover the amount of an attorney's bill, the plaintiff was non-suited on the ground that a copy of the bill could not be received in evidence because the plaintiff had not given the defendant notice to produce the original bill delivered to him. The Supreme Court declared that this decision was incorrect.

Said the court: "We think that notice to produce the bill delivered to the defendant was not necessary in this case, because the bill delivered to him was in the nature of a notice. There are three descriptions or cases where notice to produce an instrument is unnecessary; first, where the instrument produced and that to be proved are duplicate originals; secondly, where the instrument to be proved is a notice; as a notice to quit, or a notice of dishonor of a bill of exchange. . . . The third case is where, from the nature of the suit, the opposite party must know that he is charged with possession of the instrument. . . From the very nature of the suit, the defendant must have known that the plaintiff sought to recover the amount of that bill which had been delivered to him, and which in the ordinary course of things, must be in his possession."

Zipp v. Colchester Rubber Co., 80 N. W. Rep. 367 (1899), Thayer, 797.

In an action for breach of contract contained in written orders and letters passing between the parties, the plaintiff introduced letters written by the defendant to him and attempted to testify to the contents of letters which he had written to the defendant in reply. The defendant objected that if such letters existed they were at the home office and no timely notice had been given to produce the originals. The trial court overruled the objection. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision, saying: "It may be true that the notice above quoted cannot be regarded as a notice to produce, but, in view of the issues raised by the pleadings, it is not error to permit secondary evidence of the contents of the letters sent by plaintiff to defendant, because no notice to produce was necessary. Notice to produce is not necessary in case of a writing directly involved in the cause of action or defence, so that the nature of the action or the contents of the pleadings in effect give notice that it will be required.' . . . The pleadings disclosed that plaintiff's cause of action, if any existed, was founded upon the letters written by the respective parties, so the nature of the action and the contents of the pleadings in effect gave defendant notice that the letters received from plaintiff by defendant would be required." 

§ 87. The Parol Evidence Rule. 

Brady v. Nally, 151 N. Y. 258 (1896), Thayer, 828.

In a controversy over a contract performed in part only, the defendant presented a counter-claim based upon an alleged oral agreement made before the execu-

tion of the formal written document containing the contract upon which the plaintiff was suing. This oral agreement was that Brady would pay the defendant according to the work done and as payments were received from the school board. When this evidence was received it had not appeared that there was a written contract. The instrument itself when produced appeared to be a complete contract. The plaintiff's lawyer not only failed to object to the reception of the oral evidence but also failed to move to have it stricken out when he presented the written instrument. The Supreme 

Court declared that because of these omissions to act. the plaintiff was debarred from setting out of the objections in his appeal. The court declared: "By failing to take the position during the trial that it was not legal evidence, and, hence, that it should be disregarded, he impliedly consented that it should be considered and acted upon by the referee, who, indeed, had no right to refuse consideration to anything that the parties had spread before him as evidence to guide him in passing upon their rights." The court declared that the parties to a controversy had a right to waive the safeguards of evidence and would be deemed to have done so if they failed seasonably to object to evidence offered by the adverse party. 

§ 88. Classes of Cases Affected by Rule. 

Pynn v. Campbell, 6 E. & B. 370 (1856), Thayer, 830.

The plaintiff alleged that the defendants agreed to purchase a three-eighths share in a certain invention of his own and produced a written document to this effect, signed by the defendants. The defence was that the

instrument was signed under the following circumstances: The defendants had agreed to make the purchase provided the plaintiff made a satisfactory demonstration of the machine to two engineers, Ferguson and Abernethie. The plaintiff failed to keep his appointment and did not arrive at the meeting place until after the engineers had gone. Later Ferguson was found and expressed his satisfaction with the demonstration. 

It was then proposed that since the parties were all present and that it might be difficult to meet again that a tentative agreement be drawn up and signed, the same to become effective if Abernethie approved the invention but to be null and void if he was not satisfied. 

Abernethie later advised against purchasing the invention, not being satisfied with it. The admissibility of this oral evidence was the chief question in dispute. 

The Court of Queen's Bench declared that the evidence was admissible to show that there had never been any agreement at all Said the court: "The production of a paper purporting to be an agreement by a party, with his signature attached, affords a strong presumption that it is his written agreement; and, if in fact he did sign the paper animo contrahendi, the terms contained in it are conclusive, and cannot be varied by parol evidence; but in the present case the defence begins one step earlier; the parties met and expressly stated to each other that, though for convenience they would then sign the memorandum of the terms, yet they were not to sign it as an agreement until Abernethie was consulted. I grant the risk that such a defence may be set up without ground; and I agree that a jury should therefore always look on such a defence with suspicion; but, if it be proved that the paper was signed with the express intention that it should not be an agreement,

. 

the other party cannot fix it as an agreement upon those so signing." 

Gilman v. Gross, 97 Wis. 224 (1897), Thayer, 833.

The defendant, at the solicitation of the president of a corporation, signed a written subscription for shares of its capital stock to the amount of $1000, with the express understanding and agreement that such subscription was not to be binding on the defendant until nor unless ten stock subscriptions of $1000 each had been obtained. The subscription was not delivered to the corporation, but was left in the custody of the president, the party soliciting subscriptions, for the purpose of obtaining other subscriptions. Ten other subscriptions were not obtained, yet the defendant was charged, upon the books of the corporation, with $1000 for stock subscription and an entry was made of stock delivered to him. But no stock was in fact delivered and the defendant had no notice of the entries. In deciding for the defendant the Supreme Court said: "It is said that this evidence was inadmissible and incompetent for the purpose, because its effect was to vary the terms of the writing. This position is untenable, both in reason and on authority. In the proof of a contract are two elements-whether an agreement was made, and its terms. 

Whether the parties consummated an agreement is, in general, subject for proof and refutation by oral testimony... If it is wrongly delivered, contrary to the agreement of the parties, such delivery has no effect to make it become operative and binding. So, the question whether it was delivered in fact, contrary to the agreement on that behalf, is always necessarily open to ques-

tion on parol testimony. This in no degree infringes upon the rule that the writing is the exclusive evidence of the terms of the contract." 

Guardhouse v. Blackburn, L. R. 1 P. & D. 109 (1866), Thayer, 836.

By the will of the testatrix she disposed of three different estates. Against the first she charged legacies to the amount of £500 and against the third legacies totalling £800. She later executed a codicil in which were other legacies. This codicil as signed contained the following clause "I release and discharge my said estate from the payment of the legacies therein given to my executors and I direct that all legacies therein and herein given (and not revoked) to be paid out of my personal estate." The plaintiffs contented that the textatrix never intended the codicil to apply to the legacies in the will itself and that the words "therein and" had been inserted by the lawyer by mistake. The lawyer who drew the will testified that he received no instructions to use the words and that he did so inadvertantly, without realizing that several legacies to the parties now suing would be defeated. He testified that he read the draft to the testatrix and that she signed it without objection. The court refused to expunge the words from the will, saying: "The codicil was proved to have been read over to the testatrix before the execution thereof, she duly executed the same, and the court conceives it to be beyond its functions or powers to substitute the oath of the attorney who prepared it, fortified by his notes of the testatrix's instructions, for the written provisions contained in a paper so executed."

Angell v. Duke, 32 L. T. R. N. S. 320 (1875), Thayer, 843.

In an action for damages brought by a tenant against his landlord, the following facts appeared: The defendant let to the plaintiff by a written lease the house and furniture at a specified location. The plaintiff claimed that some time prior to the signing of the lease the defendant had agreed to put in more furniture and to change some that was already in the house. It was claimed that this promise was repeated at the time of the signing. The trial judge rejected the evidence of the earlier promise and ruled that the written agreement was conclusive upon the parties. The Court of Queen's 

Bench affirmed this decision, Blackburn, J., saying: "It is a most important rule that where there is a contract in writing it should not be added to if the written contract is intended to be a record of all the terms agreed upon between the parties; where there is a collateral contract the written contract does not contain the whole of the terms. .. Here the lease expresses the whole of the terms; the defendant agrees to let, and the plaintiff to take, the house and furniture at a certain rent; there is said to have been an agreement made beforehand during the negotiations that the defendant should let the plaintiff have more furniture for the same rent; how is this collateral? I cannot perceive that it is." 

Thompson v. Libby, 34 Minn. 374 (1885), Thayer, 859.

Action was brought for the purchase price of logs, bought according to the following memorandum : "Hastings, Minn., June 1, 1883. I have this day sold to 

R. C. Libby of Hastings, Minn., all my logs marked 'H. C. A.', cut during winters of 1882 and 1883 for ten

dollars a thousand feet, boom scale at Minneapolis,

Minn. Payments cash as fast as scale bills are produced." The defendant, Libby, set up in defence that the plaintiff had warranted the quality of the logs and that they were not of satisfactory grade. The admissibility of oral evidence to establish the alleged warranty was the chief question in dispute. The trial court admitted the testimony but the Supreme Court held that the evidence was inadmissible, saying: "To allow a party to lay the foundation for such parol evidence by oral testimony that only part of the agreement was reduced to writing, and then prove by parol the part omitted, would be to work in a circle, and to permit the very evil which the rule was designed to prevent. The only criterion of the completeness of the written contract as a full expression of the agreement of the parties is the writing itself. If it imports on its face to be a complete expression of the whole agreement,—that is, contains such language as imports a complete legal obligation,-it is to be presumed that the parties have introduced into it every material item and term; and parol evidence cannot be admitted to add another term to the agreement, although the writing contains nothing on the particular one to which the parol evidence is directed. The rule forbids to add by parol where the writing is silent, as well as to vary where it speaks." 

Beaumont v. Fell, 2 P. Wms. 141 (1723), Thayer, 1026.

The will in question bequeathed a legacy of £500 to Catherine Earnley. There was no such person. The legacy was claimed by Gertrude Yardley. In support of her claim evidence was offered that the testator in his lifetime had declared that he would "do well for her" by his will. Evidence was offered also that at the time

when the will was being drawn the testator's voice was very low and scarcely intelligible, so that the scrivener who took instructions for drawing the will had great difficulty in understanding him. The testator usually called Gertrude Yardley, "Gatty" which, it was argued, the scrivener might have mistaken for "Katy" and was by him written as "Catherine." The testator's widow declared that the claimant was the person intended. 

The court, in deciding for the claimant, said that if the devise was of land it would fail but being a gift of personal property more latitude of proof was permitted. "Now in the principal case," said the court, "the name and not the person is mistaken; and it is very material that here is no such person as Catherine Earnley claiming this legacy, which together with the proofs of the testator's having a very low voice when he made the will, and of his having usually called the plaintiff Gatty instead of Gertrude, and often declared he would do well for her, is sufficient to entitle the plaintiff to this legacy." 

Asten v. Asten, 3 Ch. 260 (1894) Thayer, 1060.

Robert Asten, by his will, specifically disposed of the four houses which he had recently built in Sudeley 

Place, Colchester, to his four sons. But in each devise the number of the house was left blank and the description of each house was identical with that of the others. 

It was shown that the houses were not numbered until more than two years after the will was drawn. The court found itself obliged to hold that Asten died intestate so far as the four houses were concerned. Said the court: "Owing unfortunately to the houses not being numbered at the date of the will, and their descriptions as given in the will being undistinguishable, I cannot

tell from the will which house the testator intended to give to any of the sons. And it is admitted that no extrinsic evidence will supply the deficiency of description, or enable the court, on inquiry, to ascertain which house was intended by the description of it in the will." 

In re Wolverton Mortgaged Estates, 7 Ch. D. 197 (1877), Thayer, 981.

Thomas Pawsey by his will bequeathed a sum of £600 to his executors, upon ust, to invest the same and pay the income to his daughter, Rosa, for life and, on her death, to hold the trust fund for the children of Rosa by any husband with whom she might intermarry "other than and except Thomas Fisher of Bridge street,

Bath," in which event the legacy was to pass to the testator's son. At the time of the testator's will there was a Thomas Fisher living on Bridge street but he was a married man with grown-up children. He had a son Henry Tom Fisher, a travelling salesman who had been paying attentions to the testator's daughter, and to whose suit the testator objected. After the father's death Rosa married Henry Tom Fisher. Whether the child of this marriage was entitled to share in the estate was the question at issue. The court held that since it was so obviously improbable that the testator had the elderly Thomas Fisher in mind parol evidence was admissible to prove that the son was the person intended by the testator. "Under the very peculiar circumstances of this case," said the court, "as there were two persons, as I consider, within the description in the will, to one of whom it was impossible that the testator's words could have applied, I think I shall not be going out of the rule if I admit parol evidence; and I must hold that

the infant respondents are not, and the petitioners are, entitled to the legacy." 

Stringer v. Gardiner, 4 DeG. & J. 468 (1859), Thayer, 976. 

The testator made certain bequests "to my niece

Elizabeth Stringer." The plaintiff at the time when the will was drawn, was about five years old. She was a granddaughter of a niece of the testator who had recently died and whose name was Elizabeth Stringer. It was argued by the defendant that the testator had the grandmother in mind and had written an earlier will during the latter's lifetime in which the same clause occurred. At the time of the earlier will Elizabeth was only a few months old. The lawyer who drew up the will testified that he copied from the earlier will without knowledge that the older woman was dead. In the plaintiff's behalf it was testified that the plaintiff was well known to the testator and had visited him. The court (Lord Chancellor Campbell) held that the evidence offered by the defendant was inadmissible. "There was, when the will was made," said the court, “a person who answered sufficiently the description of 'my niece 

Elizabeth Stringer' . . . She was a great-grandniece; still she was a niece, and there being no other person of any similar name who answered the description of niece at all, she was naturally described as 'niece.' At the time the will was made there was no one else who in the slightest degree answered the description in the will. 

We are bound judicially to hold that the plaintiff was the object of the testator's bounty." 

Violette v. Rice, 173 Mass. 82 (1899), Thayer, 958.

The plaintiff brought an action for damages against the defendant theatrical agency, alleging breach of con-

tract for failure to continue to employ her in the part of "Bertha Gessler in a play called Excelsior Junior." The written contract under which she was employed engaged the plaintiff in general terms "to render services in any theatres," etc. the plaintiff agreeing "to conform to and abide by all the rules and regulations" adopted by the defendants. On the back of the contract were printed rules of the Rice Burlesque Companies, one of which provided that: "No person shall . . . refuse a part allotted to him or her by the manager," on certain penalties, including discharge at the option of the manager. 

The plaintiff offered evidence that at the time of signing the contract it was understood and agreed that "services" meant services in the part "Bertha Gessler."

The trial court excluded this evidence and the supreme court affirmed the decision, saying: "With or without rules the engagement to render services expresses a general employment, which could not be limited to single part without contradiction. For to give evidence requiring words to receive an abnormal meaning is to contradict. It is settled that the normal meaning of language in a written instrument no more can be changed by construction than it can be contradicted directly by an avowedly inconsistent agreement, on the strength of the talk of the parties at the time when the instrument was signed." 

Bank of New Zealand v. Simpson, 82 L. T. R. 102 (1900), Thayer, 960.

Simpson, an engineer, was suing upon a contract based upon a letter, which being orally accepted by the bank, embodied the terms of the contract to build a railway which were as follows: "For contract specifications and drawings, 2½ per cent on £35,000; for super-

vision of work, 5 per cent on ditto; for measurement of work, I per cent on ditto. Provided I should be allowed another 1½ per cent on the estimate of £35,000, in the event of my being able to reduce the total cost of the works below £30,000."

The cost of constructing the railway, exclusive of the purchase of land was £28,116 16s. 4d. The cost of the land was £5,774 12s 11d. Simpson sued for the additional 12 per cent but the bank claimed that "total cost of works" included the land, thus exceeding £30,000. 

In proof of this contention the bank offered oral evidence of conversations at the time and also a circular prepared by Simpson and the bank jointly to influence the sale of stock in which the "estimated cost of the line" was set at £35,000. The trial judge rejected this evidence and the bank appealed to the Privy Council where the evidence was declared admissible. Said the court: "Extrinsic evidence is always admissible not to contradict or vary the contract, but to apply it to the facts which the parties had in their minds and were negotiating about. . . . Their Lordships think that 'the total cost of the works' may mean the cost to the owner of the completed railway, and they think that any person receiving the letter with a knowledge of the previous circular and of the conversation of the previous day, according to the Chapman version (which the jury evidently believed) might and would have so understood it." 

§ 89. Collateral Oral Agreement. 

Durkin v. Cobleigh, 156 Mass. 108 (1892), Thayer, 870.

In order to induce the plaintiff to purchase a certain lot of land, the defendant orally promised to grade and

build a street so as to connect the new development with a street already built; and also to cause city water to be put into the street. The deed, when drawn, contained no covenants to this effect. The question arose, in an action for breach of contract, as to whether oral evidence was admissible. "The obligation of the defendant," said the court, "to do the acts now in question depends wholly on his alleged oral agreement. A rule has been established which may be stated in general terms to be that an agreement by parol, which is collateral to the written contract and on a distinct subject, may be proved. . . . A more difficult question arises where the oral agreement relied on relates to something not specified in terms in the writing. It must then be determined whether the written document is to be deemed to contain all that was agreed between the parties. . . . It seems to us that the case falls within the last class of decisions, and that the alleged agreement of the defendant should be treated as an independent collateral agreement, which need not be included in the deed. The result is that the plaintiff was entitled to have his case submitted to the jury." 

Morgan v. Griffith, L. R. 6 Ex. 70 (1871), Thayer, 842.

This action was brought by a tenant against his landlord to recover for damages sustained to his crops by rabbits which the landlord had agreed to destroy. The facts were that before the lease was prepared the plaintiff entered into occupation of the premises. Finding them overrun with rabbits he refused to sign the lease unless the rabbits were destroyed. He continued to occupy but resisted the landlord's urgings to sign the lease. Finally the latter orally agreed to destroy the

rabbits and the lease was signed. By the terms of the lease the tenant was forbidden to "shoot, hunt or sport on the land, or destroy any game, but would use his best endeavors to preserve the same, and would allow his landlord or friends at any time to hunt, shoot or sport on the land." The landlord failed to keep his agreement to destroy the rabbits. They became so troublesome that the tenant terminated the lease at the end of three years and brought this action. The court held that the lease was signed upon an express collateral agreement that was founded upon a good consideration. 

Said the court: "The plaintiff, unless the promise to destroy the rabbits had been given, would not have signed the lease, and a court of equity would not have compelled him to do so, or only on the terms of the defendant performing his undertaking." The plaintiff received an award of damages. 

Chapin v. Dobson, 78 N. Y. 74 (1879), Thayer, 845.

This was an action to collect for the price of certain mechanical appliances to be attached to machinery in the defendant's mill, and also for damages for breach of contract for refusal to receive other similar appliances called for by the written agreement signed by the parties. The defendants alleged that at the time of signing the agreement the plaintiff "agreed and guaranteed that the machines would work well and to the satisfaction" of the defendant. Not proving satisfactory the appliances were detached and the plaintiff was notified to remove them and also not to ship the remainder of the order. The question arose whether oral evidence was properly admitted to prove the agreement that the appliances should be satisfactory. The court held that

the agreement in question was a collateral undertaking and might very properly be proved orally. "The written contract and the guaranty," said the court, "do not relate to the same subject matter. The contract is limited to a particular machine as such. The guaranty is limited to the capacity of the machine. It is one thing to agree to sell or furnish machines of a specific kind, as of such a patent, or of a particular designation, and another thing to undertake that they shall operate in a particuular manner or with a certain effect, or, as in this case, that they shall do the buyer's work satisfactorily. 

It seems plain both upon principle and authority that the plaintiff's undertaking was collateral to the cantract." 

Naumberg v. Young, 44 N. J. L. 331 (1882), Thayer, 849.

The plaintiffs leased in writing for six years a certain brick factory in which was a boiler and engine. 

The defendant himself was a lessee of the premises. 

The plaintiffs were button manufacturers and rented the premises for that business. The lease contained a provision that the factory should not be used for any purpose but that of button making. It was found, however, that the engine was not suitable for the work. Because of the necessity of repairs and alterations the plaintiff was put to heavy expense and damage. He brought suit upon an alleged collateral oral assurance that the premises were in proper condition for the plaintiff's use.

The Supreme Court held that there was no collateral agreement and that the plaintiff could not vary the terms of the lease by oral evidence. "In the present case," said the court, "the lease is perfect and complete

in all its parts. On its face it purports to express the terms of the letting as finally agreed upon. The effort is to engraft, by parol evidence, a contract of warranty upon a contract in writing, which appears to be complete and perfect, and is silent on that subject. Oral testimony cannot be admitted for this purpose without breaking down the rule which permits parties to make their written contracts the only evidence of their undertakings, and enables them to protect themselves from the hazard of uncertain oral testimony with respect to their engagements. Where the lease contains no warranty of the condition of the premises, declarations of the lessor on that subject are not admissible to create a warranty."

Hawley Down-Draft Furnace Co. v. Hooper, 45 Atl. Rep. 

456 (1900), Thayer, 862.

In this case the plaintiff made a contract in writing to install furnaces which were guaranteed to effect a saving in fuel of twelve per cent "over the present method of making steam using same coal." The writing did not specify how the test was to be made of whether the guaranty had been fully met. The defendants claimed that there was an oral agreement that the test should be "the ordinary everyday test measured by the number of tons of coal consumed by our boiler that went in before as compared with what went in afterwards." Measured by this test the guaranty was not met, but by a scientific test by which the evaporation of water as well as coal consumption were considered, the boilers were a success. The plaintiff appealed from the decision of the trial court in admitting oral evidence of the nature of the test to be applied, claiming that it of-

fended the parol evidence rule by enlarging the written guaranty. The Supreme Court declared that inasmuch as the writing was silent on the method of testing the boilers, the evidence introduced, of an oral agreement between the parties, did not offend the rule. "We have seen," said the court, "that the effect of the evidence objected to cannot add to or take anything from the written contract, but only tends to establish the existence of a separate oral agreement as to a matter on which the written contract is silent and which is not inconsistent with its terms." 

Gould v. Boston Excelsior Co., 91 Maine 214 (1898), Thayer, 864. "The defendant had purchased some poplar cut upon the land of Adams, and desired to have it driven down the streams to the mill. It also desired other poplar on Adams' land cut, peeled and driven. To this end, its agent had some conversation with the plaintiffs with reference to their doing the cutting, peeling and driving. 

As the result of these conversations the plaintiffs gave the defendant a written memorandum signed by them only, and the defendant at the same time gave them a written memorandum signed by its agent only."

The question in dispute between the parties was the number of cords of poplar cut or driven by the plaintiff. 

Both memoranda were silent on the identity of the scaler. The plaintiff offered to show that during the oral negotiations prior to the writing it was agreed by both parties that the scaler sent by Adams, the owner of the land should be accepted as controlling. The trial court admitted this evidence and the defendant appealed. The Supreme Court declared that in view of

the brevity, informality and incompleteness of the writings they did not purport to be the entire contract between the parties. The oral agreement was held not to conflict with the written stipulations. "The alleged oral agreement," said the court, "that Adams, the landowner, should send the scaler does not add to, subtract from, nor in any way vary the duties of either party. It was equally for the benefit of both parties. It was competent for either party to prove the stipulation by parol evidence, notwithstanding the writings." 

§ 90. Subsequent Oral Agreement. 

Thomas v. Barnes, 156 Mass. 581 (1892), Thayer, 867.

Thomas, a carpenter and builder, built a refrigerator for Barnes according to certain specifications, a copy of which, signed by Barnes, was given to Thomas. The refrigerator proved to be useless and had to be torn out of the store. Quite naturally Barnes refused to pay for it. Thomas brought suit and contended that he had fulfilled the terms of the contract. The defendant offered oral evidence that the parties understood and agreed at the time that the writing was not a complete contract and that the plaintiff made an oral warranty that the refrigerator to be constructed by him would be satisfactory. This evidence was excluded. The defendant also offered evidence that while the refrigerator was being constructed he objected to the use of paper as insulation and urged Thomas to use shavings. Thomas replied that paper was the best thing, that he knew how to build a refrigerator and would warrant it when finished. This evidence was likewise excluded. The Supreme Court sustained the defendant's exceptions on both pieces of evidence, saying that whether the speci-

fications were regarded by the parties as embodying the entire contract was a question for the jury. If so regarded, the evidence of prior conversations would not be admissible. On the second part of the evidence the court said that even if there had been a complete written contract the evidence of the controversy over the insulation of the refrigerator was important as showing a subsequent oral agreement. "It is well settled," said the court, "that an executory bilateral written contract may be varied by a subsequent oral agreement between the parties. . . . The contract, when modified for the subsequent oral agreement, is substituted for the contract as originally made, and the original consideration attaches to and supports the modified contract." 

Goss v. Nugent, 5 B. & Ad. 58 (1833), Thayer, 875.

By an agreement in writing, the plaintiff contracted to sell the defendant several lots of land for the sum of £450, and to give a good title to them. £80 was paid as a deposit. It was afterward discovered that there was a flaw in the title of one lot and it was agreed by the defendant that he would accept the defective title. 

The lots were duly conveyed and the deeds accepted by the defendant, but he now refuses to pay the balance of the purchase money and relies upon the defect in the title. The Court of King's Bench declared that if this were an ordinary contract in writing there would be no question as to the enforcibility of the substituted agreement. Because contracts for the conveyance of land are governed by the Statute of Frauds and must be in writing, the plaintiff's action must fail. Said the court: "We think the object of the Statute of Frauds was to exclude all oral evidence as to contracts for the sale of

lands, and that any contract which is sought to be enforced must be proved by writing only. But, in the present case, the written contract is not that which is sought to be enforced. It is a new contract which the parties have entered into, and that new contract is to be proved, partly by the former written agreement, and partly by the new verbal agreement. The present contract, therefore, is not a contract entirely in writing, and as to the title being collateral to the land, the title appears to us to be a most essential part of the contract." 

Hickman v. Haynes, L. R. 10 C. P. 598 (1875), Thayer, 877.

An action for breach of contract disclosed the following facts: The defendant contracted in writing for one hundred tons of pig iron at a stipulated price per ton. 

Seventy-five tons had been delivered and paid for when, at the request of the defendant, the delivery of the balance was postponed from week to week from June 2, 1873 to August 1 of the same year. On the latter date the plaintiff wrote to the defendant calling attention to the fact that he had held up the shipments until then but now requesting him to accept the shipments or to pay the difference between the contract price and the market price on twenty-five tons. After more than a year of excuses and delays this action was brought. The defence of the Statute of Frauds was set up. In deciding against the defendant the court said: "The proposition that one party to a contract should thus discharge himself from his own obligations by inducing the other party to give him time for their performance, is, to say the least, very startling, and if well founded will enable the defendants in this case to make use of the Statute of Frauds, not to prevent a fraud upon themselves, but to

commit a fraud upon the plaintiff." The court said that the parties could not vary the contract by an oral agreement, but that the defendant by his own conduct was estopped to set up the claim that the contract had been altered by oral agreement. He was held liable. 

§ 91. Document is not Base of Action. 

Kellogg v. Tompson, 142 Mass. 76 (1886), Thayer, 827.

This was an action of tort for the conversion of a promissory note for $3000, made by Johnson, dated April 

17, 1883, payable six months after date to the order of

Wilson. It was duly indorsed by Wilson and by Murphy and McCarthy, and then pledged to the plaintiff. 

It appeared in evidence that on June 16, 1883, Wilson, the payee of the above note, had delivered to the defendant his personal note for $425 which contained the following: "collateral in Johnson's note of $3,000, indorsed by Murphy and McCarthy." After this note was put in evidence, the plaintiff was permitted, despite the defendant's objection to show by the testimony of Wilson, that, in the loan of $400 by the defendant to Wilson, which was represented by the note of $425, nothing was said about the Johnson note; and that Wilson did not know that it was mentioned as collateral in any note he had given the defendant. The court admitted the evidence, and ruled that it "could not be received to affect the right of either party under the note and contract of 

June 16." The Supreme Court thus comments on the admissibility of the evidence: "The parties to the suit were not the parties to the note and contract. The rule which excludes parol testimony for the purpose of varying or contradicting a written contract is confined to the parties to the contract or their privies, and does not

prevent strangers thereto from introducing such evidence. . . . The plaintiff was not a party to the note and contract between Wilson and the defendant, and was therefore not bound by it. If it speaks falsely, or fails to speak the whole truth, he is not to blame, and can' show the truth, even by the testimony of one of the parties who is legally bound by its terms." 

Rex v. Inhabitants of Holy Trinity, 7 B. & C. 611 (1827), Thayer, 799.

In a contest over the removal of a pauper from one parish to another, it was contended that his last settlement was in Whitgift, where he had occupied a tenement and paid rent for the same. Counsel for the respondents objected that inasmuch as he had occupied under a written lease parol evidence was not admissible. 

Counsel for the appellant contended that they were not seeking to prove the agreement but simply to establish the fact of occupation and the annual value of the tene ment. The Court of King's Bench declared as follows: 

"The general rule is, that the contents of a written instrument cannot be proved without producing it. But, although there may be a written instrument between a landlord and tenant, defining the terms of the tenancy, the fact of tenancy may be proved by parol, without proving the terms of it. It was unnecessary in this case to prove by the written instrument, either the fact of the tenancy or the value of the premises." 

Coonrod v. Madden, 126 Ind. 197 (1890), Thayer, 803.

In a suit upon a promissory note there was a plea of payment. Evidence was introduced that the note had

been paid in part by a certain check but the plaintiff was permitted to testify that the check referred to by the defendant was received in part payment of a totally different note. He gave the date and amount of the said note with the interest rate. He also testified that the note had later been paid in full and surrendered to the defendant. The defendant contended that evidence of the contents of the note could not be introduced without first proving its loss, or serving notice upon the defendant to produce it in court for use as evidence. The 

Supreme Court overruled this objection, saying that the check was merely collateral to the issue and that if it had been produced in the court the parties would have been as far from the real controversy as they were before. Said the court: "The plea of payment filed by the appellant was no notice to the appellee that he would insist that the check read in evidence was a payment on the note in suit, and so there was no occasion to serve notice to produce, to be read in evidence, the note on which it was actually applied. To hold that notice must be served in order to authorize evidence of the existence of a written instrument, coming collaterally in question like the case before us, would result in much inconvenience, and would often result in defeating the ends of justice." 

§ 92. Oral Evidence to Explain Patent Ambiguity. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 93. Oral Evidence to Explain Latent Ambiguity. 

Goode v. Riley, 153 Mass. 585 (1891), Thayer, 885.

This was a bill in equity for the reformation of a deed. The facts were as follows: The plaintiff agreed to

sell to the defendant a lot of land on Summer street, Lowell. The parties were on the land and saw the exact boundaries. In fact the rear line was marked by a board fence five feet high. By inadvertance, when the deed was drawn, it purported to cover more than a thousand square feet (not owned by the seller) in the rear of the board fence. To reform the deed to conform with the plain intent of the parties was the object of the plaintiff. The defendant objected that both parties intended that the description of the land should be written as it was. The Supreme Court declared: "When both parties to a conveyance have intended to describe a certain parcel of land identified by their senses and by the words of their previous agreement, and have used words supposed by them to be apt for their purpose, but in fact describing that parcel and something more, the full purport of their acts taken together is only to convey the parcel intended. . . . It is not necessarily fatal that the evidence is parol which is relied on to show that the contract was not made as it purports on the face of the document to have been made." The plaintiff was allowed to reform the instrument.

Earl of Newburgh v. Countess of Newburgh, 5 Madd. 364 

(1820), Thayer, 901.

The late Earl of Newburgh, having estates in the counties of Sussex, Gloucester, and elsewhere, gave instructions to his solicitor to prepare will, one of the provisions of which was to give to his wife an estate for life in the estates in the counties of Sussex and Gloucester. The will was prepared and laid before a conveyancer to put into final form. By some accident the word Gloucester was struck out by the conveyancer

and the clerk who made a fair copy of the will changed the word "counties" to county. When the will was executed the solicitor failed to compare the document with the abstract which he had prepared for the conveyancer and neither he nor the earl read the will. It was duly executed under this mistake. After the death of the Earl, a controversy arose between the new Earl and the Dowager Countess. The latter filed a bill to have the mistake in the will rectified so that she might enjoy the intended life interest in Gloucester. The question arose whether the evidence offered by the countess was admissible. The court refused it on the ground that it had no authority under the Statute of 

Frauds. "To assume such jurisdiction," said the court, "would, in effect, be to repeal the Statute of Frauds in all cases where a devisor failed to comply with the statute by mistake or accident, and to operate this repeal, by admitting parol evidence of the intention of the devisor, which it was the very object of the statute to avoid." Litigation continued however and the House of 

Lords finally awarded the Countess a life estate in 

Gloucester. This was accomplished not by reforming the will but by construing other provisions of the will relating to the Gloucester estates as recognizing the countess as life tenant. 

Maurin v. Lyon, 69 Minn. 257 (1897), Thayer, 929.

The defendant's counterclaim in the present suit was based upon the alleged purchase of 5,000 bushels of wheat by the plaintiffs from the defendant's estate, Tileston. The plaintiff contended that there was no sufficient memorandum of the purchase to satisfy the Statute of Frauds. The memorandum was in the fol-

lowing form: "St. Cloud. 7-69-6. Sold Maurin Bros. 

Cold Springs 5000, 1° July Del. 99c. Duluth (Signed)

Geo. Tileston & Co. Maurin Bros." In construing the memorandum as sufficient, the court declared: "Both parties were engaged in buying and selling grain, and were presumably acquainted with technical terms of the trade. The referee who tried the cause admitted over the objection and exception of the plaintiff's parol wheat, the character '1°' was used to indicate the grade of the wheat and meant 'Grade No. I. Northern wheat'; also that 'July del' meant July delivery; that '99c. Duluth' indicated the price per bushel, and the place of delivery Duluth; that these were the meanings of the characters and terms used in the memorandum, as understood by the usages and custom's of the trade among wheat men. This evidence was competent, under the rule which admits parol evidence to show that abbreviations, and apparently ambiguous statements of description, price, etc., have a recognized meaning in the trade, and hence are a sufficient statement of the terms of the contract. . . The memorandum, as thus translated, fulfilled all the requirements of the Statute of Frauds." 

Myers v. Sarl, 3 El. & El. 306 (1860), Thayer, 935.

The plaintiff, a builder, brought suit for a sum alleged to be due on the contract price, plus certain extras alleged to have been done in constructing a house for the defendant. The contract contained a provision that all such extras must be authorized by the defendant's architect in writing and that weekly accounts must be rendered to the architect "on every Monday next ensuing the performance of such work." The delivery of

such an account was made a condition precedent to the right to collect therefor. The defendant claimed that some of the extras sued for were not authorized in writing by the architect and also that the plaintiff had not rendered sufficient weekly accounts. The arbitrator who heard the case permitted oral evidence to be introduced that the term "weekly accounts" had a special meaning in the building trade and found that the plaintiff had complied therewith. He rejected certain extras as not properly authorized, although the plaintiff produced drawings from the architect's office showing how the work should be done. The Court of Queen's 

Bench affirmed the decision on both points. In regard to the meaning of "weekly accounts," the court declared: "Words of a written commercial contract are to be understood in the sense which they have acquired in the trade to which the contract relates. It is a prima facie presumption that, if the parties to such a contract use expressions which bear a peculiar meaning in the trade, they use them in that peculiar meaning, which can be ascertained only by parol evidence." 

Brown v. Byrne, 3 E. & B. 703 (1854), Thayer, 941.

This action was brought to collect freight upon a shipload of cotton. The defendant refused to pay the full amount of the bill and alleged a general custom in the trade to a discount of three months' interest on such bills. The plaintiff contended that the custom was not good law and insisted upon payment in accordance with the written language of the contract. The court held for the defendant, saying: "Mercantile contracts are very commonly framed in a language peculiar to merchants; the intention of the parties, though perfectly

well known to themselves, would often be defeated if this language were strictly construed according to its ordinary import in the world at large. Evidence, therefore, of mercantile custom and usage is admitted in order to expound it and arrive at its true meaning." 

Gilbert v. McGinnis, 114 Ill. 28 (1885), Thayer, 947. 

In February, 1881, McGuinnis sold to Gilbert for delivery in August and September of the same year seven thousand bushels of corn. As a part of the same agreement Gilbert promised to make advances on the contract of what money McGuinnis_might require from time to time. Shortly after making the agreement 

McGuinnis called on Gilbert and requested an advance. 

The clerk in charge told him that he could have the money and commenced writing a note for the amount. 

McGuinnis indignantly declined to sign the note and declared that if he could not get the advance without signing a note he would not deliver the corn. This threat was fulfilled and in October Gilbert brought suit for breach of contract. At the trial the plaintiff offered evidence that it was customary among grain merchants to take notes for all advances made to sellers but this evidence was declared inadmissible. After a verdict for the defendant the plaintiff appealed. The Supreme 

Court held that the action of the trial court was in accordance with law. It declared that if the language were ambiguous the custom of the trade would be admissible in evidence but the language in the present case was clear. Said the court: "Instead of collecting something on his corn, as provided by the agreement, the seller is offered a loan of money on his individual note, which would be a complete change of the legal

relations of the parties. Whereas the seller was, before, a mere creditor of the purchaser, he at once upon giving such a note, becomes the debtor of the purchaser, and no part of the debt due him on account of the sale is thereby discharged." 

Smith v. Jeffryes, 15 M. & W. 561 (1846), Thayer, 1062.

This was an action of assumpsit for non-delivery of potatoes sold by the defendant to the plaintiff by the following written contract: "I hereby agree to sell Mr.

Smith, of Tanner-hill, Deptford, sixty tons of ware potatoes at £5 a ton," etc. At the trial the plaintiff offered evidence that there were three qualities of potatoes known in the neighborhood; wares, nuddlings and chats, of which wares were the largest and best. He was permitted also to offer evidence that it was orally agreed that the defendant was to deliver "Regent's wares" whereas he had tendered "wares" of an inferior quality. The case was taken to the Court of Exchequer where it was argued that the term "wares" was a latent ambiguity and that when its ordinary meaning had been explained it was permissible to prove by oral evidence what kind of "wares" were contracted for. The court held that there was no latent ambiguity and that the oral evidence admitted to establish what kind of "wares" were intended "went to vary and limit the written contract between the parties." A new trial was granted.

§ 94. Real Evidence.
