§ 95. Origin of Rule. 

(See textbook.)

§ 96. View by Jury. 

Tully v. Fitchburg R. R. 134 Mass. 499 (1883), Thayer, 722.

Tort for personal injuries occasioned to the plaintiff's intestate by being struck by a locomotive engine belonging to the defendant at a footpath crossing in Somerville. At the trial which occurred more than two years after the accident the jury viewed the scene where the accident occurred. The plaintiff contended that the knowledge thus obtained might be considered as a part of the proof and that the defendant's request that the court rule the case out for insufficient evidence was improper since the court could not know the facts gained by the jury from the view. The Supreme Court, however, in sustaining the defendant's exceptions, declared as follows: "In the present case, we are unable to see that the jury, from the view, could have acquired any knowledge of material facts which were not put in evidence in court, or that the presiding justice could have supposed that they had such knowledge. The view was not taken until more than two years after the accident, and at a different season of the year. It is suggested by the plaintiff, that the jury might have rejected the testimony of Elliott, as incorrect, from their own observations. We cannot say that this is impossible, but it is so highly improbable as to require little attention. Elliot was a civil engineer, and made measurements, and the material part of his testimony was, that for a distance of five hundred feet, towards Boston, from posts in the northeast fence, the railroad and rails were straight. He must have testified after the view, as his measurements were not made until the evening of the day of the view, and it cannot with reason be supposed that the jury undertook to reject, as

REAL EVIDENCE 

203 not in accordance with their previous general observations, the sworn measurements of an engineer, which so far as appears, the plaintiff did not question at the trial." 

§ 97. View of Things in Court. 

Gaunt v. State, 50 N. J. L. 490 (1888), Thayer, 723.

The defendant was on trial for fornication. One of the elements of proof relied upon by the prosecution was the alleged resemblance of the illegitimate child of the girl in the case and the defendant himself. The defendant requested the trial court to rule "that they must find a verdict upon the testimony in the case from the mouths of witnesses, and not from their own view of the resemblance of the child alleged by the complaining witness to be the issue of the alleged fornication." 

The court refused to rule as requested, and the defendant, after conviction, appealed to the Supreme Court. In affirming the judgment the court said: "There seems to be no good reason why a jury, if the question of resemblance is to be considered by them, should be compelled to base their decision upon a second-hand view. The effect of the substitution of testimony for inspection is to put the subject matter of investigation one further remove from its responsible judges, and thus to add to the infirmities inherent in proof of this class the additional danger of bias and imposition. 

In the case under consideration the child was in court during the trial, the attention of the jury was directed to it as the offspring of the alleged fornication, the defendant was a witness in the case; under these circumstances it was not error for the court to refuse to charge the jury that they must not consider the question of resemblance at all, and if they did consider it, it

must be from the testimony from the mouths of witnesses, and not from their own view.” 

Ihinger v. State, 53 Ind. 251 (1876), Thayer, 727.

The defendant was found guilty of selling liquor to a minor. He took the case to the Supreme Court on exceptions to the following instructions given by the court to the jury: "The mere fact that a minor represents himself to be twenty-one years old, is not of itself sufficient to excuse the sale. A mere child might make such representation, yet any person of common sense would know the statement to be untrue. The real question is, whether the defendant in making the sale acted in good faith. (1) Did the defendant use reasonable caution in making the sale? (2) Was the witness's personal appearance such as would indicate that he was twenty-one years old? In determining this question you should look at his entire personal appearance; first, his size; second, the appearance of his face. Did he have a beard or not, together with his whole general appearance, should be regarded by the jury in determining the question of good faith on the part of the defendant." 

The Supreme Court declared this charge to be erroneous. "Whether or not the defendant acted in good faith," said the court, "depended upon whether he had reasonable grounds to believe, and did believe, that Carmichael was twenty-one years of age. That. might have depended, in part, at least, upon the appearance of the latter as to age. And, doubtless, evidence would have been competent to show the appearance of the witness as to age.

But we know of no principle of law that would permit the jury to pass upon the age of the witness by his appearance to them. There is no mode of

REAL EVIDENCE 

205 putting such evidence upon the record in order that it may be passed upon by an appellate tribunal. On a motion for a new trial in the court below, the judge would have to substitute his impressions, as to the appearance of the witness as to age, for those of the jury.” 

Lucas v. Williams, 2 Q. B. 113 (1892), Thayer, 732.

In an action for damages for infringement of copyright the chief point of controversy was whether or not the defendants had sold a copy of a picture painted by Marcus Stone upon which the plaintiff's copyright was based. The picture sold was produced in court but the original picture was not in evidence. The Court thus discussed the matter: "It is said that you cannot prove that the picture which the defendants sold was such an imitation without producing the original picture. That you may produce the original picture, and put it and the alleged copy before the jury, and ask them to use their own eyes and come to a conclusion whether the picture sold is a copy of the original picture or not, is undoubted. In actions for piracy of trade-marks, it is the constant practice to produce the plaintiff's trade mark and the mark which is said to be an imitation of it, and ask the judge or jury to compare the two, and see whether the one is not an imitation of the other. To produce the original picture and the alleged copy, so that the jury may compare the two, may be the most satisfactory evidence of imitation, but it does not follow that it is the only evidence of it which can be given, nor does it follow that the evidence which was given in this case is only secondary evidence. . . . The witness says, 'Looking at the picture which the defendants sold, I say it is exactly like Mr. Marcus Stone's picture.'

When he is asked 'How do you know that?' he answers, 'Because I have seen Mr. Marcus Stone's picture.' That is not secondary evidence, but original evidence. 

If the jury were not satisfied, it would be open to them to say, 'You could have produced better evidence; you have not produced the original picture; we will not act upon this evidence though it is legal evidence.'" The jury found for the plaintiff in the original trial and this decision was affirmed. 

Commonwealth v. Williams, 2 Cush. (Mass.), 582 (1849).

The defendant was tried and found guilty of burglary. 

One of his grounds for exception was the fact that during the trial the burglar's tools taken from him by the officers of the law were brought into court and exhibited to the jury, although the prosecuting attorney admitted that some of the tools were probably not used in the crime for which the defendant was being tried. 

The Supreme Court in overruling the exceptions commented on this particular exception as follows: “As we understand the facts stated in the bill of exceptions, they import nothing further than this, that certain tools and implements were found in the possession of the defendant, and were brought into the court room in mass and exhibited to the jury, as well those that might probably have been used in the alleged burglary, as other instruments not adapted to the commission of this particular offence. The latter, being however, parcel of the larger quantity, found in the possession of the defendant, at the time of his arrest, and a part of the articles thus found being of a character that might properly have been submitted to the jury, and relied upon as evidence applicable to the case, the admission

REAL EVIDENCE 

207 of evidence as to the finding of the whole parcel, and the exhibition of the same to the jury, were not objectionable." 

Commonwealth v. Brelsford, 161 Mass. 61 (1894).

The defendant was on trial for the unlawful sale of intoxicating liquors. The liquor in question was a socalled "dandelion beer." According to the state chemist it contained more than one per cent of alcohol. The defendant claimed that it was not intoxicating and offered to prove it by permitting the jury to sample it. 

The trial judge refused to permit the jury to test the liquor. He was found guilty and one of his grounds of exception was the action of the judge in the above matter. The Supreme Court held that the statute had declared that liquor containing more than one per cent should be deemed intoxicating liquor and that such statute was constitutional. Said the court: "The issue was not whether the liquor illegally kept for sale was actually intoxicating, but whether it contained more than one per cent of alcohol. Besides, there are grave reasons against giving a jury liquor to drink for the purpose of determining whether it is or is not intoxicating."

§ 108. Handwriting as Evidence. 

Davis v. Jenney, 1 Metc. (Mass.), 221 (1840), Thayer, 721.

This was an action by the indorsee against the payee and indorser of a bill of exchange which was admitted to have been drawn for the accommodation of the drawer. The defence was that the bill had been altered

REAL EVIDENCE 

209 after indorsement by extending the time of payment from six to sixty days. The only evidence of the alteration arose from the bill itself. The trial court instructed the jury that the question must be decided upon inspection to ascertain whether the letters of the word "sixty" showed evidence of alteration after the bill was completed. He also called attention to the fact that it was an accommodation paper and asked them to consider the probability or improbability that such a bill would be made payable in six days. After a verdict for the plaintiff the defendant moved that the verdict be set aside because it was against the evidence. In affirming a judgment on the verdict the Supreme Court said: "The report states, that no evidence was offered but the bill itself. The case was to be decided upon inspection, taken in reference to the relations in which the parties stood to each other, and the admissions made by them, respectively, before the jury. Had the judge stated, that it must be decided by inspection only, without regard to any other considerations, we do not see how it could have been supported. The question as the evidence stood, was to be determined by inspection; but the inspection was to be made by the jury, with eyes and understandings qualified and prepared to make it by a knowledge of the subject matter, the nature, purposes, and effect of such acts and the relation of the parties to each other." 

Rex v. Harringworth, 4 M. & S. 350 (1815), Thayer, 742.

A controversy arose over evidence introduced in a trial for the removal of paupers, (W. Parr, his wife and child), from Gratton to Harringworth. The birth settlement of Parr has been proved to be in Harringworth.

To escape liability the latter parish alleged that in 1790 Parr was bound apprentice to one Drake of Oakham and duly served his time there with Drake. They produced an indenture purporting to be executed by Parr, by his father and Drake and attested by two witnesses. 

The death of one of the witnesses as well as of Drake and Parr's father were duly proven. The death of the second witness was not proved to the court's satisfaction, so as to entitle them to prove his handwriting. 

They tendered Parr as a witness to prove his own execution of the indenture, as well as that of his father and his master. The Court of King's Bench held that 

Parr's evidence was not admissible saying: "I know of none that is a greater or more serious inconvenience than to depart from a clearly established rule of law, that a party who would prove the execution of any instrument that is attested must lay the groundwork by calling the subscribing witness to prove it, if he can be produced and is capable of being examined.... Here the only question is, whether the parties who seek to prove the execution of this indenture must not make their way to what may be called secondary proof through the medium of those witnesses who are the plighted witnesses to the transaction, by first disposing of their testimony.

The rule therefore is universal that you must first call the subscribing witness; and it is not to be varied in each particular case by trying whether in its application it may not be productive of some inconvenience, for then there would be no such thing as a general rule." 

Wyman v. Garth, 8 Exch. 803 (1853), Thayer, 745.

In an action of ejectment by a mortgagee, the plaintiff's counsel produced the mortgage deed, but

REAL EVIDENCE 

211 instead of proving its execution by calling the attesting witness he called the defendant to the witness stand and asked him if he had not executed the deed. Upon objection by the defendant's counsel the court ruled that the execution of the deed could be proved only by the attesting witness. The plaintiff was non-suited and appealed to the court of exchequer. The court declared: "We think that the rule of law requiring proof by the subscribing witness is so inflexible, clear and universal, that it cannot be set aside by any reasoning, however cogent, and certainly it must be admitted that there is considerable force in the arguments founded on the recent statute. The cases, when carefully examined, will be found to have established, as we think, these principles as those on which our decision must turn. 

The attesting witness must be called to prove the execution of a deed, for this reason, that by an imperative rule of law the parties are supposed to have agreed inter se that the deed shall not be given in evidence without his being called to depose to the circumstances attending its execution. If, therefore, the attesting witness is not called, the deed cannot be read, because this agreement cannot be broken." 

Brigham v. Palmer, 3 Allen (Mass.), 450 (1862), Thayer, 759. 

This was an action of contract for a wagon sold and delivered. In support of his title to the wagon the plaintiff relied upon three personal property mortgages executed to him by James McKerwell, all of which were attested by a witness who was admitted to be in a neighboring town and competent. The defendant objected to proof of the mortgages except by the attesting

witness. The trial judge overruled the objections and allowed McKernell to testify to their objection. The defendant carried the case to the Supreme Court on exceptions. The court granted a new trial, saying: "The rule that the execution of an instrument which is offered in evidence by one who is a party to it cannot be proved without calling the attesting witnesses, where they are living, competent and within reach of the process of the court, is a fundamental rule of evidence in this Commonwealth, long ago established and strictly adhered to. . . . The counsel for the plaintiff have argued that the reasons for enforcing the rule have ceased since the passage of the statute which allows parties in a suit to be witnesses (St. 1857 c 305). 

But it was held in Call v. Dunning, 4 East, 53, that the admission of an obligor in an answer to a bill of discovery, that he executed the bond, did not dispense with the production of an attesting witness. . . . This decision is entirely in harmony with the views expressed by this court in Kelly v. Cunningham, 1 Allen, 473, and the ruling of the court below upon this point was erroneous." 

Stebbins v. Duncan, 108 U. S. 32 (1882), Thayer, 760.

In an action of ejectment, the question of the validity of a deed from Dunbar to Prout arose. Quite evidently this deed was not recorded. It had been executed sixtyone years before and the original had been destroyed. 

A copy of the original was offered in court. The names of the subscribing witnesses were known and the court permitted proof of the deed by proof of their handwriting. It is hard to see how proving the handwriting of a subscribing witness to a deed no longer in existence

REAL EVIDENCE 

213 could prove the signature on the lost deed to have been the genuine signature of the witness. But that procedure was approved by the U. S. Supreme Court as will be seen from the following: "Having established by proof the fact that the deed had existed and had been destroyed, and that the copy offered in evidence was a copy of the original, it only remained to prove the signing and sealing of the deed by the grantor. As the witnesses to the deed were shown to be dead, the method pointed out by law to establish the execution of the deed was by proof of the handwriting of the witnesses to the deed." 

Valentine v. Piper, 22 Pick. (Mass.), 85 (1839), Thayer, 762.

This case arose in the trial of a writ of entry in which the demandant claimed the premises as appurtenant to an ancient wharf, formerly called Price's and more recently Valentine's wharf. To show title, the demandants produced a deed executed by Sullivan as attorney of Andrew Price, a clergyman, in England and a power of attorney alleged to have been executed in England by the said Price, and signed by two witnesses. 

Eliot was called as a witness. He testified that he had corresponded with Price and believed the signature to be his. The power of attorney authorized the sale of the premises in question. After a verdict for the demandants the defendant moved to set aside the verdict and grant a new trial on the ground of misdirection and mistake of law in admission of evidence. Chief Justice

Shaw in affirming the judgment declared: "The first exception is to an instrument purporting to be a letter of attorney from Andrew Price to George Sullivan.

Evidence was given, tending to show that Andrew Price, at the time of making the instrument, and for many years previous, had resided in England, and the instrument purported to have been executed there. 

Under these circumstances, secondary evidence was offered to prove the execution of the instrument, which was objected to, without calling the attesting witnesses, or proving that they were not within the jurisdiction of the court, but the objection was overruled, and secondary evidence was admitted. And the court are of opinion that this was correct. If the instrument was apparently executed in a foreign country, we think that fact raised a sufficient presumption that the subscribing witnesses were not within the jurisdiction of the court, so as to let in other evidence of the execution." The court also held that when the attesting witnesses are not within the jurisdiction of the court, the handwriting of the party might be identified by one who had had correspondence with him. 

Gelott v. Goodspeed, 8 Cush. (Mass.), 411 (1851), Thayer, 763.

In an action of quare clausum fregit the plaintiff relied upon a deed attested by two witnesses, Goss and Scott. It was proved that both witnesses resided in 

Vermont at the time of the execution of the deed. Scott was proved to reside there at the time of the trial but nothing could be learned of Goss. The plaintiff was permitted to prove the handwriting of Goss. He offered to prove the handwriting of Stone, the grantor. The court rejected this evidence and refused to allow the deed to be read to the jury. The Supreme Court granted a new trial and a part of its opinion was as follows:

REAL EVIDENCE 

215 "Although a deed is valid in Massachusetts without any attesting witness, yet when thus attested the rules of law require that the deed be proved by the subscribing witnesses, or some one of them, unless the party shows a legal excuse for not producing such evidence. If the witnesses are out of the Commonwealth and thus beyond the control of the court, the party may properly resort to other evidence to prove the execution of the deed. If the witnesses were within the Commonwealth, proof of the execution by one of them would entitle the party to read his deed to the jury, and the like rule applies as to the handwriting where both are shown to be out of the jurisdiction of the court. 

But the plaintiff here not only offered proof of the handwriting of one of the subscribing witnesses, but also, as secondary evidence, offered proof of the handwriting of the grantor. Under our practice, this species of secondary evidence is competent when the attesting witnesses are not within the jurisdiction of the court." 

Curtis v. Belknapp, 21 Vt. 433 (1849), Thayer, 770.

The plaintiff made a contract in writing with Thompson & Kent to transport stone for work on the Vermont Railroad. Thompson & Kent failed to keep their agreement and the defendant agreed that if the plaintiff would continue to transport the stone he would pay him in accordance with the terms of the original agreement. The defendant later refused to pay and this suit was brought. To prove the execution of the contract between himself and Thompson & Kent, the plaintiff called Thompson as a witness. The defendant objected on the ground that there was a subscribing witness and that the contract could be proved only by him. The

lower court overruled the objection and the Supreme Court affirmed the decision in the following language: "The objection made in this case to proving the execution of the contract between the plaintiff and Thompson & Kent is one that has been many times held by this court to be ill-founded. The contract was was only incidentally in question here. The parties to this contract have never constituted the subscribing witness to that contract the exclusive witness of their contract. Nor could it be well argued, that the subscribing witness to any contract could ordinarily know more about the contract than the parties. It seems to involve an absurdity, that the parties shall not know all about their own contract." 

§ 109. Having Seen a Person Write. 

Commonwealth v. Nefus, 135 Mass. 533 (1883). 

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for adultery. The most incriminating evidence was a writing in cipher addressed to the woman in the case and alleged to be in the defendant's handwriting. It contained plain evidence of adulterous relations. This writing, when compared with a letter which the defendant admitted having written to the woman, had marked similarities of expression. The same pet name was used and both made reference to a threat of arrest for abortion committed upon the woman. A witness testified that he had seen the defendant write and also that for twenty years he had been working as an expert in comparing handwritings. He declared that the writing in cipher was that of the defendant. The 

Supreme Court said that in the absence of anything to

REAL EVIDENCE 

217 cast doubt upon the expert's qualification his opinion was properly received. 

§ 110. Having Corresponded with Party. 

(See text-book.) 

§ III. Testimony of Handwriting Expert. 

Moody v. Rowell, 17 Pick. (Mass.), 490 (1836), Thayer, 706. 

In an action on a promissory note for $2750, the defence was that the signatures of maker and payee were forged. The defendant called as a witness one 

Whitney who had been a teacher of writing for forty years. He was permitted to testify as to the want of genuineness by comparison of the signature to the note with the handwriting of the defendant. The court held that this testimony was proper. 

Vinton v. Peck, 14 Mich. 287 (1866), Thayer, 711.

The defence to an action on a promissory note was that the note was drawn for eight dollars and had been altered to read "eighty." Clark, a conveyancer and notary for twenty-five years, testified for the plaintiff that he had been familiar with the defendant's handwriting for many years and that the note was in his opinion genuine. He declared that the "y" could not have been added because of its relation to the other letters. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence was competent. "The value of a witness's belief," said the court, "must depend upon circumstances, but it is proper to go to the jury like other questions upon the genuineness of writings."

§ 112. Standards of Comparison. 

Moore v. United States, 91 U. S. 270 (1875), Thayer, 714.

The plaintiff brought suit in the Court of Claims to recover $5,780 on account of cotton seized by the United States during the Civil War. He appealed from the decision of the court and the only question of importance presented to the U. S. Supreme Court was whether the signature to a document contradicting his present claim and purporting to be signed by the plaintiff was properly proved. The court itself acted as a "handwriting expert" and compared the signature on the disputed document with a power of attorney executed by the plaintiff to authorize his attorney in fact to appear for him in the case. In its decision the court declared: "One of these exceptions is, that if a paper admitted to be in the handwriting of the party, or to have been subscribed by him, is in evidence for some other purpose in the cause, the signature or paper in question may be compared with it by the jury. It is not distinctly stated in this case that the writing used as a basis for comparison was admitted to be in the claimant's hand; but it was conceded by counsel that it was, in fact, the power of attorney given by him to his attorney in fact, by virtue of which he appeared and presented the claim to the court. This certainly amounted to a declaration, on his part, that it was in his hand; and to pretend the contrary would operate as a fraud on the court. We think it brings the case within the rule, and that the Court of Claims had the right to make the comparison it did.

§ 113. Hearsay Evidence Defined, 

(See textbook.) 

§ 114. The Hearsay Evidence Rule. 

Bacon v. Towne, 4 Cush. (Mass.), 217 (1849), Thayer, 311.

Action for malicious prosecution. The defendants were charged with having maliciously, and without reasonable or probable cause instituted against Bacon a complaint for burning his factory in order to defraud an insurance company. In order to rebut malice and show probable cause the defendants offered Squires as

a witness who would testify that two women, Mrs. 

Shaw and Mrs. Randall, had told him and requested him to inform the defendants that they had seen Bacon throw burning cotton into the picking room of his factory thereby, as they believed, causing the burning thereof. The trial judge rejected the evidence as hearsay. The defendants were found liable but the Supreme 

Court ordered a new trial. The issue was whether the defendants had reasonable or probable cause to bring the accusation. The court said: "In ordinary transactions of life, men do not hesitate to act upon information until they can put their informants upon oath, if, indeed, such oath would not be extra-judicial and unlawful. . . . It was important for the defendants to show that this information was communicated to them before the complaint, and, therefore, that they had reasonable expectation of being able to prove it. We think that this evidence ought to have been received and laid before the jury." 

§ 115. Exceptions-Reported Testimony. 

Wright v. Doe d. Tatham, 1 A. & E. 3 (1834), Thayer, 318.

Wright claimed certain lands under a will alleged to have been executed by John Marsden, deceased. Doe claimed under a lease from Tatham, an heir at law of Marsden. The validity of the will was therefore in issue. It appeared that in previous litigation over the will between Wright and Tatham one of the attesting witnesses, who had since died, had testified under oath. 

Wright sought to introduce as evidence a duly authenticated copy of the testimony. The trial court ruled that inasmuch as there was a surviving attesting wit-

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

221 ness such witness must be called to testify before the will and codicil could be read in court. The Appellate 

Court said that if the present litigation were between Wright and Tatham there would be no doubt of the admissibility of the testimony in the former trial. But 

Doe represented the same interest as Tatham himself. 

The fact that in his suit at equity Tatham had voluntarily dismissed his own bill could not affect the credibility of the testimony of the attending witness. Because of the refusal of the trial judge to admit the testimony a new trial was granted. 

Rex v. Inhabitants of Eriswell, 3 T. R. 707 (1790), Thayer, 322. 

John Sharpe, a pauper, was ordered by court removed from the parish of Icklingham to Eriswell. The question arose as to the legality of the order. It appeared that 

Sharpe came into the parish of Icklingham in 1767, where he was employed as a day laborer. In 1779 he was taken before two justices of the peace by the overseer of the poor to be examined as to what parish should care for him if he became a public charge. The record of this examination was produced in court and the signatures of the justices as well as of the pauper were proved. Sharpe had then testified under oath that his home was in Eriswell. At the time of this examination he was not a public charge. He continued to find employment in Icklingham for five years thereafter, when he became hopelessly insane. The court held that the record of the examination was competent as evidence for two reasons: First, that the justices had authority to make the examination and, second, that the signature of Sharpe might be received a that of a person since

deceased, because, in the eyes of the law, his insanity was equivalent to death. 

Yale v. Comstock, 112 Mass. 267 (1873), Thayer, 332.

This case was last of a series of trials over the same alleged grievance. Comstock's predecessor in title, Brace, had owned a reservoir and a mill pond, separated by some distance. Between the two was a mill, owned by Yale. Yale complained that at times Brace let much more water down the brook than was needed for the Yale mill and at other times much less than was needed to run the mill. In 1863 Brace had altered and enlarged his mill dam. Five years later he had sold to Comstock. 

In the present litigation it became important to prove whether the dam that Brace had built in 1863 was higher than the previous dam. For the purpose of proof it was proposed to offer the testimony of Brace, now dead, in a previous trial between Brace and Yale. Said the Supreme Court: "It is an established rule of law that evidence of what a deceased witness testified on a former trial is competent in any subsequent trial of the same issue, between the same parties. And privies in estate are deemed to be the same parties within this rule. . . . The parties to this suit derive their titles respectively from said Yale and Brace, and as to them are privies in estate. . . It is clear that the witness called to prove the testimony of Brace fulfilled this condition. Acting as a master in chancery, he took the testimony in writing, read it carefully to the witness, and made a report thereof to the court. He identified the report and testified that he intended to state the testimony of Marshall Brace in the exact language of said Brace, and believed it was so stated in his report."

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

223 

Minneapolis Mill Co. v. Minn., Etc. R. R., 51 Minn. 304 (1892), Thayer, 333.

In the first trial, the defendant produced a witness, one Fuller, who was examined and cross-examined, and his testimony taken down in full by the official reporter or stenographer of the court. On the second trial Fuller was no longer available as a witness, now residing in a distant State. Against the objection of the plaintiff the previous testimony of Fuller was admitted. The Supreme Court declared the evidence admissible, saying: "The admission of the testimony of a witness on a former trial is frequently inaccurately spoken of as an exception to the rule against the admission of hearsay evidence. The chief objections to hearsay evidence are the want of the sanction of an oath, and of any opportunity to cross-examine, neither of which applies to testimony given on a former trial. . . . But where the words of a witness as they come from his lips are taken down in full by an official court stenographer, this objection does not apply." 

Drayton v. Wells, 1 N. & McC. (S. C.), 409 (1819), Thayer, 336.

Action of assumpsit for services by plaintiff as overseer of the defendant's plantation for the year 1809. At the first trial a verdict was rendered for the plaintiff, but it was later set aside. At the second trial, which occurred about six years later, the defendant offered a witness who had testified at the previous trial that the agreement by which the plaintiff was hired (an oral agreement) was otherwise than what plaintiff alleged. 

The witness now stated that when he testified in 1813 the events were fresh in mind, but that he had now so

on. far forgotten the details that he could not testify there- He affirmed, however, that whatever he had said in the previous trial was the truth. The defendant then moved for leave to give parol evidence (there being no written record) of what the witness testified at the first trial. The motion was over-ruled. The Supreme Court declared that there were only four cases where the testimony of a witness in a previous trial could be introduced in a trial (1) when such witness was dead; (2) when he was insane; (3) when beyond seas; (4) when the court was satisfied that he was being kept away by contrivance of the opposite party. The court refused the motion, saying that "cases must be very few in which third persons will recollect better what a witness said than he will himself." 

Ibanez v. Winton, 222 Mass. 129 (1915).

This was an action of tort for personal injuries which had previously been tried in the Superior Court, appealed to the Supreme Court and a new trial granted. 

The plaintiff now offered in evidence a transcript of the testimony given by one Landrez in the former trial, alleging that the said Landrez was a native of Spain and when last heard from was in his native land. Landrez had been present when the accident occurred and was the chief witness. The trial judge excluded the transcript of testimony and the jury found for the defendants. (In the prior trial there had been a verdict for the plaintiff). The plaintiff alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court over-ruled the exceptions, saying: "It appears that the former witness was last heard from in Spain. No testimony was given or offered of the time of the former witness's departure from the jurisdiction

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

225 of the court, of the probable length of his absence or the likelihood of his speedy return, or of the plaintiff's ignorance thereof. There was no testimony or offer of testimony of any attempt to produce or induce the return of the witness, to take his deposition or to excuse such non-action. The case at bar presented the bare question whether the mere unexplained absence from the jurisdiction of the former witness is as a matter of law an adequate and controlling reason for the admission and introduction in evidence of the former testimony of the absent witness. However it may be in other jurisdictions, it would seem to be clear on principle and by authority, so far as may be inferred from the decisions. above cited, that such a rule should not be the law." 

Commonwealth v. McKenna, 158 Mass. 207 (1893). 

The defendant was on trial for the unlawful sale of intoxicating liquor. In the lower court the defendant's mother, who was seventy-three years old, had testified. 

She was too ill to appear at the trial in the Superior Court. The defendant offered to prove that his mother had claimed the liquor that was found on the premises. 

For the purpose of showing what her testimony had been he asked the chief of police of the city if he had heard the mother's testimony. He admitted that he had. 

The defendant then asked him if his mother had not claimed to own the liquor in question. The question was excluded. The Supreme Court declared that the action of the Superior Court was correct. Said the court: "The remaining exception is to the refusal of the court to permit a witness to testify to what Mrs. 

Kearns testified at the trial of this case in the district court, she being ill and unable to be present at the trial

in the Superior Court. The witness heard her testimony, and we assume that it was material. On this question there is a conflict of authority in other jurisdictions, but here, so far as we know, the practice has been to confine such testimony to cases where the witness has died; and the weight of authority elsewhere in criminal proceedings is, we think, that the rule should not be extended to cases where the witness is ill at the time of the trial." 

§ 116. Identity of Issues Involved. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 117. Exceptions-Depositions. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 118. Exceptions-Confessions. 

Hall v. Phelps, 2 Johns. (N. Y.) 451 (1807), Thayer, 754.

This was an action on a promissory note to which there was a subscribing witness. At the trial the defendant denied that he had given the note. The plaintiff called a witness who testified that the defendant had told him that he had executed the note in question. 

This evidence was objected to on the ground that the subscribing witness ought to have been produced or that the plaintiff must show that he had diligently endeavored to procure his attendance. The trial judge over-ruled the objection. After a verdict for the plaintiff, the defendant appealed. The court held for the plaintiff. "From these considerations," said the court, "that we are unfettered by any positive adjudication

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

227 anterior to the Revolution that the party's own confession is the highest evidence, and that you may contradict the subscribing witness, I think it results that an instrument, though attested by a subscribing witness, may be proved by the confession of the party who gave it. Allowing evidence of confession does not touch upon the rule that requires the best evidence of which the nature of the case is susceptible. The rule means only that inferior evidence shall not be given when higher evidence is in possession of the party, or is presumed to be within his power. The confession of a party that he gave a note, or any instrument, precisely identified, is as high proof as that derived from a subscribing witness." 

Fox v. Reil, 3 Johns. (N. Y.), 477 (1808), Thayer, 755.

In an action on a bond the plaintiff offered to prove that the bond had been shown to the defendants a short time before the commencement of the suit and that they had then confessed that they had made the bond. This evidence was barred upon the objection of the defendant. The plaintiffs, not being prepared to prove the bond by the subscribing witnesses or to account for their not being produced, were non-suited. The Supreme Court sustained the action of the lower court, saying: "The case of Hall v. Phelps has been thought to have decided this question. I do not consider it in that light. That case arose upon a promissory note, and this is upon a deed. The rules of evidence may be more safely relaxed in the one case than in the other. The parties may go into an inquiry as to the consideration of a note or other simple contract; but a deed concludes them from such an inquiry. A deed is an act of

much higher force and solemnity in the law. It carries with it internal evidence of good consideration. . I have not met with any case where the absence of the subscribing witness was not accounted for, in which the courts have allowed in evidence any species of confession by the party, which was inferior in its nature and less solemn in its form than that in the instance to which I have alluded, and I take it for granted that there is no such case." 

§ 119. Ignorance of Rights by Party Confessing. 

Regina v. Baldry, 2 Denison C. C. R. 430 (1852), Thayer, 288.

The prisoner was indicted for having administered poison to his wife with intent to murder her. At his trial a confession was offered. The police constable who took the confession testified that he went to the prisoner's house with two companions. "I told him," said the witness, "what he was charged with. He made no reply, and sat with his face buried in his handkerchief. I believe he was crying. I said he need not say anything to criminate himself; what he did say would be taken down and used as evidence against him." The prisoner's counsel objected to the receipt of confession, but it was allowed. The defendant was convicted and sentenced to death. The appellate court held that the confession was admissible. "It is very important for the protection of innocence," said the court, "that any man charged with a crime should be told at the time of his apprehension what that charge is. Attention should be paid to any communication made by him at that time, because generally a prisoner has no means of paying for witnesses. The accused may frequently be

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

229 in a situation at once to say that he was in such a place and could prove an alibi, and may be able to make some statement of extreme importance, in order to show that he did not commit the crime, or was not the person intended to be charged. I think whatever he says ought to be adduced either as evidence of his. guilt, or as evidence in his favor." 

Roesel v. State, 62 N. J. L. 216 (1898), Thayer, 292.

Roesel and Manshande were indicted for the murder of James Pitts. Manshande turned State's evidence. 

An alleged confession by Roesel was offered at his trial. It appeared that the officer who secured the confession had told Roesel that he needn't talk if he didn't want to, but whatever he said could be used for or against him. It appeared that the defendant was in jail and in irons at the time. He was told what his companion in crime had said and was shown a pair of pants that he himself had worn at the time of the killing. The court said that the confession was admissible. Said the court: "Though it is necessary to the admissibility of a confession that it should have been voluntarily made,that is, that it should have been made without the pressure of hope or fear from persons having authority,yet it is not necessary that it should have been the prisoner's own spontaneous act. It would be received though it were induced by a promise of some collateral benefit or boon, no hope of favor being held out in respect to the criminal charge against him, or by any deception practiced on the prisoner, or false representation made to him for the purpose; provided there was no reason to suppose that the inducement held out was calculated to produce any untrue confession."

Commonwealth v. Morey, 1 Gray (Mass.), 461 (1854).

The defendant was under indictment for breaking and entering a certain shop in the night time and stealing bank bills and silver coin, belonging to the prosecutor.

While he was alone in a prison cell, the prosecutor visited him and told him that if he wished to make any explanations he would be glad to hear them. The prisoner was silent for a minute or two. The prosecutor then told him that he thought it was better for all concerned in a criminal case for the guilty party to confess. 

The prisoner then said that he supposed he would have to stay there whether he confessed or not. The prosecutor replied that he supposed he would have to stay, but that it was always considered more honorable to confess. Under these circumstances a confession was given. The defendant's attorney objected to its admission in court on the ground that it was induced by promise that it would be better for him. The Supreme 

Court, however declared that the confession was admissible and adopted the following language of another court: "To exclude the confession, there must appear to have been held out some fear of personal injury, or hope of personal benefit, of a temporal nature." 

§ 120. Artifice or Deception. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 121. Threats or Inducements. 

Commonwealth v. Preece, 140 Mass. 276 (1885), Thayer, 296. 

Preece, Burns and Guerin were indicted for burning a warehouse in the night time. The principal question

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

231 involved in the bill of exceptions after the conviction of the three defendants was whether the confession of Burns was admissible. It appeared that Burns was brought into the presence of three officers. Munyan, one of the officers, testified: “I did not tell him' he had better tell about it. I think Mr. Wright (one of the other officers) said he had better." Wright testified that he "did not say to Burns that he had better tell the truth, but that he might have told him to tell the truth." 

The court said that: "If either officer had said to Burns 'you had better tell the truth,' or 'you had better tell about it,' we should be of the opinion that the confession would be incompetent, because such language, in the connection in which, according to their testimony, it was spoken, would naturally convey to the mind of Burns the idea that he would gain some advantage if he confessed his guilt. But, on the other hand, if the officer merely asked him to tell the truth, this would not imply that the officer promised any advantage if he confessed. As the evidence was conflicting, we cannot say as a matter of law, that the decision of the presiding justice of the Superior Court, admitting the evidence, was erroneous." 

Commonwealth v. Flood, 152 Mass. 529, 25 N. E. 971 (1890). 

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for adultery with his step-daughter. A physician testified that he was called to treat professionally the girl in question and found that she was pregnant. The defendant then acknowledged that he was the father of the child and that he wanted an abortion performed. Soon after this the physician sent for the defendant and told

him that if he wanted him (the witness) to do anything to rid his daughter of the child, it would be necessary for him to relate all the circumstances in order to remove any doubt as to his being the father of the child. 

The defendant then admitted having had criminal relations with the girl on many occasions. Several witnesses were concealed in an adjoining room, with the door ajar. The Supreme Court held that the confession was admissible. "So far as appears," said the court, "the confessions were voluntary, and the defendant was not induced to make them by any 'fear of personal injury, or hope of personal benefit, of a temporal nature' threatened or promised by any person who, as he supposed, had 'some power or authority to assure to him the promised good, or to cause or influence the threatened injury." 

Commonwealth v. Nott, 135 Mass. 269 (1883).

The defendant was on trial for larceny of bacon in a building. It appeared that losses had been suffered in a certain market. A police officer testified that he concealed himself in the market and saw the defendant, who was also a police officer, reach in the window and steal the bacon. The witness did not give the alarm, but later met the defendant on the street and asked him where he got it. The defendant said he found it on the street. The witness suggested that he take it to the station house. After the defendant had related the same story at the station, the witness said: "Now, Jim, you had better own up. I was it the place when you took it. We have got you down fine. This is not the first you have taken. We have got other things against you nearly as good as this." The defendant then confessed.

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

233 The question arose on exceptions whether the confession was admissible. The Supreme Court said that the confession was inadmissible because of the circumstances under which it was made. The words used, "you had better own up" were deemed equivalent to saying, "you had better confess." "The words which follow," said the court, "imply that denial would be useless because the defendant had been seen to do the act. Finally, there was an intimation that other criminal acts could be proved against him. . . . They were not simply an admonition to tell the truth, but they held out an inducement to make a confession of guilt. The confession should therefore be excluded." 

§ 122. Corroboration, When Necessary. 

Commonwealth v. Tarr, 4 Allen (Mass.), 315 (1862).

The defendant was on trial under an indictment for adultery with Susannah Miller. A witness testified that the defendant admitted to him being the father of Susannah's bastard child, and that he and the woman had lived together as man and wife in his father's house. A physician testified that prior to Susannah's confinement the defendant had spoken to him about it and had referred to her as his wife. Evidence was offered in defence to the effect that Susannah and the defendant's mother slept together. Susannah denied that the defendant had ever had carnal intercourse with her. It was objected that the confession was not corroborated. 

The Supreme Court held that the confession was clearly admissible. "The facts in proof," said the court, "that the defendant resided in the same house with the woman; that he had ready means of access to her; that she was delivered of a child who was apparently a bastard;

that he applied to a physician some weeks prior to the birth of the child to attend her in her confinement, and then called her his wife, were all circumstances corroborative of his confession as testified to by the witness Marshall, and properly submitted to the jury, in connection with his admissions, as evidence of his guilt." 

§ 123. Confessions of Joint Defendants. 

Queen v. Manning, 12 Q. B. D. 241 (1883), Thayer, 129. 

Manning and Hannam were indicted for conspiracy to cheat and defraud the prosecutor. The jury were directed that they might find one prisoner guilty and acquit the other. They returned a verdict of guilty against Manning, but were unable to agree as to Hannam, and were discharged from further service. Counsel for Manning endeavored to have the verdict against him set aside for misdirection. The court granted a new trial, saying: "In this case I have come, after considerable doubt, and I confess with great reluctance, to the conclusion that there must be a new trial. The man who was convicted had a perfectly fair trial, and a summing up distinctly in his favor; and every precaution was taken which could be taken to prevent any evidence being acted on by the jury which could be legitimate evidence against him. Nevertheless, I am satisfied by the argument of Mr. Charles that there is an imperative rule of law which should have prevented my Lord from informing the jury, as he did, that it was possible in such a case to acquit one of the men and convict the other. The rule appears to be this: In a charge by conspiracy in a case like this, where there are two defendants, the issue raised is whether both the

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

235 men are guilty and if the jury are not satisfied as to the guilt of either, then both must be acquitted." 

State v. Mainor, 6 Ind. (N. C.), 340 (1846), Thayer, 132.

Two defendants, a man and a woman, were indicted for committing the crime of fornication, by bedding and cohabiting together without being married. Upon trial one was found guilty and the other not guilty. 

The case was appealed to the Supreme Court where an order arresting judgment was confirmed. "The crime charged on these persons," said the court, "could not be committed but by both of them; and upon a verdict that one of them was not guilty, it appears conclusively that the other could not be. . . . A joint act is indispensible to the crime in either, and the record affirms that there was no such joint act." 

State v. Rinehart, 106 N. C. 787 (1890), Thayer, 182.

Rinehart and Mary Lindsay were on trial for fornication and adultery. While there was ample evidence to convict the male defendant, there was very little except the admissions of the latter to involve the woman.

The judge instructed the jury that unless there were evidence of corroboration they could not consider the confession of Rinehart to be used against the woman. 

Both were found guilty and the conviction was affirmed. 

Rex v. Warichshall, Leach (4th Ed.), 263 (1783), Thayer, 286. 

Thomas Littlepage was indicted for grand larceny and Jane Warickshall was indicted as accessory after

Columps) the fact to the crime. The accessory made a full confession of her guilt and in consequence the stolen property was found in her lodgings, concealed between the sackings of her bed. Because the confession was received by promises of favor the court refused to admit it in evidence against her. Her lawyer contended that the fact of finding the stolen property in her room, flowing as it did from her confession, was likewise inadmissible as evidence. The court held that the evidence was admissible. "Facts thus obtained, however," said the court, "must be fully and satisfactorily proved, without calling in the aid of any part of the confession from which they may have been derived; and the impossibility of admitting any part of the confession as a proof of the fact, clearly shows that the fact may be admitted on other evidence; for as no part of an improper confession can be heard, it can never be legally known whether the fact was derived through the means of such confession or not; and the consequences to public justice would be dangerous indeed; for if men were enabled to regain stolen property, and the evidence of attendant facts were to be suppressed, because they had regained it by means of an improper confession, it would be holding out an opportunity to compound felonies." 

§ 124. Exceptions-Dying Declarations. 

Wright & Clymer v. Littler, 3 Burr. 1244 (1761), Thayer, 351.

This was an action of ejectment for certain copyhold lands. The lessor of the plaintiff made out his title under an indisputed will of his grandfather, John Cly-

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

237 

The defendant claimed 

This will was in mer, dated February 17, 1743. under an alleged will dated in 1745. the handwriting of Medlicott, a son-in-law of the alleged testator. It was witnessed by Medlicott and one 

Mitchell, and was in favor of John Clymer's youngest son. Medlicott died in May, 1747, having had custody of the first will until three weeks before his death, when it was delivered by him to his sister. The second will was alleged to have been found among Medlicott's papers. The grandson who was to take under the first will was a small child. The will was proved in 1751, but no action was brought to oust the uncle from the premises for ten years. At the trial the defendant produced the second will. Both witnesses were dead, but their handwritings as well as that of testator were proved. The plaintiff insisted that this will was a forgery. He called Mary, a sister of the alleged subscribing witness, who testified that when the latter was dying he pulled out of his bosom the will of 1743, and gave it to her saying that it was the "true will of John Clymer." Another witness testified to the language used.

When Mary was cross-examined by the defendant's counsel she not only persisted in her previous testimony but declared that the dying man, Medlicott, then confessed that he had forged the will of 1745. The latter will was found by the judge and jury to be a forgery. 

The matter was taken to the appellate court, one of the grounds being the admission of the dying declaration of Medlicott that he had forged the will. The court declared: "The instrument of 1745 was equally in his custody and secreted. The account he gave of it in his last moments is equally proper. . . . As the account was a confession of great iniquity, and as he could be under no temptation to say it, but to do justice and ease

his conscience, I am of opinion the evidence was proper to be left to the jury." 

Wilson v. Boerem, 15 Johns. 286 (1818), Thayer, 358.

In an action on a promissory note it was alleged in defence that S, the maker, secured the indorsement of Brown and defendant as accommodation indorsers and that the note was delivered to the plaintiff for discount, but instead of discounting it he had pledged it to another for his own debt. In order further to make out the defence, defendant offered the dying declarations of Brown with reference to the note. Against the plaintiff's objection the judge ruled that if Brown were in extremis at the time of making the declarations the same were admissible. The Supreme Court declared the trial court erred. It declared that dying declarations are not admissible except in trials for homicide. "Having an opportunity to cross-examine a witness," said the court, "is a high and important right, and ought not to be violated, except from the most imperious necessity; and I am persuaded that neither principle nor policy requires the adoption of any such rule of evidence in civil cases. The dying declaration of Brown, in the case before us, ought not, therefore, to have been admitted in evidence." 

Rex v. Mead, 2 B. & C., 605 (1824), Thayer, 361.

The defendant was indicted for perjury and was found guilty. The perjury consisted in Mead's swearing that one Law had been present at and engaged in smuggling operations at a place called Salt Pans. Upon this charge Law was tried and found not guilty. He

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

239 then prosecuted Mead for perjury, and after the conviction was mortally wounded by Mead. A dying declaration made by Law was offered in opposition to the granting of a new trial for Mead. In this declaration 

Law had described the circumstances under which he was shot by Mead and also proceeded to deny that he was present at or had any concern in the smuggling transaction. The court held that the declaration was not admissible. "We are all of opinion," said the court, "that the evidence cannot be received. .. Here, the dying declaration of Law was for the purpose not of accusing, but of clearing himself. It therefore falls, not within the exception on which those decisions proceeded but within the general rule, that evidence of this description is only admissible where the death of the deceased is the subject of the charge, and the circumstances of the death the subject of the dying declaration." ... 

People v. Davis, 56 N. Y. 95 (1874), Thayer, 368.

Davis was indicted for "advising and procuring one Clara Penry to submit to the use of an instrument by one Crandall with intent to procure a miscarriage, causing the death of the mother and child." The prosecution offered to show by the dying declarations of Clara Penry that the defendant was guilty of the offence alleged. The evidence was admitted and the defendant was found guilty. The Supreme Court declared that the trial court erred in receiving proof of the declarations. "Such evidence is admissible, in cases of homicide,” said the court, "only where the death of the deceased is the subject of the charge, and the circumstances of the death are the subject of the dying declarations. . . .

This is the settled rule, and it is unnecessary to discuss the reasons upon which it was founded. Applying the rule to this case, the declarations were not admissible. 

The charge against the prisoner was not homicide in any degree. The crime charged against him is that of persuading the deceased to submit to the use of an instrument upon her person, and to take drugs with intent to produce a miscarriage-in consequence of which the death of the child and her own were produced." 

§ 125. Possession of Mental Faculties at Time. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 126. Expectation of Death. 

Commonwealth v. Haney, 127 Mass. 455 (1879), Thayer, 369.

Haney was indicted for manslaughter in causing the death of Patrick Cannon by kicking him in the abdomen. A controversy arose over a dying declaration of 

Cannon. The local justice of the Police Court went to the house four days before Cannon's death and found the injured man propped up in bed, very weak, so much so that it was hard to distinguish what he said. He asserted several times that he could not live and also expressed doubt that he would live until he completed his statement. The statement was finally reduced to writing and Cannon signed it with his mark. The witness was about to use the memorandum to refresh his recollection when, upon call of defendant's counsel it was put in evidence and read to the jury, subject to the defendant's objection that it did not appear affirmatively

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

241 that Cannon, at the time of the statement had no expectation and hope of recovery. The defendant was found guilty and alleged exceptions. The court overruled the exceptions, saying: "The rule as to the admissibility of dying declarations does not require that they should have been made while the sufferer is literally breathing his last. It is enough that they were made when he understands that his injuries are fatal, and believes his death to be near at hand. If he believed himself to be in a dying state, it is immaterial that he lived for four days after making the declaration. . . . It is true that, if he had any expectation or hope of recovery, however slight, the declarations are inadmissible. But we find nothing in the bill of exceptions that indicates that he had any such expectation." 

Woodcock's Case, Leach (4th Edition), 500 (1789), Thayer, 355.

Woodcock was on trial for the murder of his wife. 

It appeared in evidence that the injured woman was found apparently dead from wounds and exposure but was revived and restored to consciousness. A magistrate was sent for. He took down the woman's statement placing the crime upon her husband, read the statement over to her and she signed it with her mark. The woman lived for two days after the examination by the magistrate. The surgeons testified that there was no evidence that the woman realized the seriousness of her wounds or that her death was imminent. The court declared that to be admissible the statement must be made "when the party is at the point of death, and when every hope of this world is gone; when every motive to falsehood is silenced, and the mind is induced by the most powerful considerations to speak the truth; a

situation so solemn and so awful is considered by law as creating an obligation equal to that which is imposed by a positive oath administered in a court of justice." 

The matter was left to the jury to decide whether notwithstanding her failure to give evidence of her fear of imminent death, the deceased had not after all spoken under the sanction of that fear and knowledge. The prisoner was found guilty and executed. 

Regina v. Morgan, 14 Cox C. C. 337 (1875), Thayer, 366.

The defendant, a soldier, was on trial for murder of a fellow soldier. At the time of the occurrence the defendant and the deceased were in the hut used as sleeping quarters, no one else being present except another soldier who was dead drunk in bed. Two boys had just been sent from the hut by the defendant on various pretexts. Shortly afterward the deceased was seen coming from the hut with his throat cut from ear to ear and bleeding terribly. The deceased could not speak but motioned for a paper and wrote upon it naming the defendant as the attacker. When the latter was brought before the dying man he pointed at him. The surgeon was unable to do anything to save the man because except for the vertebrae the neck was completely severed. Death occurred within ten minutes after the writing of the paper. It was excluded as a dying declaration because the judge said that it was not clear that the accuser knew that he was dying. Because of the circumstances of the case and the action of the wounded man in pointing at his attacker the latter was found guilty and sentenced to death. 

127. Dying Declarations Limited to Res Gestae. 

(See textbook.)

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

243 

§ 128. Hearsay Evidence in Case of Lost Wills. 

Woodward v. Goulstone, 11 App. Cases 469 (1886), Thayer, 620. 

The case was originally brought by Goulstone and others seeking probate of a will of Morgan executed in 

1868. Woodward and others set up an alleged lost will supposed to have been executed in 1877 or 1878. The probate court found in favor of the lost will. Goulstone took the case to the Court of Appeal where the decision was reversed. Woodward thereupon appealed to the 

House of Lords where the decision of the Court of Appeal was confirmed. According to the evidence offered in proof of the lost will the deceased had said that he had "remembered" certain persons in his will or that he "had treated them handsomely." No witness was produced who had read the alleged will and could testify to its exact contents. Lord Herschell declared: "It will be observed that the only evidence of the contents of the will in this case are the post-testamentary declarations of the testator. If these declarations be inadmissible, then there is absolutely no evidence to support the case of those who are propounding the will. As the court below came to the conclusion that even admitting them the will was not established, and as we concur in that view, of course the question does not arise here for determination; but as far as I am concerned I desire to guard against it being supposed that I hold that these post-testamentary declarations are admissible." 

Clark v. Turner, 50 Neb. 290 (1897), Thayer, 624.

Clark and Scott propounded for probate what purported to be a copy of the last will of John J. Turner.

5 The will was alleged to have been stolen by burglars. 

No witnesses were produced who had actually read the will. Scott was blind. He testified that Turner read the will to him, asking if it were in legal form. Declarations were also made to others as to the contents of the will. Turner told Clark that he and Scott were named as executors. After the death these various declarations of the deceased were written out and presented for probate. The sons of Turner contested the alleged will.

The court declared that since no witness had read the will all the evidence was hearsay. In deciding the case the court adopted the following language from a Kentucky decision: "The books of reports contain many cases in which wills lost or destroyed have been offered for probate upon parol or other secondary evidence of their contents, and many in which such wills were established. But, in an examination of these cases as extensive an opportunity would allow, we have found none in which there does not seem to have been the evidence of witnesses who knew, or might be presumed to have known, the contents of the will from their own inspection; none in which the declarations, or even professed reading, of the decedent have been held to be alone sufficient on this point."
