§ 129. Exceptions-Declarations Part of Res Gestae. 

Regina v. Buckley, 13 Cox C. C. 293 (1873), Thayer, 558.

Buckley was indicted for the wilful murder of a policeman, James Green. Buckley, during the previous year, had been convicted of stealing, largely through the testimony of the deceased. After his imprisonment 

Buckley had been heard to mutter threats of vengeance against Green. On the day of the homicide, in consequence of certain information, Green had made certain statements to his superior officer of his intended duty that night. Inasmuch as the entire evidence for the crown was circumstantial it was important that this

statement be put in evidence. The defendant's counsel objected, but the judge overruled the objection. The police inspector stated that Green had reported that 

Buckley was "at his old game of thieving again" and that he intended to watch his movements that night. 

The body of Green was found two days later covered with wounds in the canal some distance from Buckley's house. The decision of the judge in admitting the statement was concurred in by his associate on the bench. 

Chase v. Lowell, 151 Mass.. 422 (1890), Thayer, 601.

Action of tort for personal injury occasioned to the plaintiff by the fall of a shade tree standing in a public street in Lowell. For the purpose of proving that the tree was in an unsafe condition and that the city officials knew, or had reason to know of its condition, the plaintiff offered evidence of the remarks made by persons when looking at the tree and its exposed roots. The earth had been dug away from the tree and the roots had been exposed for a day or more while the street was being paved. In deciding that the evidence was properly received the Supreme Court declared: "The street was a thoroughfare near the centre of the city, and in that part of the street where the tree stood were two churches, a schoolhouse, and private residences. The decayed roots were visible to persons on the sidewalk opposite, and the evidence tended to show that large numbers saw them. All this was competent evidence of the notoriety of the defect, and was not objected to. 

The acts of persons in looking at the roots were an important part of the evidence. From this it might be inferred that they noticed the decayed condition of the

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

247 roots, and so that knowledge of the defect became general. But that evidence might properly be strengthened by introducing as part of the res gastae the declarations which accompanied the acts, and which characterized them as acts that communicated intelligence of the condition of the tree to those who looked. The remarks made at the time rendered it certain that the view of the roots gave notice of the defect to those who saw them. 

If the fact that these persons looked at the decayed roots was competent as tending to show the notoriety of the defect, then clearly the accompanying declarations which tended to show the nature of the act of looking were also competent." 

Doe d. Shallcross v. Palmer, 16 Q. B. 747 (1851), Thayer, 608. 

An action of ejectment was brought by one who claimed under an heir at law of William Brookes seeking to oust from possession the defendant who was occupying certain premises as devisee of William Brookes. The question turned on the admissibility of evidence concerning the execution of the will of William Brookes and whether certain alterations existed at the time of the execution. The alteration in dispute was a clause devising the land in controversy to the defendant. 

The evidence relied upon consisted of declaration by the testator frequently made before and nearly down to the time of the execution of the will, that he intended to make provision for the defendant therein. Without this alteration there would have been no mention of the defendant. The alteration was in the testator's handwriting. The defendant argued that the testator had altered this clause before signing the document. The

jury decided that the alteration was made prior to the execution of the will. The judge directed a verdict for the defendant. The Court of Queen's Bench declared that the evidence was admissible. "It being quite certain," said the court, "that the testator intended that 

Appollina Biddulph (Mrs. Palmer) should take the premises after the death of Francis, and the intention appearing to us to be testified according to the rules of law, we think that she ought to be allowed to remain in the possession of them, and that this rule to enter the verdict for the lessor of the plaintiff ought to to be discharged."

Mutual Life Ins. Co. v. Hillmon, 145 U. S. 285 (1892). 

Thayer, 630.

Mrs. Hillmon brought action on three policies of insurance on the life of her husband. The insurance companies refused payment on the ground that the dead body buried as John W. Hillmon was not that of the plaintiff's husband. The three cases, involving the same facts, were ordered to be tried together. After the impanelling of the jury each defendant claimed a right to challenge peremptorily three jurors but the judge declared that after the consolidation of the cases they had a right to but one such challenge each. The defendants claimed that the body buried was that of Walters. For the purpose of proving that Walters was travelling with Hillmon and of his death, letters were offered in evidence. A letter written by Walters to his sister a few days before the alleged death stated that he expected to leave Wichita, Kansas, "with Mr. Hillmon a sheep trader." In a letter to his bethrothed written at about the same time he spoke of Hillmon who had engaged

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

249 him to travel with him in search of a site for a sheep ranch. The letters were excluded. There was evidence that nothing was ever heard from Walters after the trip began and that the body resembled his. The jury found for the plaintiff. The U. S. Supreme Court reversed the judgment and ordered a new trial because of the curtailment of the right of peremptory challenge of jurors and the exclusion of the letters from Walters. "The letters in question were competent," said the court, "Not as narratives of facts. . . but as evidence that, shortly before the time when other evidence tended to show that he went away, he had the intention of going, and of going with Hillmon, which made it more probable both that he did go and that he went with Hillmon, than if there had been no proof of such intention. In view of the mass of conflicting testimony introduced upon the question whether it was the body of Walters that was found in Hillmon's camp, this evidence might properly influence the jury in determining that question." 

§ 130. Spontaneous Exclamations. 

Chapin v. Marlborough, 9 Gray (Mass.), 244 (1857), Thayer, 590.

Action of tort for injuries received by the plaintiff on 

January 11, 1856, from a defect in the highway. The plaintiff called a physician who testified that he saw the plaintiff for the first time on June 2, 1856, and that the plaintiff showed him his leg and wished him to examine it; that plaintiff complained of extreme pain in the leg. He was also permitted to testify as to what the plaintiff said about how the injury occurred. The defendant objected to the testimony and after a verdict for the plaintiff took the case to the Supreme Court on

exceptions. The court sustained the exceptions saying: "It was a statement of a fact, and was used as evidence of that fact. It was therefore wrongly admitted." The court adopted the language of a prior decision, as follows: "Where the bodily or mental feelings of a party are to be proved, the usual and natural expression of such feelings, made at the time are considered competent and original evidence in his favor. There are ills and pains of the body, which are proper subjects of proof in courts of justice, which can be shown in no other way. Such evidence, however, is not to be extended beyond the necessity on which the rule is founded. Anything in the nature of narration or statement is to be carefully excluded, and the testimony is to be confined strictly to such complaints, exclamations and expressions as usually and naturally accompany and furnish evidence of a present existing pain or malady." 

Agassiz v. London Tramway Co., 21 Weekly Rep. 199 (1873) 

Thayer, 654.

Action for personal injuries to the female plaintiff while a passenger in one of defendant's horse cars. The plaintiff testified that after the car toppled over she got out and saw the damage caused by the collision. It was further proposed that she be permitted to relate the following: A passenger exclaimed, "By Jove, this fellow's conduct ought to be reported," and that the conductor had replied. "He has already been reported, for he has been off the line five or six times today; he is a new driver." The trial court refused to admit this testimony and the Appellate Court declared that the exclusion was proper. Said the court: "Now, if these remarks can be considered a part of the res gestae, I

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

251 1 think they ought to have been admitted. But I am of opinion that they are not part of the res gestae. It is just this species of evidence which a judge ought to reject. The conductor's remark had no relation to the accident in question, but referred to the conduct of the driver at another time or times. His remark was well calculated to prejudice the jury against the driver, and might have induced them to believe in his culpability in the case before them, when in truth it referred to his conduct under perfectly distinct circumstances." 

§ 131. Time of Declaration. 

Regina v. Bedingfield, 14 Cox, C. C. 341 (1879), Thayer, 655. 

The defendant was on trial for murder of a woman with whom he had formerly been unlawfully intimate. 

He had been heard to utter threats to kill her by cutting her throat and she had asked police protection.

The woman was a laundress. Her assistants were in the yard when the defendant went into the house. In a short time the deceased came out of the house, her throat cut, bleeding terribly. She said something and pointed to the house. A controversy arose over the admissibility of the statement made by the deceased. The statement was apparently offered as a part of the res gestae and not as a dying declaration. It was declared inadmissible as a part of the res gestae for the following reasons: "It was not a part of anything done, or something said while something was being done, but something said after something done. It was not as if, while being in the room, and while the act was being done, she had said something which was heard. . . .

Anything uttered by the deceased at the time the act

was being done would be admissible, as, for instance, if she had been heard to say something, as 'Don't, Harry!'

But here it was something stated by her after it was all over, whatever it was, and after the act was complete." 

The accused was nevertheless found guilty and hanged. 

Barber v. Merriam, 11 Allen (Mass.), 322 (1865), Thayer, 591. 

In an action of tort for personal injuries the plaintiff called as a witness a surgeon who was allowed to repeat to the jury the statements of the plaintiff herself, made since the suit was brought, for the purpose of receiving medical advice, as to the character and seat of her injuries and sensations. This was done against the objections of the defendant. There was a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendant alleged exceptions. The 

Supreme Court overruled the exceptions, saying: "The opinion of a surgeon or physician is necessarily formed in part on the statements of his patient, describing his condition and symptoms and the causes which have led to the injury or disease under which he appears to be suffering. This opinion is clearly competent as coming from an expert. But it is obvious that it would be unreasonable if not absurd to receive the opinion in evidence, and at the same time to shut out the reasons and grounds on which it was founded. . . . It is suggested, in behalf of the defendant, that the statements in the present case were made by the plaintiff after the commencement of this action. But we do not think that for this reason only they ought to have been rejected. It was a circumstance which may have detracted from the weight of the evidence of the opinion of the physician, so far as it was founded on these statements.

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

253 But as the statements were made to a medical man and for the purpose of receiving medical advice, they were competent and admissible." 

Williams v. Great Northern R. R., 68 Minn. 55 (1897), Thayer, 593.

The plaintiff won a verdict of $20,000 against the defendant for injuries sustained in a railroad collision.

The defendant appealed the case on the ground that certain statements made to third parties by plaintiff descriptive of his pain and suffering and of the effect and symptoms of his injuries were admitted in evidence. 

The court laid down certain tests of admissibility of such evidence. Said the court: "According to the great weight of modern authorities, the mere descriptive statement of a sick or injured person as to the symptoms and effects of his malady are only admissible under the following circumstances: (1) They must have been made to a medical attendant for the purpose of medical treatment. (2) They must relate to existing pain or other symptoms from which the patient is suffering at the time, and must not relate to past transactions or symptoms, however closely related to the present sick- This was probably always the rule, but the courts are now disposed to apply it more strictly than formerly. (3) Such statements are only admissible when the medical attendant is called upon to give an expert opinion based in part upon them. He cannot merely testify to the statements, and then stop. In the absence of any expert opinion based on the statements, they stand on the same footing as if made to a non-expert witness." The court decided that the plaintiff's wife, being a non-expert could testify only to "such explana-

tions or complaints as were apparently the natural and instinctive manifestations of existing suffering." A doctor who testified to other statements beyond mere expressions of pain or of symptoms of disease was not asked for an expert opinion. The court said that he stood on the same grounds as a non-expert witness and that part of his testimony that a non-expert could not testify to was admitted erroneously. 

Kilburn v. Bennett, 3 Metc. (Mass.), 199 (1841), Thayer, 597. 

This was an action by the collector of taxes to recover the amount of the taxes assessed upon the defendant by the town of Groton for the year 1839. After a verdict for the town, the defendant took the case to the Supreme Court on exceptions. The principal question was whether certain evidence offered by the defendant was rightly rejected. In order to establish the fact that the defendant was not an inhabitant of the town on the 1st of 

May the defendant proved that on April 27th he removed to Tyngsboro to the house of a brother. For the purpose of showing the intent with which he moved he offered his former landlord in Groton who would testify that the defendant notified him early in April of his intention to vacate before the 1st of May and to reside with his brother in Tyngsboro until he could move his family to California. The lower court held that this was a mere declaration of the defendant in his own favor and hence inadmissible. The Supreme Court declared: "The gen eral rule undoubtedly is, that a party cannot give in evidence his own declarations in his favor, unless they accompany some act, and are a part of the res gestae. 

But it appears to us that the declarations offered to be

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

255 proved are within the qualification of the rule. They were made in the ordinary course of business, and in relation to the defendant's removal; and they were made to the owner of the house in which he was at the time residing. This giving notice of his intended removal is to be considered an act which he might prove in any case in which it became material, and if so, all that he said, explanatory of his intention in relation to his removal seems to us to be admissible." 

Viles v. Waltham, 157 Mass. 542 (1893), Thayer, 598.

The chief question involved in the case was whether 

Viles was domiciled in Waltham, May 1, 1890, for the purpose of taxation. Evidence was undisputed that he resided in Waltham until April 28, 1890, when he left for Chicago where he afterward dwelt. He was taxed in Waltham and seeks to prove that the assessment for 

1890 was unlawful. The court declared: "To acquire a domicile there must be residence in a place and an intention to make that place one's home." To maintain his suit the plaintiff was required to prove, not only that he took up his abode in Chicago, but also that he did it with the intention of giving up his home in Waltham and acquiring a home in Chicago. The change in abode might be temporary or permanent. . . Declarations of a person accompanying a change of his abiding place have always been held competent to explain the change as a part of the res gestae; but declarations in such cases are often admissible on a broader ground than as a part of the act of removing from one place to another.' 

Evidence was received that before leaving Waltham the plaintiff notified the assessors of the town that he was about to change his residence to Chicago. Evidence was also offered that immediately after reaching Chicago the 

---

plaintiff consulted two witnesses as to the proper measures to be taken in establishing a residence. The court declared that the decision in the lower court was correct and that the evidence alluded to was properly admitted. 

Waterman v. Whitney, 11 N. Y. 157 (1854), Thayer, 602.

In a will contest, in which mental capacity of the testator was the chief question at issue, witnesses were offered who would testify that after the execution of the will the testator stated on various occasions that he had disposed of his property in a manner very different from the terms of the will. This testimony was objected to and excluded. The will was allowed. The Court of 

Appeals declared the exclusion prejudicial error and ordered a new trial. "These declarations," said the court, "were offered in connection with other evidence bearing upon the competency of the testator at and before the execution of the will. If evidence of the mental condition of the testator after the execution of the will is admissible in any case, as to his capacity when the will was executed, and the competency of such proof seems to be sustained by many authorities and contradicted by none; then it is clear that the testimony offered should have been admitted. . . . The object of the evidence is to show the mental state of the testator at the time when the will was executed. Of course, therefore, it is admissible only where it has a legitimate bearing upon that question; and of this the court must judge as in every other case where the relevancy of testimony is denied." 

Commonwealth v. Felch, 132 Mass. 22 (1882), Thayer, 628.

The defendant was on trial charged with an attempt to procure the miscarriage of Mary Finley from which

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

257 attempt she died. The defendant contended that the woman had performed the operation herself. A woman friend of the deceased was offered as a witness to testify that a short time before the fatal operation Mary told her that she was pregnant by one Edward Titcomb and that if Titcomb did not perform an operation to procure a miscarriage, or get some one to do so, she would perform the operation on herself with a leadpencil. Since it appeared that this declaration neither accompanied nor explained any act known to have been done by the deceased, it was declared by the presiding judge to be incompetent. The defendant was convicted and excepted to the exclusion of the evidence above indicated. The Supreme Court declared; "There is no claim that the evidence is admissible under other specific exception [the court had been discussing pedigree] to the rule excluding hearsay. There is no pretence that it was a dying declaration, so as to make it necessary to consider the principles upon which such declarations are admissible. It accompanied no act. 

It gave character to no transaction. There existed no one of the circumstances which sometimes in law are deemed a sanction equivalent to the ordinary sanction of an oath. It is mere recital." 

Commonwealth v. Trefethen, 157 Mass. 180 (1892), Thayer, 637. 

The defendant was on trial for murder of Deltena J. 

Davis by drowning. There was evidence that on the night of her death the girl left her home to meet the defendant and was never seen again. Evidence of the defendant's conduct and replies upon questioning were introduced against him. He offered in defence a "trance

medium" who was ready to testify that on the day prior to her death Miss Davis called upon her for advice. She was alleged to have stated that she was five months along in pregnancy and was intending to drown herself. This evidence was excluded. The body of the girl when found bore no marks of violence nor did her clothing indicate a struggle. The defendant was found guilty of murder but the Supreme Court granted a new trial because of the exclusion of the testimony of the trance medium. "The counsel for the defendant," said the Court, "concede that the declaration in this case is not, under our decisions, admissible as a part of what has been called the res gestae, although some courts have admitted similar declarations on this ground, it must accompany an act which directly or indirectly is relevant to the issue to be tried, and must in some way qualify, explain, or characterize that act, and be in a legal sense a part of it. . . . They contend that the declaration is some evidence of the state of mind or intention of the deceased at the time she made it; that the intention which it tends to prove is a material fact, which, in connection with other facts proved, tends to support the theory of suicide. . . . The person making the declaration, if one was made, is dead; she had an opportunity to commit suicide, and it was competent for the jury to find that she had a motive to commit it; and the declaration, if made, was made under circumstances which exclude any suspicion of an intention to make evidence to be used at the trial.” 

Insurance Co. v. Mosley, 8 Wall (U. S.), 397 (1869),

Thayer, 657. 

Arthur H. Mosley took out a policy of insurance against loss of life or personal injury by accident. His

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

259 widow, the beneficiary, sued upon the policy and alleged that her husband had met his death by accident. She testified that at about midnight July 18, 1866, her husband left his bed, "that when he came back he said he had fallen down the backstairs, and almost killed himself; that he had hit the back part of his head in falling downstairs; . . . that his voice trembled; he complained of his head and appeared to be faint and in great pain."

The son also testified that he saw his father after the alleged accident and received the same explanation. The injured man died several days later but the insurance. company declared that death was due to natural causes. 

After a verdict for the plaintiff the defendant appealed the case because of the admission of the statements of the deceased as to his bodily injuries and pain. The court discussed the question of whether it was error to admit the declarations as to his bodily injuries or to prove that he had fallen downstairs. "It is to be remarked," said the court, "that the declarations of the former class all related to present existing facts at the time they were made. Those of the latter class were made immediately, or very soon after the fall; the declarations to his son, before he returned to his bedroom; those to his wife upon his reaching there. Wherever the bodily or mental feelings of an individual are material to be proved, the usual expressions of such feelings are original and competent evidence. Those expressions are the natural reflexes of what it might be impossible to show by any other testimony. . . . Here the principal fact is the bodily injury. The res gestae are the statements of the cause made by the assured almost contemporaneously with its occurrence, and those relating to the consequences made while the latter subsisted and were in progress. Where sickness or affec-

tion is the subject of inquiry, the sickness or affection is the principal fact. The res gestae are the declarations tending to show the reality of its existence, and its extent and character." The judgment was affirmed. 

Waldele v. New York R. R., 95 N. Y. 274 (1884), Thayer, 661. 

This action was brought to recover damages for the death of John E. Waldele, alleged to have been caused by the negligence of the defendant. Waldele was a deaf mute, intelligent and in possession of all faculties except of speech and hearing. He was fatally injured at a railroad crossing. No one saw the accident. The injured man lived for three hours after the accident. His brother who was also a deaf mute communicated with him by sign language and was produced as a witness. 

The defendant objected that the declarations were incompetent; not a part of the res gestae; that they were made subsequent to the injury at a place other than that where the injury occurred. The court overruled the objections and the witness answered “John said there was a long train, that he stood waiting for it to go, and the engine followed and struck him." After a verdict for the plaintiff the case was taken to the Court of Appeals on the ground of improperly admitted evidence. The court reversed the decision, speaking in part as follows: "The res gesta, speaking generally, was the accident. These declarations were no part of that, —were not made at the same time, or so nearly contemporaneous with it as to characterize it, or throw any light upon it. They are purely narrative, giving an account of a transaction not partly past, but wholly past and completed. They depend for their truth wholly

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

261 upon the accuracy and reliability of the deceased, and the veracity of the witness who testified to them.. 

Even dying declarations are not received in a civil court unless part of the res gesta. Such declarations made in the immediate presence of death, under the most solemn circumstances, when all motive to pervert the truth may be supposed to have ceased to operate, are received only in trials for homicide of the declarant in cases where the death of the deceased is the subject of the charge." 

Vicksburg R. R. v. O'Brien, 119 U. S. 99 (1886), Thayer. 

667. 

This action was brought to recover damages for injuries sustained by the plaintiff in a railroad accident. 

The careless and negligent operation of the train was one of the plaintiff's contentions. In proof of this he introduced a witness who, against defendant's objections, was permitted to testify to a conversation with the conductor of the train "between ten and thirty minutes after the accident," relative to the speed at which the train was travelling at the time of the accident. After a verdict for the plaintiff the defendant took the case to the Supreme Court. The decision was there reversed and a new trial granted but a strong dissenting opinion (by four justices) was filed. Justice 

Harlan who delivered the majority opinion declared: "His declaration, after the accident had become a completed fact, and when he was not performing his duties of engineer, that the train, at the moment the plaintiff was injured, was being run at the rate of eighteen miles an hour, was not explanatory of anything in which the injuries in question arose. It was, in its essence, the mere narration of a past occurrence, not a part of the

1*310 res gastae, simply an assertion or representation, in the course of conversation, as to a matter not then pending, and in respect to which his authority as engineer had been fully exerted. It is not to be deemed part of the res gestae, simply because of the brief period intervening between the accident and the making of the declaration. The fact remains that the occurrence had ended when the declaration in question was made." 

§ 132. Declarations in Rape Cases. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 133. Statements Made After Regaining Consciousness. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 134. Declarations Against Interest of Persons Since 

Deceased. 

Framingham Mfg. Co. v. Barnard, 2 Pick. (Mass.), 532 (1824), Thayer, 500.

In an action to recover the value of goods shipped to the defendants in North Carolina for sale the defendants sought to prove that they had used due diligence in disposing of the goods and in endeavoring to collect, as agents of the plaintiff. They offered in evidence the letters of one King, now deceased, to whom they had sent the goods and who had sold them. The trial court rejected the letters. It was urged by counsel for the defendants that King, if living, would be a competent witness and that now, his letters were the best evidence and admissible as declarations against interest. The 

Supreme Court refused to accept this view. It admitted

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

263 that entries against interest in a book of account would be admissible. "The case of verbal declarations or of letters," said the court, "is totally different, the first being easily misapprehended and misrepresented, and the second being too easily fabricated, to make them safe sources of evidence. The entries in books come very near to facts proving themselves, in the nature of records, and where they go to charge the party making them, at a time when there could be no view to any use of them to prove a title, they deserve much credit.

No case has been cited in which letters of a person employed by an agent, have been received in evidence to prove diligence or faithfulness in the agent; which was the object of introducing this evidence. We are therefore of opinion that the letters, even if sufficiently proved, of which there is some doubt manifested in the bill of exceptions, were properly rejected." 

Taylor v. Witham, 3 Ch. D. 605 (1876), Thayer, 497. 

William Taylor by his will made in September, 1872, gave several legacies to members of his own family and also a legacy for £2000 to Witham who had married his wife's niece. Taylor died in July, 1875. Joseph

Taylor was one of the executors of the will. A controversy arose between Taylor and Witham as as to whether the latter had not already received his legacy. 

It was proven that in December, 1871, the testator had advanced to Witham £2000. Witham claimed that it was a marriage settlement upon his wife. The testator's account book was offered as evidence that the advance to Witham was a loan. On October 1, 1872, the testator had recorded the payment by Witham of £20., "interest for three months." In another place there was

a record of the loan and of various interest payments amounting to £80. It appeared also that December 

27, 1872, Witham "paid off £20. Left £1980." In deciding in favor of admissibility the court said: "Why should a man enter in his book 'Interest paid me' intending to make an entry for himself and not against himself? Obviously the natural meaning of it is that it is against himself. 'Interest' standing alone would not help him; but these entries appear to be connected with two other entries: 'December 27. Paid off £20'; that is against his interest, but following that there is this, 'Left £1980' and that enables you to carry your eye to the connecting entry, which is 'Jan., 1872. J. Witham acknowledged loan to this date £2000'." 

Middleton v. Melton, 10 B. & C. 317 (1829), Thayer, 485.

This was an action against a surety on a bond given by Squire, a deceased collector of taxes, for the faithful performance of his duties. It appeared that a duplicate assessment list had been delivered to Squire and that it was his custom to check off on this list from time to time the sums collected from the individuals whose names appeared thereon. According to the record thus made the account was balanced at the time of his death. 

But immediately after his death a private book in which he had copied the assessment list and which he had used in collecting was checked up by two witnesses and found to total £996 more than Squire had indicated on the official list or which he had accounted for. This book was given to the defendant, surety on the bond, by the defendant's daughter. He refused to produce it at the trial. The real issue in the trial was whether this book was admissible as evidence against the defendant.

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

265 He urged that the persons who were alleged to have paid taxes not accounted for should be called. The 

Court of King's Bench declared that secondary evidence of the contents of the book was properly received. The court also held that: "Where a person makes an entry charging himself with the receipt of a sum of money, that entry is evidence of the fact of the receipt of that money against a third person." 

Davies v. Humphreys, 6 M. & W. 153 (1840), Thayer, 490.

The plaintiff, one of the makers of a joint and several note for £300., had been obliged to pay the entire note. 

He brought suits against his co-makers. In a second suit against Evan Humphreys, in which he was seeking complete reimbursement, Humphreys defended on the ground that there was no evidence that the plaintiff had acted merely as a surety on the note. To meet this objection the plaintiff relied upon a receipt, indorsed on the back of the note by the payee (since deceased) acknowledging the payment by the plaintiff of £280, on account of the £300, "the £300 having originally been advanced to Evan Humphreys." The admissibility of this receipt was the chief question carried up to the Court of Exchequer. The court declared it admissible saying: "The entry of a payment against the interest of the party making it, has been held to have the effect of proving the truth of other statements contained in the same entry, and connected with it. . . . The effect of the evidence was for the jury, to whom the question was properly left on this and the parol testimony in the cause, whether he was the principal debtor or not; and no fault is found with their verdict." ...

Roe d. Brune v. Rawlings, 7 East 279 (1806), Thayer, 476.

Action of ejectment for certain lands in Cornwall. 

The lessor of plaintiff produced a deed of settlement of 1728 whereby the lands in question were settled upon Edmund Prideaux, the elder, for life, remainder to 

Edmund Prideaux, the younger, for life, remainder to 

Humphrey Prideaux, (eldest son of E. P. the younger) remainder to his first and other sons in tail male. The lessor of the plaintiff was the first tenant in tail under that settlement. Prideaux the elder died in 1728, Prideaux the younger died in 1745 and Humphrey died in 1793. The eldest son of the latter leased the premises to the plaintiff. The settlement of 1728 had contained a provision whereby the tenant for life could lease for any term not exceeding 21 years or for the life or lives of any one two or three person or persons "so as the ancient yearly rent or a proportionable part thereof, be reserved." The defendant

The defendant claimed under a lease executed to him in 1778 for Henry Prideaux, the last tenant for life. This lease was for 99 years determinable on three lives (still extant) at a yearly rent of 14S. 

This lease was claimed by the present tenant in tail to be void because the ancient yearly rent was not reserved. This rent he claimed was £14 and not 14s. 

To prove this ancient rent a writing was produced in court which had been found among other papers of the estate. The writing was in the form of a letter dated 

January, 1728, from one Hobart to Edmund Prideaux the younger, in which Hobart enumerated the names of tenants and the amount of rent paid by each. Prideaux had indorsed on the outside the date when the letter was received and also "From Hobart, a particular of my estate in Cornwall." The defendant claimed that this letter was no more than hearsay and that the leases

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

267 ought to be produced. He further claimed that such statement could not be binding upon the successor in title of Edmund. The letter and memoranda was rejected by the trial court. A new trial was granted by the court of King's Bench. "The contents of the paper," said the court, "were adverse to the title of the person who had possession of it; it diminished his interest in the fine or renewal, in the same proportion as it raised the rent to be reserved. . . . The paper, therefore, having been found among the muniments of the family, accredited by one who could not have used it in his own favor, and preserved by him and by the succeeding tenant for life, against whom it might have been used adversely; we think that it was evidence to be left to the jury of the amount of the ancient rent at the time it bears date, and upon the same grounds we think that the entries in the book of the receipt of rent made by the same person were also evidence of the fact, which ought to have been submitted to the jury." 

Mahaska v. Ingalls, 16 Iowa 81 (1864), Thayer, 502.

This action was brought against the executor of Shoemake and the sureties on his official bond as treasurer of the County of Makaska. It was found that 

Shoemake was a defaulter, during his entire term of office in the sum of $3,415.44. There was a verdict in favor of the county for $715.44 and both parties appealed. The ground of appeal was improperly received evidence. John White testified that in the summer of 

1858 (prior to the execution of the bond being sued upon) he had been requested by Shoemake to advance him $2000. The latter stated that he was much behind in his account with the county. The negotiations fell

through and nothing was done. Another witness testified that he was approached by Shoemake during the summer of 1858 and that the latter had told him that he was short in his accounts to the amount of $2700. 

The court made an exhaustive review of the law and declared the evidence admissible. It laid down four tests for evidence of this type: (1) The declarant must be dead; (2) the declaration must be against the pecuniary interest of the declarant at the time; (3) The declaration must be of a fact or facts in relation to a matter concerning which the declarant was personally and immediately cognizable; (4) The court must be satisfied that there was no motive to falsify the fact declared and that the same was made at a period so remote as to preclude all suspicion that it was manufactured for the occasion. 

§ 135. What Must Be Proved. 

Aveson v. Lord Kinnaird, et als., 6 East 188 (1805), Thayer, 587. 

On November 9, 1802, the plaintiff took out a policy of insurance on the life of his wife for £1500. She died on April 29, 1803. When sued for the amount the defendant alleged deceit and that the wife was in precarious health from "a settled habit of immoderate drinking.' The surgeon who examined her testified that there was nothing in her appearance to indicate ill health and that her replies were very satisfactory. The defendant introduced a witness, Susannah Lees, an intimate acquaintance of Mrs. Aveson. She testified that a few days after the insurance was applied for she called on Mrs. Aveson and found her still abed, although it was nearly noon. "Mrs. Averson then said,"

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

269 declared the witness, "she was very poorly; that she had been to Manchester the Tuesday before, and that her husband had been insuring her life; that she was not well when she went. And she spoke in a faint way."

The defendant objected to the admission of this testimony but Lord Ellenborough, C. J., declared, "The question being, what was the state of her health at a certain period, a witness has been received to relate that which has always been received from patients to explain, her own account of the cause of her being found in bed at an unseasonable hour with the appearance of being ill. . . . The declaration was upon the subject of her own health at the time, which is a fact of which her own declaration is evidence; and that, too, made unawares before she could contrive any answer for her own advantage and that of her husband." 

Chambers v. Bernasconi, 1 C. & J. 451 (1831), Thayer, 546.

In an action by the plaintiff to try the validity of a commission of bankruptcy against him, the place where the alleged act of bankruptcy occurred became important. This question depended upon where the arrest of the plaintiff on November 9, 1825, had taken place. The plaintiff claimed that he was arrested in South Moulton street and the defendants (his assignees in bankruptcy) alleged that he was arrested in Paddington. The defendants produced the assistant to one Wright, the late sheriff, who testified that on the day in question he and Wright went to the plaintiff's house in Paddington and were told that he was not at home. They searched the house, found him and placed him under arrest. The plaintiff called the deputy under-sheriff who produced a certificate signed by Wright. "9th November, 1825.

Arrested Abraham Henry Chambers the elder, in South 

Moulton street, at the suit of William Brereton. (signed) Thomas Wright." The witness testified that officers were accustomed to state in the certificate the place of arrest. The Court of Exchequer declared the certificate inadmissible. "Now I cannot see," said the court, "how the statement in the present case was against the interest of the officer. He had a duty to perform and the effect of what he says is only this: ""I have done my duty.' I think therefore that the statement in question does not fall within the principle upon which the class of cases which has been referred to were decided." 

Carr v. Dighton, 229 Mass. 304 (1918).

This action was brought against the town of Dighton by three minors (by their next friend) who alleged that they had been unlawfully excluded from school.

It appeared that the children were sent home by the school physician because they were declared to be afflicted with head lice. They were given instructions how to rid themselves of the pests but when they returned to school after four weeks' absence they were again condemned for the same reason. The father of the children demanded a hearing before the school committee but the committee declined to grant the request. At the trial a certificate by a physician, since deceased, was offered to prove that at the time of the exclusion the children were free from head lice. After a verdict for the plaintiff the defendant alleged exceptions. The admission of the certificate was one of the grounds of exception. The Supreme Court held that the statement was admissible because "it was a declara-

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

271 tion of a deceased person made before the commencement of the action and upon the personal knowledge of the declarant.” 

§ 136. Exceptions-Admissions Against Interest by 

Litigants. 

Reed v. McCord, 18 N. Y. Supreme Court, Appellate Div. 381 (1897), Thayer, 112.

At the coroner's inquest the defendant, McCord, who was not present at the accident testified that "for some reason or other, that day the dog was not in position and that caused the accident. The man who had charge of it supposed the dog was in position, and he released his hold on the thing, and it commenced to revolve, and then he got down, so as to put his foot on it, and it was going so rapid that it slipped past." At the trial this evidence was admitted against the defendant's protest. The Supreme Court held that the evidence was admissible. Said the court: "It is not necessary that admissions of a party to an action, in order to be evidence, should be of facts within the knowledge of the party making them. Such admissions do not come within the category of hearsay evidence. . . . The admission of the defendant was a 'statement adverse to his own interest;' and it was made under the solemnity of an oath in a legal proceeding instituted for the purpose of determining upon whom rested the responsibility of the accident, where it may reasonably be assumed that the defendant would be careful of admissions which would be injurious to his interests, and possibly form the basis of an action against him for recovery of the damages occasioned by the accident." ...

Moriarty v. London, Etc., Railway Co., L. R. 5 Q. B. 314 

(1870), Thayer, 114.

The plaintiff and his wife were suing for injuries sustained by the wife while a passenger on the train of the defendant company. Witnesses were permitted to testify that the plaintiff endeavored to persuade them to swear in court to falsehoods in order to assist in proving his case. They alleged that he offered them onethird of any verdict that he might receive. The Appellate Court held that the evidence was admissible "as evidence of an admission by the party against whom it is given that the case he was putting forward was not a true one. It was an admission by conduct and receivable on that ground. The effect of the evidence is entirely for the jury. No doubt there are cases, perhaps it frequently happens, in which a person has endeavored to bolster up a good case by fictitious evidence." 

Smith v. Palmer, 6 Cush. (Mass.), 513 (1850), Thayer, 116.

The defendant, Palmer, was a partner in a firm that was suing Smith in the courts of the State of Maine for an alleged indebtedness. During the pendency of the suit Smith made an agreement with Palmer by which, for a consideration paid by Smith, Palmer agreed to have the case discontinued and no further litigation brought. Notwithstanding this contract Palmer prosecuted the pending case to judgment and Smith was obliged to pay the same. At the trial of the plaintiff's suit against Palmer he was permitted, in proof of the court proceedings in Maine, to introduce "the oral declarations, confessions and admissions of the defendant in connection with a writing purporting to be

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

273 an execution issued in the said action, which execution contained an assignment written by the defendant. The defendant contended that the plaintiff should be required to produce a certified copy of judgment, or account for its non-production before being permitted to introduce secondary evidence. The Supreme Court declared that: "The general principle, as to the production of written evidence as the best evidence, does not apply to the admissions of parties; as what a party admits against himself may reasonably be taken to be true. In the present case the principal fact was, that the defendant had not performed his contract, in regard to which there could be no doubt his admissions would be important evidence, and the execution reciting the judgment assigned by the defendant himself was produced in connection with the admission." 

Woolway v. Rowe, 1 A. & E. 114 (1834), Thayer, 120.

Action of trespass for breaking and entering the plaintiff's close, spoiling the herbage, etc. The defendant claimed that the land in question was a common to be equally enjoyed by the parishioners. In proof of this contention he offered as a witness the son of a former proprietor or tenant of the estate now held by the plaintiff. The witness declared that his father had never claimed anything more than a right in common and that he had heard him say that "he had no right to enclose the down." The father was alive and present in court. The plaintiff objected that since the father himself might have been put on the stand, the evidence of his declarations was inadmissible. The judge ruled that the evidence was admissible. The jury found a verdict for the defendant. Lord Denman, C. J.

(of the court of King's Bench) declared: "The first question raised in this case was, whether the declarations of a person formerly interested in the estate now the plaintiff's were admissible in evidence, when the party himself might have been called. We think they were receivable on the ground of identity of interest. The fact of his being alive at the time of the trial, when perhaps his memory of facts was impaired, and when his interest was not the same, does not, in our opinion, affect the admissibility of those declarations which he formerly made on the subject of his own rights." 

Paige v. Cagwin, 7 Hill (N. Y.), 361 1843), Thayer, 122. 

Cagwin brought an action of assumpsit against Paige seeking to recover the amount of a promissory note made by Noble, Smith & Paige, whereby they jointly and severally promised to pay Van Dyke or bearer $400, with interest, thirty days after date. At the trial a question arose as to the right of Paige to introduce evidence of certain declarations or admissions alleged to have been made by Van Dyke while he was owner and possessor of the note. The circuit judge refused to admit the evidence. The transfer from Van Dyke to 

Cagwin was proved to have been absolute and for a full and bona fide consideration although after maturity of the note. The case went to court for correction of errors (composed of members of the state senate) the majority of whom held that the admissions of Van

Dyke could not be used to defeat or impair the rights of Cagwin. "Where the rule is applicable," said the court, "there must be, it is conceded, an identity of interest between the assignor and the assignee. That relation appears to me to be based on the fact that the

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

275 rights of the assignor continue and are represented by the assignee. Where a person become a purchaser of a chose in action or a chattel, for a valuable consideration, his rights are independent of the assignor and beyond his control." 

Robinson v. Robinson, 1 Sw. & Tr. 362 (1858), Thayer, 127.

Suit for dissolution of a marriage brought by the husband against his wife, by reason of her adultery with Dr. Lane, the co-respondent. Diaries written by 

Mrs. Robinson were put in evidence. A witness, who was later found to be utterly untrustworthy, testified to acts of improper familiarities between the accused pair. During the trial the court ruled that the uncorroborated admission of the wife could not be used to establish the criminality of Dr. Lane and ordered the case dismissed as to him. It was then objected that the acquittal of the co-respondent must be fatal to the case against the wife. But the court held that “a principle of so purely technical a nature should be confined to the cases in the criminal law in which it had hitherto been applied." The evidence against the wife was then considered and the case was dismissed. 

§ 137. Exceptions. Entries in Books of Account. 

Regina v. Worth, 4 Q. B. 132 (1843), Thayer, 555. 

In a controversy over an order of justices removing 

William Worsell and his wife and children, paupers, from the parish of Worth to the parish of Horne it became material to prove that Worsell had obtained a settlement by hiring for one year with Thomas Stone. 

For the purpose of rebutting this evidence the inhab-

itants of Worth called a daughter of the late Thomas Stone and offered to prove by her that she was in possession of the book of accounts of her father. In this book were entries showing that he hired Worsell for six months only and that he worked thereafter by the week but less than a year in all. The court rejected the evidence and the Court of Queen's Bench affirmed the decision. "In a case of this kind," said the court, "the entry must be made against the interest of the party who writes it, or made in the discharge of some duty for which he is responsible. This book here does not show any entry operating against the interest of the party. . . . Nor was this an entry made in the course of duty.... It was argued that we might inquire whether a reasonable probability appeared that the entries were true, and that for this purpose we might go into the contents of the book beyond the particular entry. But the question is, not what may be inferred from the other entries, but whether the particular entry, at the time when it was made, imported something contrary to the maker's interest." 

Brain v. Preece, 11 M. & W. 773 (1843), Thayer, 556.

This was an action brought to recover the sum of £4 15s for coal alleged to have been sold by the plaintiffs testator to the defendant. There was evidence that the coal had been delivered but no entries in the book to show that it had been paid for. The defendant produced witnesses who testified that they had seen the defendant pay for the coal when it was delivered to him at the mine. The testator's book was kept in the following manner. It was the duty of Harvey who worked in the coal pit to give notice to Yem, the fore-

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

277 man, of the coal which he sold. Yem could not wirte, but he dictated matter to be written in the book and Baldwin was the clerk who did the writing. Both Yem and 

Harvey were dead. The book was rejected as evidence and a verdict was found for the defendant. The court of exchequer declared that the book offered was not admissible in evidence. 

Vosburgh v. Thayer, 12 Johns. (N. Y.), 461 (1815), Thayer, 525. 

Thayer sued Vosburgh in the court below for butcher's meat furnished by him to Vosburgh and his family. It was proved, by several witnesses, that he had been in the daily practice of supplying them with meat during the period for which he claimed payment. 

It was proved, by some of those who had dealt with him, that he kept just and honest accounts. He then offered his books of account in evidence, it appearing that he had no clerk. The books were objected to but admitted in evidence. The Supreme Court held for the plaintiff. "The only point for our consideration," said the court, "is whether the evidence in support of the plaintiff's demand, in the court below, was admissible. . . . In a case like the present, it is believed that the usage and the necessity of admitting such proof has been so long sanctioned and felt in our courts of justice, that it is now too late to question the admissibility of it. The admission of books of account in evidence, under proper limitations and restrictions, is not calculated to excite alarm, or to produce injurious consequences... They ought not to be admitted where there are several charges, unless a foundation is first laid for their admission, by proving that the party had

no clerk, that some of the articles charged have been delivered, that the books produced are the account books of the party, and that he keeps fair and honest accounts, and this by those who have dealt and settled with him." 

Smith v. Reutz, 131 N. Y. 169 (1892), Thayer, 527.

This action was brought to recover moneys advanced and paid out by the plaintiff's testator for the defendant. The testator was a banker and it appeared that for several years he handled the defendant's financial affairs. Whenever her funds were insufficient to meet taxes and tradesmen's bills the testator had advanced cash to cover the need. In this way there had accrued a balance alleged to have been $3,744.75 which the plaintiff was seeking to recover. Some of these items were proven by independent evidence but there were others of which no proof could be found except the ledger itself. The main question arose on the admissibility of the ledger. The Supreme Court held that the ledger was not admissible as original evidence. 

After commenting on the shop book rule the court said: "But the same necessity does not exist in respect to cash transactions. They are usually evidenced by notes or writing or vouchers in the hands of the party paying or advancing the money. Moreover, entries of cash transactions could be fabricated with much greater safety; and with less chance of the fraud being discovered, than entries of goods sold and delivered or of services rendered. It would be unwise to extend the operation of the rule admitting a party's books in evidence beyond its present limits, as would be the case, we think, if books containing cash dealings were held to be competent."

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

279 

Cram v. Spear, 8 Ohio 494 (1838), Thayer, 529.

A statute provided that in actions or defences founded "on book accounts of not more than eighteen months' standing, in which is drawn in question the validity or amount of any such book accounts, the court or justice may, upon the trial of such action, examine the party under oath or affirmative, touching the validity of such account or accounts." The plaintiff relied upon this statute in offering himself as a witness to prove certain book accounts in a suit thereon. The defendant objected on the ground that $687.50 of the total claim of $872.50 was not properly book accounts. 

Three separate charges out of the seven were for cash. 

The trial court sustained the defendant's contention and the plaintiff was non-suited. The Supreme Court declared in part as follows: "In transactions among men, it is the ordinary custom to keep books, in which charges are made for goods and chattels sold, labor performed, or other services rendered. The merchant, the farmer, the mechanic, the professional man, all keep books of account. It could not have been the intention of the legislature to make any change in the business of the country, or to authorize charges to be made upon books for things which are not properly the subject of book accounts; and if any person shall make such charges upon his book, with a view to sustain them by his own oath, it can be viewed in no other light than as an attempted fraud upon the law. Money lent, or paid is not ordinarily charged upon book. The person lending or paying usually takes a note or receipt. . . . In the opinion of the court, it could not have been the intention of the legislature to admit this kind of testimony."

§ 138. Entries Must Be Contemporaneous. 

Faxon v. Hollis, 13 Mass. 427 (1816.) 

Action of assumpsit for work and labor. The plaintiff was a blacksmith. He offered an account book in evidence, as his original entry, to prove his account. 

From inspection it proved to be in ledger form. The plaintiff explained that he kept a slate in the shop, on which he set down all entries as they accrued. It was his custom to transcribe the entries from the slate to the book which was offered in evidence, erasing from the slate the entries aforesaid and beginning over again. 

He took oath to the accuracy of the transcription. The defendant objected to the admission of the ledger as a book of original entries. The book was admitted and the jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff. The Supreme Court refused to set aside the verdict. "The evidence admitted in the trial of this cause," said the court, “although of a dangerous nature, is necessary for the security of tradesmen and small dealers, who are generally unable to support clerks, on whose testimony they might establish their claims. . . . It is no objection to the book received in evidence in this case that it was kept in the ledger form; for such is the way in which ordinary mechanics, especially in the country, make their charges; having a separate page for each of their customers. Such a form, however, used by a shopkeeper, who is in the habit of making many entries in the course of a day, would be liable to more suspicion. 

The entries in this book may be considered original, although transcribed from a slate; the slate containing merely memoranda, and not being intended to be permanent."

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

281 

8139. Entries Must Have Been Made in the Course of 

Business. 

Levine v. Lancashire Ins. Co., 66 Minn. 138 (1896), Thayer, 533. 

In a suit against an insurance company to collect for losses sustained to merchandise the plaintiffs were permitted to introduce (a) an inventory of stock on hand January 1, 1893; (b) their journal and ledger kept in the regular course of business, purporting to contain an account of the amount and value of merchandise sold after the inventory and until December 13, 1893, the date of the fire. In connection therewith the plaintiffs also introduced the original invoices of goods bought between January 1 and December 13, 1893.

The defendant contended that the "shop book rule" did not extend to cases of this kind. The Supreme Court approved the action of the lower court and affirmed the judgment for the plaintiff. "Books of account," said the court, "or 'shop books,' as they are often called, are usually introduced in evidence only to prove services performed or articles delivered to the person named in the entries in the course of dealings between the parties creating the relation of debtor and creditor. 

But the competency of such evidence in cases like the present, and for the purpose for which these books were introduced, is fully recognized by the authorities. . 

This rule is founded on considerations of necessity, for ordinarily there is no other or better evidence in existence." 

Poole v. Dicas, 1 Bing. (N. C.) 649 (1835), Thayer, 552.

In an action on a bill of exchange, drawn by the defendant, accepted by Wheeler and indorsed by the

defendant to the plaintiff it became important to prove that the bill was duly protested for non payment. The bill became due Saturday, June 8, 1833, and on the same day was left with a notary to demand payment. A copy of the bill was made in a book kept by the notary for that purpose and Manning, one of his clerks, now dead, went out about seven in the evening to demand payment from Wheeler. In a short time Manning returned, and in the margin of the book containing the copy of the bill, wrote by the side of the copy of the bill "no effects." This entry was produced at the trial and 

Manning's handwriting was proved. Another clerk made a similar entry, from Manning's dictation, in a separate book which contained protests for non-payment. All this was done in the regular course of the notary's business. After a verdict for the plaintiff, the defendant moved to set aside the verdict on the ground of improper evidence. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence was admissible on the ground that it was an entry made at the time of the transaction, and made in the usual course and routine of business by a person who had no interest to misstate what had occurred. The entry being thus prima facie consistent with truth, there are many accompanying circumstances which tend to confirm its correctness; and the letter addressed to the holder on Monday to apprise him of the presentment and dishonor of the bill is a link in the chain which gives consistency to the whole." 

Welsh v. Barrett, 15 Mass. 380 (1819), Thayer, 559.

Action of assumpsit by the plaintiff as indorsee, against the defendant as indorser, of a promissory note.

The note was lodged with the State Bank for collection.

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

283 The plaintiff, being called upon to prove a demand upon Peter Dickerman, the maker, and reasonable notice to the defendant as indorser, stated that the messenger of the bank, who had delivered the usual printed notice, was dead. He proved by the cashier that pursuant to the rules of the bank, the said messenger had kept a record book concerning his doings with reference to notes left for collection. The book kept by the deceased messenger was produced and his handwriting was proved. Upon the entries therein contained the plaintiff relied for proof of the necessary legal formalities to fix the liability of the defendant. The book was objected to but the court held that it was clearly admissible. "What a man has actually done, and committed to writing," said the court, "when under obligation to do the act, it being in the course of the business he has undertaken, and he being dead; there seems to be no danger in submitting it to the jury.” 

Augusta v. Windsor, 19 Me. 317 (1841), Thayer, 567.

This was an action of assumpsit for the support of Absalom Howes and family as paupers, whose settlement was alleged to be in Windsor in consequence of his having his home and dwelling there in March, 1821. 

The defendants contended that Howes and his family resided in Pittston prior to March 1821 and that he was then in the employ of one Linscott. The date of this employment seems to have coincided with a time when Linscott was laid up with a broken leg. The plaintiff, against the defendant's objection, offered in evidence a day book of Dr. Neal (now deceased) who had attended

Linscott. This book indicated that the leg was set September 28, 1821. The entry was in Dr. Neal's hand-

writing. The jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendant alleged exceptions. The Supreme 

Court overruled the exceptions. The argument had been advanced that the entry was not admissible because there was no record of the charge having been paid and that it was consequently not against the interest of the person making it. The court adopted the language of an English judge that a memorandum "made at the time when the fact it records took place, by a person since deceased, in the ordinary course of his business, corroborated by other circumstances which render it probable that the fact occurred, is admissible in evidence." 

New York v. Second Ave. R. R., 102 N. Y. 572 (1886), Thayer, 583.

When the defendant company was given permission to lay tracks in the streets of the city it agreed to pave and keep in repair that portion of the streets affected by such tracks. Failing to do so, it became necessary for the city (after due notice) to pave a certain street. In suing the company for reimbursement, the question arose as to the admissibility of a time book kept by one Wilt in the following manner: Wilt visited the work twice a day. There were two gangs of ten men each. 

The foreman of each gang reported to him and he checked the time of each man as thus reported. He knew the men personally but was dependent upon the foremen for each man's time. Wilt testified to the exactness of his record and each gang foreman testified that they had given Wilt correct information. Against the defendant's objection the time book was admitted as evidence. The Court of Appeals declared that the time

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

285 book would not be admissible on the testimony of Wilt alone but when his testimony and that of the foremen were taken together it was admissible. "By combining the testimony of Wilt and the gang foremen," said the court, "there was, first, original evidence that laborers were employed, and that their time was correctly reported by persons who had personal knowledge of the facts, and that their reports were made in the ordinary course of business, and in accordance with the duty of the person making them, and in point of time were contemporaneous with the transactions to which the report related; and second, evidence by the person who received the reports, that he correctly entered them reported, in the time-book, in the usual course of his business and his duty." as 

§ 140. Authentication of Entries. 

Pratt v. White, 132 Mass. 477 (1882), Thayer, 518.

In an action contract upon an account annexed, the plaintiff offered in evidence the book of original entries kept by his intestate, with his own oath and that of the intestate's clerk. The defendant objected to its admissibility, on the ground that no measure, weight, or quantity was given in connection with the several items charged. The judge admitted the book and the jury returned a verdict for the plaintiff. The Supreme Court overruled the defendant's exceptions, saying: "It is for the court to decide upon the admissibility of the book offered, although the weight to be given to it afterwards must be largely a question for the jury, in connection with its appearance, the manner in which it is kept, and the other evidence in the case. It must appear to have been honestly kept, and not intentionally erased or altered, and to have been the record of the daily

DER/CRY 

business of the party, made for the purpose of establishing a charge against another. . . . There would seem to be no reason why a delivery of specific articles might not be shown by a book of accounts, even if more evidence were needed to show the amount which the plaintiff was entitled to recover." 

Dohmen Company v. Niagara Ins. Co., 96 Wis. 38 (1897), Thayer, 540.

In an action against an insurance company, one of the plaintiffs was permitted to testify as to the amount of goods on hand at the last inventory. With that as a starting point, and without verifying the correctness of the account books in any way, he was allowed, against objection, to testify to the amount of purchases from the time of such inventory to the time of the fire; also as to the amount of sales during such period, as recorded in the aforesaid books of account. The value of the goods when the fire occurred was then determined by adding to the amount disclosed by the inventory the amount of the subsequent purchases and deducting the sales as shown by the books. The Supreme Court declared that the evidence was improperly received. Said the court: "Here there was no proper foundation laid for the use of the books. They were merely produced as books of account kept in the business, and, without any verification whatever, the witnesses were allowed to testify respecting their contents. There is no rule with which we are familiar that warranted the admission of the evidence under the circumstances. It was prejudicial error, for which the judgment must be reversed." 

Guy v. Mead, 22 N. Y., 462 (1860), Thayer, 576. 

Action was brought on a promissory note, made by

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

287 the defendant July 13, 1843, and payable one day after date to George Sharts, or bearer, for $250. It was transferred to the plaintiff July 25, 1854, and this action was commenced immediately. The defence set up was payment to Sharts while he was the holder, and the Statute of Limitations. There was an indorsement of $90 

January 20, 1846, and of interest to April 1st, 1848, as well as one of $40 February 26, 1849. The defendant gave evidence that this $40 was paid and indorsed in February, 1847. The plaintiff introduced a witness, Ingersoll, who stated that at Sharts' request he had made a calculation in writing of the interest then due upon the note, making due allowance for the payments indorsed upon it. He produced the memorandum from which it appeared that the calculation was made April 1, 1848, and that no indorsement of $40 on the principal was then on the note. Ingersoll stated that he believed the memorandum to be accurate but that aside from what he had written he had no definite recollection as to the time when it was made. The court held that the paper was not then admissible because it did not state that the $40 was not then on the note. The Appellate Court held that inasmuch as the calculation made allowance for the first payment on the principal but did not for the second it might be regarded as evidence that no such payment had at that time been made on the note. The memorandum was declared admissible in connection with the testimony of the witness who made it. The judgment of the defendant was set aside. 

Holbrook v. Gay, 6 Cush. (Mass.), 215, (1850).

This was an action of assumpsit to recover the amount of an account annexed to the writ. For the purpose of proving a set-off a book of original entries,

kept by the defendant at the time the items declared upon had accrued, was offered in evidence. The entries in the book of accounts was in the handwriting of the defendant, who was now a lunatic under guardianship. 

The guardian offered to prove by his oath, that the book came into his hands as the genuine and only book of accounts of the defendant, and that to the best of his knowledge and belief entries therein were original and contemporaneous with the transactions and that the same were unpaid. The trial court refused to admit the proof offered in setoff. The Supreme Court declared that the trial court erred. "If a party is dead," said the court "his books may be offered by the executor or administrator, accompanied by a proper statement under oath, of the party offering them. . . . The same necessity which justifies the introduction of the books of the party, . . alike seems to require and justify the admission of them, where the party has become incapacitated to take the oath by reason of insanity." 

§ 141. Entries by Strangers. 

Hingham v. Ridgway, 10 East 109 (1808), Thayer, 480.

Action to reverse a recovery (an old method of conveying an estate tail) suffered by William Fowden the younger, of certain lands of which he was the first tenant in tail under a settlement of December, 1763 limiting to the "first son of the body of William Fowden (the father) with remainder to the second and other sons in tail, remainder to the daughters in tail. After the death of William Fowden, the younger, without issue his niece Elizabeth, the present plaintiff, brings this action. The basis of the action was that when 

William, her uncle, had suffered the recovery he was a

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

289 minor. The recovery itself William Fowden, the elder, swore that "Wm. Fowden the younger was born April 2, 1768, but being a Protestant dissenter, no entry was made of his baptism in any register." Since the recovery was acknowledged on April 15, 1789, it would appear that William was of full age. Mary contended however that William was born April 22, 1768. She offered as a witness a son of the man mid-wife who proved that his father had died twenty years before and produced his book of regular entries of all matters relating to his business. This book disclosed the entry that William 

Fowden, Junior, was born at 3.00 P. M., April 22, 1768.

It also contained an entry of the charge for services and the fact that the same was paid. This latter fact the court declared "repels the claim that he would otherwise have had against the father from the rest of the evidence as it now appears. Therefore the entry made by the party was to his own immediate prejudice, when he had not only no interest to make it, if it were not true, but he had an interest the other way, not to discharge a claim which it appears from other evidence he had. The evidence, therefore, in this case properly received." was 

Regina v. Birmingham, 1 Best & Son, 763 (1861), Thayer, 491.

Two justices of the peace for the borough of Birmingham made an order for the removal of the wife and four children of William Day (who had absconded) from the parish of Birmingham to the parish of Kingswood, from which order the latter parish appealed. The evidence relied upon in support of the order was that John L. Day, father of William, had occupied a tene-

ment for eighteen years, prior to his death in 1847, in the parish of Kingswood and that he had told a witness that he was occupying as a tenant at £20 per year. 

The admissibility of this evidence was the chief question in dispute. In deciding that the evidence was admissible the court reasoned thus: "It is now settled that a declaration against interest by a deceased person is, with certain limitations, admissible evidence in proceedings between strangers. There

There are numerous cases which show that where a person is in possession of real property, which possession is prima facie evidence of a tenancy in fee, any statement that he makes to cut down that interest is admissible in evidence after his decease, as being a statement against his interest." 

Cooper v. Marsden, 1 Esp. 1 (1793), Thayer, 544.

Action of assumpsit for money lent and advanced. 

The defence was that the loan had been paid. To prove payment of a draft at a banker's, a clerk of the banking house produced one of the books belonging to the house in which payment of the draft was entered. He was asked if the entry was in his own handwriting. He explained that it was the handwriting of a clerk who was now in the East Indies and not likely to return. He took oath to the genuineness of the handwriting. The plaintiff objected to the receipt of the evidence, but the defendant's counsel argued that the case was analogous to that of a witness to any deed, bond or instrument in writing; in which case if the subscribing witness was abroad proof of his handwriting was sufficient. The court ruled that the evidence was inadmissible saying: "Entries in books of bankers or persons keeping books respecting their trade or business, could only be proved

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

291 by the clerks who made the entries, inasmuch as they might give some material evidence, independent of the mere entry in the books, from having some acquaintance with the dealings upon which the entries were founded." 

Pritt v. Fairclough, 3 Camp. 305 (1812), Thayer, 545.

This was an action for goods sent out by the plaintiffs from London to be sold on commission by the defendants in the West Indies. Defendants were notified to produce all the letters written to them by the plaintiffs. A letter was placed in evidence, written by the defendants to the plaintiffs, acknowledging the receipt of a letter from them dated December 18, 1807. 

This letter was alleged to have contained an invoice of the goods in controversy. The defendant not having produced the original, the plaintiff offered secondary evidence of its contents. The clerk who had charge of the letter book at the time the letter was written was now dead but they produced proof of his handwriting and of their custom to have all business letters copied before being mailed out. Testimony was offered that the deceased clerk was accurate and punctual in his duty. The question was whether the entry in the letter book in the handwriting of the deceased clerk could be received as evidence of the contents of the letter. The court said that unless such evidence were permitted the most careful of merchants could not prove the contents of a letter after the death of an entering clerk. The letter was admitted and a verdict was found for the plaintiff.
