§ 142. Exceptions-Ancient Documents. 

Whitman v. Sham, 166 Mass. 451 (1896), Thayer, 457.

Two writs of entry were brought to recover possession of two parcels of land in that part of Boston, formerly Brookline and lying on the southeasterly side of Brookline Avenue. A controversy arose over the admission in evidence of a plan and field notes alleged to have been made by Mather Withington in 1818 and to certain deeds supposed to refer to this plan. There was evidence from a date in the field notes that the survey was made in July, 1818. The plan appeared by evidence to be well known to surveyors, and to have been used and tested by them in their own surveys. 

The court said: "We are of opinion that no error is shown in the admission of the preliminary evidence;

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

293 and that on this evidence, which comprised much testimony in addition to that specifically set forth in this opinion, it was competent for the justice who presided at the trial to find that the plan and field notes were not only ancient, but also genuine and accurate, and prepared for use in actual transactions. The plan was therefore properly admitted in evidence, and we see no reason why the field notes were not also admissible. There was expert evidence that, taking the field notes in connection with the plan, the line of the White marsh included both lots. The presiding justice was therefore, so far as this part of the case is concerned, warranted in finding for the demandants." 

Harlan v. Howard, 79 Ky. 373 (1881), Thayer, 774.

In a controversy over the title to certain lands the appellants offered in evidence a document purporting to be a deed from Keith (original owner under a land patent) to the appellants' supposed ancestor bearing the date of October 24, 1794, offering the same as an ancient document. The appellees objected and evidence was introduced that the document was seen by witnesses thirty years prior to the trial and several times subsequently in the possession of appellant's lawyer. Despite this testimony the court refused to permit the deed to be read to the jury. The Supreme Court declared this to be prejudicial error, saying: "Where such instruments are over thirty years old, unblemished by any alterations, and are produced by those whose custody affords a reasonable presumption of their genuineness, they are said to prove themselves, the witness to the fact of execution being dead. It has been settled by the weight of authority that ancient deeds of convey-

ance of real estate are admissible without first requiring the party offering them to show acts of possession over the lands embraced by them. For until the court is made acquainted with the tenor of the instrument, the natural order of introducing the evidence would be reversed by requiring proof of corresponding possession." 

§ 143. Production From Proper Custody. 

Blandy-Jenkins v. Dunraven, 2 Ch. 121 (1899), Thayer, 455.

This was an action to restrain alleged acts of trespass on uninclosed lands in Wales. The admissibility of a document dated 1659 was the chief question before the chancery court. In declaring the document admissible the court said: "It purports to show, and does show, that a person named Jenkins had been persuaded to stop some proceedings which he had taken against a man named Morgan for trespassing on what Jenkins called his freehold by bringing sheep and cattle there. It shows upon the face of it that Jenkins must have been in possession, or else he would not have brought an action of trespass. To my mind it is evidence of an act of ownership: I do not say it is an act of ownership. Then the question arises, what custody does it come from? It comes from the muniments of a successor in title to a man named Jenkins. So that the custody in which is found is the place where one would expect such a document as this to be kept-that is to say, it is kept by those who are interested in the land, the possession of which was vindicated by Jenkins. It comes out of the proper custody. . . . 'That rule is, that ancient documents coming out of proper custody'-we have that here and purporting upon the face of them to show

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

295 exercise of ownership, such as a lease or license, may be given in evidence without proof of possession or payment of rent under them as being in themselves acts of ownership and proof of possession.'" 

§ 144. The Document Must be Fair on its Face. 

(See textbook.) 

$145. Public Documents. 

Sturla v. Freccia, 5 App. Cas. 623 (1880), Thayer, 429.

This was an appeal to the House of Lords involving the following facts. Mrs. Mangini Brown died intestate in London, leaving a personal estate of about £200,000. 

She was a widow about ninety-three years of age, the daughter of Antonio Mangini. The estate was claimed by persons of the Freccia family as next of kin. Mangini had been consul in London for the Ligurian Republic. 

The Freccia claim was investigated and it was decided that Antonio Mangini was a native of the village of St. 

Ilario and was born in 1735. The Freccias were held to be next of kin. Before the decision could be acted upon the Sturla family filed a claim as next of kin. To support their claim they presented a certified copy of a report made to the government of Liguria by those who investigated Mangini's application for appointment as consul in 1790. This report stated that Mangini was a native of Quarto, aged about forty-five, which would place his birth ten years later than Mangini of St. Iario. 

Of this Mangini the Sturla family claimed to be next of kin. Their claim, however, was rejected because the report of 1790 was not a public record, and hence not admissible. A public record, the court declared, was a

record open to inspection but this report was confidential and never intended as a public document. “I understand a public document there to mean," said the court, "a document that is made for the purpose of the public making use of it, and being able to refer to it." 

Oakes v. United States, 174 U. S. 778 (1899), Thayer, 435.

In an appeal from the court of claims it became necessary to prove whether a steamboat, belonging to a citizen of the United States, had been obtained by the 

Confederate government by capture or purchase. Extracts from the Confederate archives in the possession of the United States were offered and the matter of their admissibility was taken to the Supreme Court. The court said: "The government of the Confederate States, although in no sense a government de jure, and never recognized by the United States as in all respects a government de facto, yet was an organized and actual government, maintained by military power. . . . . . . For the collection and preservation of such papers, a bureau, office, or division in the War Department (now known as the Confederate Archives office), was created by the executive authority of the United States soon after the close of the war of the Rebellion, and has been maintained ever since, and has been recognized by many acts of congress. . . . It would be an anomalous condition of things if records of this kind, collected and preserved by the government of the United States in a public office at great expense, were wholly inadmissible in a court of justice to show facts of which they afford the most distinct and appropriate evidence, and which, in the nature of things, can hardly be satisfactorily proved in any other manner."

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

297 

Evanston v. Gunn, 99 U. S. 660 (1878), Thayer, 435.

Jessie Gunn sued the village of Evanston for damages which she had sustained by reason of the alleged neglect of duty by the defendant. For the purpose of showing the velocity of the wind and the falling of the snow on the morning in question the plaintiff offered in evidence the record made by the U. S. Signal Service in the nearby city of Chicago where conditions were admitted to have been similar. The jury brought in a verdict for $6500 and the defendant removed the case to the U. S. Supreme Court upon a writ of error. The court declared: "Extreme accuracy in all such observations and in recording them is demanded by the rules of the Signal Service, and it is indispensible, in order that they may answer the purpose for which they are required. They are, as we have seen, of a public character, kept for public purposes, and so immediately before the eyes of the community that inaccuracies, if they should exist, could hardly escape exposure. They come, therefore, within the rule which admits in evidence 'official registers of records kept by persons in public office in which they are required, either by statute or by the nature of their office, to write down particular transactions occurring in the course of their public duties or under their personal observation." 

Kennedy v. Doyle, 10 Allen (Mass.), 161 (1856), Thayer, 437. 

This action was brought against two sisters upon an agreement by both to pay money borrowed on their joint account from the plaintiff. One sister pleaded her insolvency but the other alleged infancy at the time of the agreement. In order to prove that the second de- 1

اری اور کسی مردانه است fendant was of age the plaintiff offered a baptismal record of the Roman Catholic church of Lowell, regularly kept by a priest now deceased and produced from the custody of his successor. It contained the following entry in the handwriting of the deceased clergyman: "1837, December 17th. Baptised Joanna, born 12th, of 

Michael and Mary Doyle." The Joanna named in the record was proved to be the second defendant. The memorandum was objected to but was duly admitted as proof of the date of baptism only. On appeal, the Supreme Court, after reviewing prior decisions and the statutes as to public records of births, declared the evidence inadmissible. "Our law not requiring any record of baptisms," said the court, "the church book offered in evidence in this case, not having been kept under any requirement of law, was not a public record, and would not, had the priest who made the entries been still alive, have been admissible in evidence, unsupported by his testimony." 

Gregg v. Learned, 109 Mass. 167 (1872), Thayer, 776.

This was a writ of entry to recover land in Brighton. 

At the trial the tenant offered in evidence an office copy of an instrument purporting to be a deed of the demanded premises from the demandant to one Miller executed March 25, 1847. It was proved that the original had never been in the possession of the tenant. The deed purported to have been duly delivered and it bore a certificate that it had been recorded. The demandant objected to its admission without further evidence of its delivery than what appeared upon its face. The court ruled that the certificate was sufficient evidence to justify the jury in inferring delivery. The Supreme

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

299 Court affirmed the decision. A part of the decision read as follows: "In none of these cases in which such a copy has been admitted in evidence as it been suggested that any further proof of delivery was necessary, when it did not appear that the deed had remained in the possession of the register or had been delivered back to the grantor. It follows that in the present case the copy from the registry was rightly admitted as prima facie evidence of the delivery as well as of the execution of the deed."

§ 148. Degree of Relationship. 

Hitchins v. Eardley, L. R. 2 P. & D. 248 (1871), Thayer, 384.

The plaintiffs claimed administration of the estate of Mary Adams as lawful second cousins and two of the next of kin of the deceased. The defendants claimed as cousins and sole next of kin and claimed that they were the lawful children of Murhall Daniel, a first cousin of the deceased. In order to maintain their claim the defendants sought to introduce oral and written declarations made by their said father. In discussing the admissibility of this evidence the Appellate Court said: "It is obvious that the application of this rule must lead to some practical difficulties, where the person whose declarations are tendered and objected to is also the

person whose legitimacy is the question in the suit, and the court must do its best to meet those difficulties in a practical way. . . . It is impossible to lay down an abstract rule on the subject, for each case must be determined by its own facts. There is some degree of evidence in the case as it now stands that the declarant was a member of the family. . I rule that I am sufficiently satisfied of the declarant being a member of the family for the purpose of admitting the declarations." The jury found for the defendants. .. • 

Monkton v. Attorney General, 2 Russ. & M., 147 (1831), Thayer, 386.

An application was made by persons claiming to be next of kin to Samuel Troutbeck, deceased, for an issue to try this question in a court of law. The chancellor rejected a certain genealogical narrative and pedigree of the Troutbeck family in the handwriting of John Troutbeck, deceased, purporting that the writer's father and the testator were first cousins. The principal point in dispute was the relationship of two individuals of the names of Samuel and George Troutbeck. John was clearly proved to have been related to George but the declarations were objected to because the relationship of John to Samuel Troutbeck had not been established. 

The court said: "I entirely agree, that, in order to admit hearsay evidence in pedigree you must, by evidence dehors the declarations, connect the person making them with the family. But I cannot go to the length of holding, that you must prove him to be connected with both the branches of the family, touching which his declaration is tendered. That he is connected with the family is sufficient; and that connection once

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

301 proved, his declarations are then let in upon questions touching that family; not declarations of detail which would not be evidence . . . but declarations in the nature of pedigree." 

Crispin v. Doglioni, 3 Sw. & Tr. 44 (1863), Thayer, 396.

The plaintiff alleged that he was the natural son of 

Henry Crispira, deceased, a domiciled Portuguese, and that by the law of Portugal he was entitled to succeed to the whole of the property of the deceased if he died intestate and two-thirds if he died testate. The defendant, a sister of the deceased, denied among other pleas, that the plaintiff was a natural son of the deceased. The Queen's Advocate tendered as evidence for the plaintiff the declarations of Jose Crispin, a deceased brother of Henry Crispin as to the relation between the plaintiff and Henry Crispin. Sir C. Cresswell: "As the question is to be decided on principle, I certainly think the evidence is inadmissible. The admissibility of hearsay evidence is exceptional, and ought not to be carried further than the decisions in the books, for it is a departure from the first rule of evidence. I can well understand that where a matter is likely to be discussed and well known in a family, a member of the family may be allowed to give evidence of it; but in this case the plaintiff, according to his own account, is filius nullius by our law. The question is whether a declaration of one brother may be admitted as to another brother having had intercourse with a woman, and having had a child by her; I think it ought to be excluded." 

Northrop v. Hale, 76 Me. 306 (1884), Thayer, 396. "This was an appeal from the decree of a judge of probate wherein he ordered a distribution of an intestate

estate and adjudged, against the claim of the appellant, that he was not the natural son of the intestate, but that he was the legitimate son of the intestate's sister." 

It appeared from the evidence that the appellant was born in Ohio and was brought up in the family of the intestate's sister, in which also the intestate resided at the time of the appellant's birth and for several years thereafter. The appellant tendered a declaration of 

Mary Northrop (the intestate's sister) relative to the birth and parentage of the appellant. This declaration was excluded. The Supreme Court held that the Probate Court erred in excluding the statement. Said the court: "Can her declarations be admitted to show the illegitimacy of her unmarried sister's son born and brought up in her own family? . . . We are of the opinion, therefore, that inasmuch as the relationship of sister existed between the intestate and the declarant, and, by force of the statute, that of mother and son between the intestate and the appellant, the declarations came literally within the exception and are consequently admissible; and that the jury should be allowed to pass upon their weight, if they find they were ever made, in connection with the other testimony in the case." 

Flora v. Anderson, 75 Fed. Ref. 217 (1896), Thayer, 400.

The complainant sought to recover certain real estate on the ground that he was the illegitimate son of a testator's daughter. A statute of Ohio, of 1853, provided that "Bastards shall be capable of inheriting or transmitting inheritance, on the part of their mother, in like manner as if they were born in lawful wedlock. 

And if the mother be dead, the estate of such bastard shall descend to the relatives on the part of the mother

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

303 6 as if the bastard had been legitimate." A question of the admissibility of declarations as to the claimant's pedigree having arisen the court ruled against it, saying: "The rule that hearsay is admissible in cases of pedigree is limited to cases of legitimate relationship.... Where a relationship is acknowledged as a matter of fact, and its lawfulness only is disputed, hearsay from members of the family may be introduced to show that such relationship was lawful or was not lawful. But hearsay cannot be introduced of an unlawful relationship per se, where a lawful relationship is not claimed. There are cases in which testimony as to declarations of members of the family has been admitted to show that the claimant was a bastard. But on examination it will appear that in those cases the testimony was introduced not to show bastardy per se, as a ground of claim, but to dispute a claim of legitimacy." 

Eisenlord v. Clum, 126 N. Y. 552 (1891), Thayer, 401.

The plaintiff brought an action of ejectment, as the son and sole heir of Peter Eisenlord, who died seized in fee simple of the premises in controversy. The defendants were brothers, sisters and nieces of the deceased and Clum claims under them, being their tenant. The plaintiff was the son of Margaret Lipe, who was still living, and the wife of one Austin. The plaintiff sought to have his mother testify that she was married to Eisenlord prior to the plaintiff's birth. She had apparently never made such a claim during Eisenlord's lifetime. The plaintiff also offered witnesses to testify that they had heard Eisenlord state that he was married to Margaret Lipe and had a son. This evidence was excluded. The Supreme Court, while expressing

Cate doubts as to the legitimacy of the plaintiff's claim, declared that the evidence was admissible and granted new trial. Said the court: "This case involves without doubt a question of pedigree simply. It is what is termed in the books a purely genealogical controversy. 

Where the facts are of comparatively recent occurrence, and the alleged declarations are at war with his known actions during his life, and when there was no cohabitation or recognition of the party as wife or husband, it may be averred that the evidence is to be looked upon with very great distrust. Still, within the authorities the evidence is competent. Under our rulings there may be evidence enough in the case to require its submission to a jury.” 

§ 149. Must Have Been Made Ante Litem Motem. 

Berkeley Peerage Case, 4 Campbell 401 (1811), Thayer, 373.

This case involved the question of who was entitled to the Berkeley peerage. The plaintiff was admitted to be the eldest son, but his brother contended that the first child of the Earl by Mary, Countess of Berkeley, was born out of wedlock and that he, as the eldest legitimate son, was entitled to the peerage. The House of 

Lords in deciding against the claimant discussed the matter of pedigree evidence. "In matters of pedigree," said the court, "It being impossible to prove by living witnesses the relationships of past generations the deceased members of the family are admitted; but here, as the reputation must proceed on particular facts, such as marriages, births and the like, from the necessity of the thing, the hearsay of the family as to these particular facts is not excluded. General rights are naturally talked of in the neighborhood, and family transactions

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

305 among the relations of the parties. Therefore, what is thus dropped in conversation upon such subjects may be presumed to be true. But after a dispute has arisen, the presumption in favor of the declaration fails; and to admit them would lead to most dangerous consequences." A disposition made by the late Earl after controversy over the legitimacy of his first born, and tending to prove such legitimacy, was rejected. § 150. Question of Pedigree Involved. 

Goodright d. Stevens v. Moss, Cowper 592 (1777), Thayer, 371.

In a dispute over the right to possession of land the question arose of the legitimacy of the plaintiff's lessor -whether he was born before the marriage of his parents or not. Witnesses were offered by the defendant to general declarations by the father and mother, that Samuel, the lessor of the plaintiff, was born before the marriage, but this evidence was rejected by the court. 

They also offered evidence that there was a general reputation in the place where the parents resided that Samuel was born before marriage. This evidence was likewise rejected. They offered an answer of the mother of Samuel to a bill in chancery in which she declared him illegitimate; that he was born before marriage and privately baptised and again publicly baptised after marriage. This evidence was rejected. The decision was for the plaintiff but the court of King's Bench granted a new trial saying that "tradition is sufficient in point of pedigree. Circumstances may be proved. 

For instance: suppose from the time of one child's birth to the death of its parent, it had always been treated as illegitimate, and another introduced and considered as

the heir of the family, that would be good evidence... 

So the declarations of parents in their lifetime. to the declaration made by the mother of the present plaintiff's lessor in her answer to the bill filed against her in the Court of Chancery, it is not like offering a deposition or an answer in evidence against a person not a party to the original suit. . . . Here the answer is offered only as evidenced under her hand, or her having made such a declaration." As 

Rex v. Inhabitants of Erith, 8 East 539 (1807), Thayer, 381.

Appeal from an order of justices removing William Harris, his wife and children, from the parish of Cranbrooke to the Parish of Erith. The attorney for Cranbrooks examined William, who stated that about twenty years before when passing through Erith as a lad of fourteen he was with his father, who appears to have been a tramp. The father told him that he was born a bastard in Erith. William testified that he had followed the same wandering life as his father and had never resided permanently anywhere. The Court of Appeals said that this was not a case of pedigree evidence and was therefore inadmissible. Pedigree has to do with the identity of parents or facts concerning the birth that might affect the right of inheritance. 

Haines v. Guthrie, 13 Q. B. D. 818 (1884), Thayer, 388.

Action for the price of certain horses sold and delivered to the defendant. The defence was that at the time of the alleged sale and delivery of the horses the defendant was a minor; that the horses were not necessaries since he was already amply supplied with horses.

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

307 For the purpose of proving the defendant's age, his counsel offered an affidavit made by the defendant's deceased father in a Chancery suit. This statement, if admissible, was evidence that the defendant was a minor at the time of the transaction. The court held that the affidavit was not admissible, since the case did not involve a question of pedigree. Said the court: "No doubt, if admissible, it would be strong evidence to show what was the true date of birth, but the question is whether it is admissible in this action, and on such an issue as is here raised. What the family of the defendant is immaterial, whose son he is is immaterial, whether he is a legitimate or an illegititmate son is immaterial, and whether he is an elder or younger son is immaterial; no question of family is raised in this case." 

§ 151. Exceptions-Declarations as to Matters of Gen eral or Public Interest. 

Thomas v. Jenkins, 6 A. & E. 525 (1837), Thayer, 411.

Action of replevin for cattle impounded for alleged trespass. The material question as to whether the cattle were trespassing depended upon the boundary dividing two estates. An old witness was called by the defendant who swore that he had kept cattle on the first mentioned estate, and turned off those of other people; that his father was at that time tenant of the estate; that his father and brother told him what boundary to keep; this father and brother were overseers of the hamlet of G. and that the boundary of the estate was the same as that of the hamlet. He was then asked whether he had heard from old persons, since dead, what was the boundary of the hamlet. The question was objected to but was allowed and the witness

stated what he had heard as to the boundary of the hamlet. The Court of King's Bench said: "Reputation is clearly evidence as to the boundary of a parish. The question is, whether in this case it was admissible as a medium of proof. Now I think that, the fact being once before the jury that the boundaries of the farm and of the hamlet were identical, any legitimate mode of proving the boundary of the hamlet was allowable in the case. The answer of the witness, that the two boundaries were the same, was admitted without objection. 

It is true that the boundary of a farm may shift; but the answer here given applied to one state of the premises, and that appeared to have continued from as far back as living memory could reach." 

Weld v. Brooks, 152 Mass. 297 (1890), Thayer, 421.

In an action for trespass in passing over the plaintiff's land, it became material to prove whether or not the public had acquired a right by prescription to pass over the land in question. For this purpose the defendants offered an attested copy of a deed executed in 1860 from Charles M. Mayo and also an attested copy of a plan of the land recorded with the deed. The parties to the deed were dead. The land conveyed was described as "bounded northerly by a street leading from Bourne street to and across the Providence Railroad, on land now or late of Ebenezer Weld." Weld was the father of the plaintiff and the street described was the one in dispute. It was objected that the deed was between third parties and mere hearsay and reputation. The court declared it competent evidence upon the question of the existence and location of a public way. It was a matter of public and general interest upon which repu-

HEARSAY EVIDENCE 

309 tation is admissible. The decision was for the defendants. 

Hall v. Mayo, 97 Mass. 416 (1867), Thayer, 421.

This was an action of tort in the nature of trespass for breaking and entering the plaintiff's close upon the seashore in Dennis, digging up the earth and erecting a fish weir. The defendants claimed title. Both parties traced their titles to a division of lands in 1714 among the original proprietors of the town of Yarmouth (of which Dennis had formerly been a part) as appearing in their records. The title to defendant's land was described in the record and one of the courses was stated as "ranging about a rod to the north of Elisha Taylor's house." In order to establish the location of Elisha 

Taylor's house in 1714 the plaintiff called James Taylor an aged descendant of Elisha. He testified that about 

1807 his great uncle had shown him some bricks remaining in the ground near the witness' house. He was then asked what his great uncle had told him and if there was a tradition in the family as to where the Elisha Taylor house had stood. This question was excluded. The Supreme Court declared that the exclusion was proper. Said the court: "The exceptions to the general rule excluding hearsay evidence, which permits the introduction of reputation or tradition, or of declarations of persons deceased, as to matters of public or general interest, or questions of pedigree, do not extend to a question of private boundary, in which no considerable number of persons have a legal interest. ... In the case at bar, the evidence did not even directly relate to a boundary line, but merely to the position of a certain house, and was clearly inadmissible."

§152. Opinion Evidence Defined.

§ 152. Opinion Evidence Defined. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 153. Opinion Generally Inadmissible. 

Wright v. Doe & Tatham, 5 Cl. & F. 670 (1838), Thayer, 677.

In a will contest, when the sanity of the testator was in question, certain letters written by persons now dead and sent to the deceased were offered in evidence. 

These letters had reference to business matters and indicated by inference the opinion of the writers that the testator was a rational person, and capable of transacting ordinary business affairs. On the admissibility of the letters the case went to the House of Lords. The judges decided that the letters were not admissible, saying: "If the writer were alive, and producible in court, it could hardly be contended that the letters could be read; the first principles of the law of evidence would

OPINION EVIDENCE 

311 ... prevent it; and I do not think it can be said that the death of the writer necessarily varies the rule. It is every day's experience that the death of a witness deprives the party of testimony he would have given, although his statement of it and his having acted on the faith of it, may be capable of the clearest proof. . . . It is prefectly clear that in this case an opinion, not given upon oath in a judicial inquiry between the parties, is no evidence; for the question is not what the capacity of the testator was reputed to be, but what it really. was in point of fact; and though the opinion of a witness upon oath as to that fact might be asked, it would be only a compendious mode of ascertaining the result of the actual observation of the witness, from acts done, as to the habits and demeanor of the deceased." 

Graham v. Penn. Co., 139 Pa. 149 (1891), Thayer, 701. 

Mrs. Graham was injured by falling from a raised platform onto the main platform of the defendant's trainshed. She claimed that she had often alighted at this station, but had never before gotten off the train at the point where the elevated platform existed. This platform was less than four feet wide and was elevated nine inches above the main platform. There was a verdict for the plaintiff and the defendant alleged exceptions one of which was that the plaintiff's son was permitted to state: "In my personal opinion, I would unhesitatingly say that the platform, from the way it was built, is not only not safe, but it is actually a trap, from the fact that it is raised nine inches above the level of the ground, without any side protection. People walking toward the front are very apt to make the least step sideways and go down." Other witnesses were

permitted to testify to the same effect. The Supreme 

Court held this to be prejudicial error. Said the court: "Whenever the circumstances can be fairly and adequately described to the jury, and are such that their bearing on the issue can be estimated by all men, without special knowledge or training, opinions of witnesses, expert or other are not admissible. . . . In the present case, the alleged dangerous place was a raised part of the platform, or broad step, four feet wide and nine inches high. It came clearly within the range of ordinary experience. The briefest statement would convey a perfect comprehension of the place, and every juryman who ever got in or out of a car, or went up or down a flight of steps, was as capable of judging of the alleged danger as the witnesses who gave their opinions." 

Ogden v. People, 134 Ill. 599 (1890), Thayer, 674.

The defendant, Ogden, was on trial for robbery of 

Andrew Martin. It appeared that Martin had just received pension money to the amount of $240. On the night of November 5, 1889, he and his wife and daughter were asleep when they were aroused by a knocking at the door. Martin went to the door, lamp in hand. A man with his face wrapped in a red flannel ordered him to put out the lamp and give up his money or he would blow out his brains. In fear for his life, Martin delivered $173. During the affair other conversation occurred. The defendant had been well known to the family for ten years. At the preliminary hearing Andrew

Martin testified that he recognized the voice as that of the defendant. Martin died before the trial but his wife and daughter each testified that they recognized

OPINION EVIDENCE 

313 the defendant by his voice. There was testimony that in an intoxicated condition the defendant had left his room at ten o'clock on the night of the robbery. The defendant was found guilty and appealed on the ground that the opinion evidence was improperly admitted. 

The Supreme Court declared: "The statement by the witnesses for the prosecution of a fact which they ascertained through the sense of hearing was not the statement of mere matter of opinion, but the statement of a conclusion reached directly and primarily from an operation of the sense of hearing. A witness can learn and know facts by and through the exercise of his perceptive faculties-his five senses-and such facts he may state." The judgment was affirmed. 

Connecticut Life Ins. Co. v. Lathrop, 111 U. S. 612 (1884),

Thayer, 681. 

This was a suit on an insurance policy which provided "that in case the insured shall, after its execution become so far intemperate as to impair his health, or induce delirium tremens, or should die by his own hand, it shall be void and of no effect." It was alleged in defence that the insured did become intemperate, did have delirium tremens and did die by his own hand. It was admitted that the insured committed suicide but it was insisted that he was insane at the time. In order to prove the insanity, certain witnesses, who were not experts, were permitted to testify that from their observation of the insured they believed him to have been insane at the time of his suicide. A verdict was returned for the plaintiff. The defendant appealed on the ground of improperly admitted evidence. The U. S. 

Supreme Court affirmed the decision, saying: "The

truth is, the statement of a non-professional witness as to the sanity or insanity of an individual, whose appearance, manner, habits and conduct came under his personal observation, is not the expression of mere opinion. 

In form, it is opinion, because it expresses an inference or conclusion based upon observation of the appearance, manner and motions of another person, of which a correct idea cannot well be communicated in words to others, without embodying, more or less, the impressions or judgment of the witness. But, in a substantial sense, and for every purpose essential to a safe conclusion, the mental condition of an individual, as sane or insane, is a fact, and the expressed opinion of one who has had adequate opportunities to observe his conduct and appearance is but the statement of a fact, not indeed a fact established by direct and positive proof, because in most, if not all cases, it is impossible to determine, with absolute certainty, the precise mental condition of another; yet, being founded on actual observation, and being consistent with common experience and the ordinary manifestations of the condition of the mind, it is knowledge, so far as the human intellect can acquire knowledge, upon such subjects." 

§ 154. Opinion of Identity or Physical Condition. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 155. Opinion of Experts. 

United States v. McGlue, 1 Curtis (U. S. C. C.) 1 (1851), 

Thayer, 675.

The defendant was indicted for the murder of Johnson. It was alleged in defence that the prisoner was suffering from delirium tremens when he struck the

OPINION EVIDENCE 

315 fatal blow. Medical experts were examined by prosecution and defence and the chief question under discussion in the reported case was the proper function of the expert witness. "It is not the province of an expert," said the court in its charge to the jury, "to draw inferences of fact from the evidence, but simply to declare his opinion upon a known or hypothetical state of facts; and therefore the counsel on each side have put to the physicians such states of fact as they deem warranted by the evidence, and have taken their opinions thereon. 

If you consider that any of these states of fact put to the physicians are proved, then the opinions thereon are admissible evidence, to be weighed by you. Otherwise, their opinions are not applicable to this case. . . . 

But these opinions, though proper for your respectful consideration, and entitled to have, in your hands, all that weight which reasonably and justly belong to them, are nevertheless not binding on you, against your own judgment, but should be weighed, and, especially where they differ, compared by you, and such effect allowed to them as you think right; not forgetting that on you alone rests the responsibility of a correct verdict." The prisoner was acquitted. 

Lincoln v. The Saratoga R. R., 23 Wend. (N. Y.), 425 (1840), Thayer, 685.

In an action for recovery of damages for a broken leg received by the plaintiff through the alleged negligence of the defendant, the plaintiff offered a physician as a witness. The witness was asked whether, according to his experience and observation, there was danger that a wrench to a limb fractured like the plaintiff's would bring on a disease that would occasion

death, to which he answered that he thought there was danger. The defendant duly excepted to the question and answer. On cross-examination, and also on redirect examination, the witness gave instances where such results had followed. After a verdict for the plaintiff, the defendant carried the case to the Supreme Court. The latter court granted a new trial, speaking in part as follows: "On questions of skill and judgment, men of science and experience are allowed to give their opinions in evidence. These are admitted, for the reason that the witnesses are supposed to possess a peculiar knowledge and understanding of the subjects in controversy, beyond ordinary men, of which the jury are composed, subjects with which they have become familiar by study, or from observation and experience in the course of their particular occupations. . . . I am also of opinion that some of the questions put in respect to the injury to the limb were pushed into consequences and conjectures too remote for the subject of judicial investigation. The present and probable future conditions of it were proper matters for inquiry; but the consequences of a hypothetical second fracture were obviously beyond the range of it, and calculated to draw the minds of the jurors into fanciful conjectures." 

Folkes v. Chadd, 3 Douglas 157 (1782), Thayer, 689.

The trustees for the preservation of Wells harbor, being of the opinion that an embankment, erected more than twenty years before to prevent flowage of the plaintiff's meadows, was contributing to the choking and filling up of the harbor threatened to remove the bankment. The plaintiff applied to the court for protection. At the first trial a Mr. Milne, an engineer,

OPINION EVIDENCE 

317 testified for the plaintiff that in his opinion the embankment was not the cause of mischief to the harbor. 

There was a verdict for the plaintiff but a second trial was obtained on the ground that defendants were surprised by the expert testimony. At the second trial the plaintiff introduced evidence that other harbors, similarly situated on the same coast, in which no embankments existed had begun to fill up. Another expert on harbors was called by the plaintiff to substantiate Mr. 

Milne's opinion. This evidence was objected to and the court excluded it. Lord Mansfield delivered the opinion of the Court of King's Bench and granted a new trial, saying: "It is objected that Mr. Smearton is going to speak, not as to facts but as to opinion. That opinion, however, is deduced from facts which are not disputed, -the situation of banks, the course of tides and of winds, and the shifting of sands. His opinion, deduced from all these facts, is, that, mathematically speaking, the bank may contribute to the mischief, but not sensibly. Mr. Smearton understands the construction of harbors, the causes of their destruction, and how remedied. In matters of science no other witnesses can be called. . . . The cause of the decay of a harbor is also a matter of science, and still more so, whether the removal of the bank can be beneficial. Of this, such men as Mr. Smearton alone can judge. Therefore we are of opinion that his judgment, formed on facts, was very proper evidence." 

Vandine v. Burpee, 13 Metc. (Mass.), 288 (1847), Thayer, 693.

This was an action to recover damages for injury to the plaintiff's garden and nursery alleged to have been

done by smoke, heat and gases proceeding from brick kilns belonging to the defendants. At the trial the plaintiff introduced as witnesses Willett and Walker, two experienced gardeners who had spent years in raising, grafting and cultivating fruit trees. They had examined the plaintiff's garden and nursery after the firing of the kiln in July, 1845. They testified to the injury to the trees, fruit and vegetation then apparent and stated as their opinion that the damage would be likely to result from firing the kiln in the state of weather that existed at the time. They were then severally asked, against the objection of defendant, to state the monetary amount of damage sustained by the plaintiff. Upon the admissibility of this evidence the case went to the Supreme Court where the decision was in the affirmative. Said the court: "It seems to us that it would be impracticable to dispense with this species of testimony, in many actions of trover for personal property, where no detail of facts could adequately inform the jury of the value of the articles. . . . The witnesses were men having practical experience with the subject of cultivating fruit-trees and shrubs; they had already stated, in detail, to the jury, the nature and extent of the injury, so far as description of that kind could give information; and to this they were allowed to add also their opinion as to the amount of damage occasioned by the injury to which they had before testified. The court are of opinion that the testimony was competent." 

Whitney v. Thatcher, 117 Mass. 523 (1875), Thayer, 695.

In suit for breach of contract to receive and pay for gunny bags the plaintiff sought to recover as dam-

OPINION EVIDENCE 

319 ages the difference between the market price in New York City of the quantity of bags contracted for and the price agreed upon in the contract. For the purpose of proving that the market price was at the time from 

3 to 32 cents below the contract price, the plaintiff called as witnesses two dealers in such commodities, each of whom had branch stores in Boston and New York. The defendant objected to these witnesses and the chief question later presented to the Supreme Court was whether their testimony was admissible. 

The court overruled the defendant's exceptions, saying: "We see no reason why merchandise brokers in Boston, members of firms doing business and having houses established both in Boston and New York, might not properly be admitted to testify as to the market value, at a particular date, of an article of merchandise in which they were familiar, even though their knowledge was chiefly obtained from daily price lists and returns of sales daily furnished them in Boston from their New York houses. It is not necessary, in order to qualify one to give an opinion as to values, that his information should be of such direct character as would make it competent in itself as primary evidence." 

Clinton v. Howard, 42 Conn. 294 (1875), Thayer, 697.

While the plaintiff was driving his horse along the public highway it became frightened at a large pile of stones placed in one side of the highway by the defendant. The horse, in its fright, caused a collision with the team of George Clinton, who was approaching the spot from the opposite direction. The plaintiff's wagon was upset and broken. The horse ran away. There was a verdict for the plaintiff. The defendant en-

deavored to secure a new trial. One of his reasons was that the lower court permitted the following question to be put to George Clinton. "Whether an object like this pile of stones would be likely to make an ordinarily gentle horse shy." The court said that since George 

Clinton had had much experience with horses he was qualified to answer the question and that such answer would be helpful to the jury. The defendant claimed that the court erred in permitting the plaintiff to answer the question: "What objects usually make horses shy," to which he answered that a new object had that effect. This was held to be admissible. A further objection was that the plaintiff was permitted to describe his horse's behavior when in the vicinity of railroad trains and road rollers. The Supreme Court held that this was legitimate evidence tending to prove that the horse was a gentle animal that would not have run away except for the defendant's wrongful act in leaving the stones in the road. 

§ 156. Qualifying as an Expert. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 157. The Hypothetical Question. 

People v. McElvaine, 121 N. Y. 250 (1890), Thayer, 715.

The defendant was convicted of murder in the first degree in the slaying of one Luca. His only defence was insanity. Two medical experts were called in his behalf, who expressed opinions that he was insane. Two medical experts appeared for the prosecution, who declared that the defendant was sane. The Supreme 

Court, in setting aside the conviction declared that the

OPINION EVIDENCE 

321 hypothetical question asked by the prosecution of one of the experts was improper. "Q. Now, are you able to say whether in your judgment, based upon all the testimony, the acts of the defendant on the night of the homicide, the testimony as to his past life given by the witnesses in his defence, and based upon the whole case, whether this young man was sane or insane?" The

Supreme Court said: "We cannot doubt but that this question was improper. The witness was thus permitted to take into consideration all the evidence in the case given upon a long trial extending over nine days, and, upon so much of it as he could recollect, determine for himself the credibility of the witnesses, the probability or improbability of their statements, and drawing therefrom such inferences as, in his judgment, were warranted by it, pronounce upon the sanity or insanity of the defendant. It cannot be questioned but that the witness was by the question put in the place of the jury, and was allowed to determine upon his own judgment what their verdict ought to be in the case."

The court declared that the hypothetical question must include a statement of the alleged facts or circumstances upon which it was based. 

Flaherty v. Powers, 167 Mass. 61, 44 N. E. 1074 (1896). 

A girl seventeen years old was injured by the spattering of a mixture of gum and caustic soda into her eye from a machine upon which she was working in making envelopes. In an action against her employer for the injury, it appeared that she knew of the tendency of the machine to spatter, and that she had complained of it. She testified that before the accident in question the substance had burned her face, and that it had

stained her clothes and sometimes made holes in them. 

There was some question whether the plaintiff had not been negligent in failing to wash the mixture out of her eye immediately after the accident. A medical expert was asked: "If an eye was inflamed violently for two weeks, and caustic soda had got into it two months before, and there had never been any trouble with the eye up to that time, what would he say was the cause." The Supreme Court declared that "the question was well enough, although to be instructive it would have been proper to add to the hypothesis that the inflammation had begun immediately after the soda had got into the eye, that being the testimony in the case."

§ 160. Sequestration of Witnesses. 

Commonwealth v. Follansbee, 155 Mass. 274 (1892). 

The defendant was on trial for having performed an abortion on Ida C. Stewart. At the trial the government called a number of witnesses, and the defendant

moved that the witnesses not under examination be excluded from the court room during the examination, offering to apply the same rule to his own witnesses. 

There was no contention that the situation made this inconvenient or impossible. The judge denied the motion and the defendant alleged exceptions. The Supreme Court declared that it was within the discretion of the presiding judge to exclude witnesses from the court room or to refuse to exclude them. The exceptions were over-ruled. 

§ 161. Direct Examination. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 162. Refreshing Recollection of Witness. 

Chapin v. Lapham, 20 Pick. (Mass.), 467 (1838).

This was an action of assumpsit to recover the amount paid on a note by the plaintiff as surety for the defendant's son at the defendant's alleged request. In the course of the trial the plaintiff's counsel called the defendant's counsel as a witness, to prove what took place on a previous trial. The latter declared that he did not recollect. He was asked to look at his minutes of the former trial, then on the table before him. This he refused to do unless so ordered by the court. 

He was so directed. The Supreme Court (Shaw, C. J.) said: "It is a question of sound judicial discretion, for the judge at the trial, to direct how the examination shall be conducted; and he will be governed in this respect by a view to the rights of the parties and the furtherance of justice, having due regard to the rights of the witness, under all the circumstances of the case. The

EXAMINATION OF WITNESSES 

325 witness is bound under his oath to testify to the whole truth, and he ought to do what is reasonable to enable him to perform that duty, faithfully and sincerely, according to the spirit of his oath." 

Mayberry v. Holbrook, 182 Mass. 463, 65 N. E. 849 (1903).

In this case a physician was suing for professional services. At the trial he testified, with his books before him, that he had made a certain number of calls. Upon cross examination he testified that he did not keep the run of these calls in his memory, and that without looking at the books he could not tell about their number. The books were not put in evidence. In discussing the right of the witness thus to refresh his recollection, the Supreme Court said: "The plaintiff having testified that he could not now state from memory, independent of his books, that he did render services as charged in the bill,-but he states that he did render them as charged in the bill; he also states that in making them that he relies on his books. He does not state that the books refresh his recollection so that he can now have a distinct recollection of all those items, but that is not necessary. If the books enable him to say now upon his oath that the fact was as the books have it that would be sufficient." 

§ 163. Right of Counsel and Jury to Inspect Memoranda. 

Commonwealth v. Haley, 13 Allen (Mass.), 587 (1866).

The defendant was on trial for the illegal sale of intoxicating liquor. A deputy constable, on behalf of the government, testified that he visited the defendant's house on certain days and witnessed several sales of 1

intoxicating liquor made by the defendant. 

On crossexamination, he stated that he kept a memorandum of the places he visited and the dates as well as the violations of law that he witnessed. In the same book he kept personal memoranda. For this reason he declined to permit the counsel for the defence to examine it. The latter demanded the right to examine the book and to submit it to the jury as evidence that it was manufactured all at one time and within a few days of the trial. The judge declined to require the witness to produce the book as requested, but did permit counsel to inspect the items used to refresh recollection and to exhibit the same to the jury. The Supreme Court declared that the action of the trial court was proper. The court declared that the witness was "only bound to show such parts of it as he consults to aid his memory, or such as relate to the subject of his testimony. Private entries or other matters, though contained in the same book, are not thereby made parts of the writing which he produces."

§ 167. Impeaching One's Own Witnesses. 

(See textbook.) 

Commonwealth v. Hudson, 11 Gray (Mass.), 64 (1858).

In a trial in the Court of Common Pleas a witness for the State testified that he drank intoxicating liquor but once in the defendant's shop in 1857, and that Abraham Lewis delivered it to him. The defendant later called the same witness and examined him only in reference to certain threats made in his presence and hearing against the defendant by another witness for the State, and to a debt from him to the defendant. 

The district attorney took the witness in hand for cross examination, among other things in regard to the origin of this debt, and whether it was for liquor. He put this question to him: "Did you not testify before the grand jury that you bought intoxicating liquor two or three times of Lewis in the defendant's shop?" The defendant objected that it was incompetent to inquire what the witness had testified to before the grand jury; but the court over-ruled the objection and the witness answered that he did so testify. The defendant was convicted. He alleged exceptions. The Supreme 

Court sustained the exceptions, saying: "The district attorney called a witness, and thereby undertook that, so far as he knew, he was entitled to credit, and held him up to the court and jury as a credible witness. . . . The attempt is not to prove the fact which he testified to before the grand jury. It can only be to disparage him by showing that he testified differently. The whole course of practice is otherwise in this Commonwealth. . . . It is said that the defendant by calling the witness again, makes him his own witness to all purposes. He

does to some purposes; it would be very difficult to determine what. But the party who first called him cannot be allowed to say or to show that he was unworthy of credit." 

§ 168. Impeaching the Adversary's Witnesses. 

(See textbook.) 

§ 169. By Contradictory Statements out of Court.

§ 170. Corroboration of Witnesses. 

Bryant v. Tidgewell, 133 Mass. 86 (1882). was 

The plaintiff brought suit against Harriet Tidgewell and Thomas Allen, alleging that her husband known to be a drunkard and that notwithstanding this fact well known to her the said Harriet sold him liquor on many occasions, thereby causing him to abuse the plaintiff and fail to provide for her support. Thomas 

Allen was owner of the premises and was alleged to have leased the same with knowledge that they were to be used for the sale of intoxicating liquor. This action was brought under a statute imposing liability upon tenant and owner of premises in such cases. The defendant 

Allen called as a witness the defendant Tidgewell, who testified to matters material to the issue. For the purpose of sustaining her credit as a witness, Allen offered to prove by her that she was without any means to satisfy any judgment which might be obtained against her in this case; but the judge excluded. After a ver-

EXAMINATION OF WITNESSES 

329 dict for the plaintiff the defendant alleged exceptions. 

On this particular point the Supreme Court said: "As to the second exception, the credibility of the witness had not been attacked by the plaintiff. It appears that the witness was one of the defendants, and therefore directly interested in the suit. .. Whether evidence of the kind offered ought ever to be received to rebut evidence of interest or bias, is at least doubtful, and it may perhaps depend upon the nature of the evidence it is offered to rebut; but we think it ought never to be received, in the first instance, when offered by the party who calls the witness, for the purpose of supporting his testimony; and that the court did right in excluding it." 

Green v. Gould, 3 Allen (Mass.), 465 (1862).

The plaintiff brought an action against the defendant to recover for articles sold to the minor son of the defendant. The latter's defence was that the son was not authorized to make the purchases, and that the plaintiff had knowledge of this fact. One witness for the defendant testified that after the son had left the plaintiff's shop on one occasion, Green had said: "That is a hard boy; I do not believe his parents know the deviltry he is up to." The defendant also called as a witness one Perry, who testified, under objection by plaintiff, that on one occasion, after the son had made some of the purchases in controversy, Daniel Bryant had asked the plaintiff if he supposed the boy's father knew that he was running up the bill. Green was alleged to have replied: "I do not suppose he does know it, but I don't care; he is able to pay for it." Daniel Bryant was later called by the plaintiff and contradicted the evidence of

Perry. Thereupon the defendant called John F. Hay, who was permitted to testify, under objection, in corroboration of Perry. After a verdict for the defendant the case was taken to the Supreme Court on exceptions. In over-ruling the objections the court declared: "The testimony of Hay, in confirmation of Perry, was properly admitted. The only possible objection to it which occurs to us is, that Hay as well as Perry should have been called to testify before Bryant was called. 

But the order in which those witnesses might testify was a matter of judicial discretion, the exercise of which is not subject to revision." 

§ 171. Scope of Cross Examination. 

(See textbook.)
